Suppose the corresponding column heights are $12\,\mathrm{mm}$ and $52\,\mathrm{mm}$. These are constructed inputs, not observed measurements. Assume the response is linear over this interval, with the same immersion and reading procedure throughout. Applying <thermometer calibration> gives
$$
\frac{t_C(X)}{{}^\circ\mathrm C}
=40\frac{X-12\,\mathrm{mm}}{52\,\mathrm{mm}-12\,\mathrm{mm}}.
$$
For $X=37\,\mathrm{mm}$, first subtract the zero-reference height, then divide by the full reference span: $(37-12)/(52-12)=25/40$. Multiplying by the $40\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$ span gives $t_C=25\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, or $T=298.15\,\mathrm K$ in <kelvin>. Thus equal $37\,\mathrm{mm}$ readings in separately equilibrated samples test equal <temperatures>, regardless of how much <internal energy> those samples contain. The Celsius offset and modern absolute-scale definition are described in <degree Celsius> and <kelvin>.

\Image[zeroth-law-thermometer-bath-calibration.png]
{id=zeroth-law-thermometer-bath-calibration}
{title=A reference-bath <thermometer> comparison and a worked two-point <thermometer calibration>}
{description=Left: two probes sample gently stirred <water>, with sensing tips immersed away from the walls and bottom; the heater is off for the ambient comparison. Right: an assumed linear column response maps $12\,\mathrm{mm}$ to $0\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, $52\,\mathrm{mm}$ to $40\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, and $37\,\mathrm{mm}$ to $25\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$. The schematic and all plotted values are illustrative, not experimental data.}

The two endpoints determine the assumed line, but do not establish linearity. An independent middle reference and a return to the initial bath can test curvature and drift. If the height has an error of $0.2\,\mathrm{mm}$ while reference values and endpoints are held exact in this calculation, the inferred <temperature> error is $0.2\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$; that is only the height contribution, not a full measurement error budget. A boiling-water reference needs a measured <atmospheric pressure> correction, rather than an automatic $100\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$ assignment. The modern <kelvin> is not defined by either bath.

= Reference-bath thermometer comparison
{parent=Zeroth-law thermometer comparison}

A motivating real procedure is NIST's ambient <thermometer> verification. Its apparatus is a deep beaker of <water>, a calibrated reference <thermometer>, a test <thermometer>, and a magnetic stirrer with any heater switched off. Let the <water> approach room <temperature>, stir gently, immerse both sensing tips well below the surface, and wait before reading. NIST specifies about $20\,\mathrm{cm}$ of <water>, $10$–$15\,\mathrm{cm}$ immersion where possible, and an initial five-minute wait. Record test then reference, repeat in reverse order, and apply the reference's certificate correction. Subtract corrected reference from test: the difference estimates the test instrument's error at this bath <temperature>. Agreement between the two reading orders checks repeatability. <Mercury Thermometer Alternatives: Verification Methods for Alternative Thermometers by National Institute of Standards and Technology>[“Comparison with another calibrated thermometer at ambient temperature”, apparatus and steps 1–10; “Measurement at the steam point”, pressure and elevation correction]{ref}

For example, suppose a test reads $24.7\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, a reference reads $24.4\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, and its certificate says to add $0.1\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$. The corrected reference is $24.5\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, so the test error is $+0.2\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$: it reads high. These are illustrative readings. This verification tests a previously calibrated instrument at one point; it does not establish the two-point response used in <two-point thermometer calibration example>.

Additional controls follow from the <thermal equilibrium> premise. Keep tips away from walls and bottom, interchange probe positions to test a <temperature gradient>, and check that longer waits and modest immersion changes leave agreement unchanged within the required tolerance. A flat trace alone can be a <steady state> maintained by competing <heat transfer> paths. Five minutes is a procedural starting point, not a universal equilibration time. Excessive stirring can supply <work> and warm the bath, while probe stems can exchange <heat> with the room. These controls test whether both probes sample the same state; neither one-point agreement nor this experiment proves the <zeroth law of thermodynamics> for every material and condition.

= Compressibility
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{title2=$\kappa_X=-V^{-1}(\partial V/\partial P)_{X,N}$}
{wiki}

Compressibility measures how readily a sample's <volume (thermodynamics)> decreases when its <pressure> increases. For a <simple compressible system> with fixed particle number $N$ and composition, specify the constraint $X$:
$$
\kappa_X=-\frac1V\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial P}\right)_{X,N},\qquad
\frac{dV}{V}=-\kappa_X\,dP\quad(X,N\text{ fixed}).
$$
Here $P$ denotes <pressure>, also written $p$ elsewhere. The minus sign makes <compressibility> positive on a mechanically stable equilibrium branch. Its units are inverse <pressure>, $\mathrm{Pa^{-1}}$; its reciprocal $K_X=1/\kappa_X$ is the constrained <bulk modulus>, measured in <pascal (unit)> units. Holding <temperature> fixed gives <isothermal compressibility>; holding <entropy> fixed gives <isentropic compressibility> $\kappa_S$ along an <isentropic process>. The latter describes a reversible <adiabatic compression>, since an irreversible <adiabatic process> can increase <entropy>. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/3-20-materials-at-equilibrium-sma-5111-fall-2003/e402e3f0f555e15ff3af29b5b44b4e57_notes1.pdf[G. Ceder, MIT thermodynamics notes, section 1.14: compressibility]{ref}

For an <ideal gas>, the <ideal gas law> $PV=Nk_BT$ and the <reversible ideal-gas adiabat> give, at the same equilibrium state,
$$
\kappa_T=\frac1P,\qquad
\kappa_S=\frac1{\gamma P},\qquad
\gamma=\frac{C_P}{C_V}>1.
$$
Here $C_P$ and $C_V$ are the whole sample's <heat capacity at constant pressure> and <heat capacity at constant volume>, and $\gamma$ is the <heat capacity ratio>. Constant $\gamma$ is required for the finite-path formula $PV^\gamma=\mathrm{constant}$. Reversible <adiabatic compression> raises the <temperature>, making the <pressure> rise more steeply than in an <isothermal compression>. Thus keeping <entropy> fixed makes the <gas> harder to compress locally. For a classical <monatomic gas> at $100\,\mathrm{kPa}$, $\gamma=5/3$, so $\kappa_T=10^{-5}\,\mathrm{Pa^{-1}}$ and $\kappa_S=6\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{Pa^{-1}}$; the corresponding values of the <bulk modulus> are $100\,\mathrm{kPa}$ and $166.67\,\mathrm{kPa}$.

For a smooth, stable single <thermodynamic phase> at positive <temperature>, <Maxwell relations> also imply
$$
\frac{\kappa_T}{\kappa_S}=\frac{C_P}{C_V},\qquad
\kappa_T-\kappa_S=\frac{TV\alpha^2}{C_P},
$$
where $\alpha$ is the <coefficient of thermal expansion>. The <ideal gas> result is one example of this broader link between mechanical and thermal responses. The equality $\kappa_T=\kappa_S$ is possible when $\alpha=0$ and the other responses remain finite. These formulas compare equilibrium <partial derivatives>, rather than arbitrary fast or dissipative compression histories. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/2-43-advanced-thermodynamics-spring-2024/1Q7kBt43LW4oPrh3pxYjs2xxe7HWsC1oR_transcript.pdf[G. P. Beretta, MIT 2.43, Lecture 7: response-function identities]{ref}

= Isothermal compressibility
{parent=Compressibility}
{title2=$\kappa_T=-V^{-1}(\partial V/\partial P)_{T,N}$}

Isothermal compressibility is the <compressibility> measured at fixed <temperature>, particle number and composition. A slowly compressed sealed piston in contact with a <thermal reservoir> can approach this constraint. For an <ideal gas>, differentiating $V=Nk_BT/P$ at fixed <temperature> gives $(\partial V/\partial P)_{T,N}=-V/P$, hence $\kappa_T=1/P$. A small <pressure> increase $\Delta P$ therefore gives $\Delta V/V\simeq-\Delta P/P$. For a finite change, use the whole <isothermal process> curve $V_2/V_1=P_1/P_2$ instead.

Take one <mole> of classical <monatomic gas>, with stipulated <molar gas constant> $R=8.314\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$, initially at $T_0=300\,\mathrm K$ and $P_0=100\,\mathrm{kPa}$. The initial <volume (thermodynamics)> is $V_0=nRT_0/P_0=0.024942\,\mathrm{m^3}$. Compressing to $V_0/2$ gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
\text{Isothermal:}\quad &P_2=2P_0=200\,\mathrm{kPa},\quad T_2=300\,\mathrm K,\\
\text{Reversible adiabatic:}\quad &P_2=2^{5/3}P_0\simeq317.48\,\mathrm{kPa},\quad
T_2=2^{2/3}T_0\simeq476.22\,\mathrm K.
\end{aligned}
$$
The <isothermal compression> releases <heat> to the <thermal reservoir>; the reversible <adiabatic compression> retains the input <work> as <internal energy>. Reversing either curve gives the corresponding expansion, with <adiabatic expansion> cooling the <gas>.

\Image[gas-isothermal-adiabatic-compression.png]
{title=An <ideal gas> resists reversible <adiabatic compression> more strongly than <isothermal compression>}
{description=Left: two <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> curves through the same initial state; arrows point toward compression. Right: the corresponding <temperature> changes, including cooling during reversible <adiabatic expansion>. The dotted line marks half the initial <volume (thermodynamics)>. One <mole> of classical <monatomic gas>, with $\gamma=5/3$ and the stipulated $R$ above.}

The equilibrium curvature of <Gibbs free energy> provides another measurement of <isothermal compressibility>:
$$
\kappa_T=-\frac1V\left(\frac{\partial^2G}{\partial P^2}\right)_{T,N}.
$$
This follows from $V=(\partial G/\partial P)_{T,N}$, with $G$ denoting <Gibbs free energy>. Within a stable single <thermodynamic phase>, increasing <pressure> reduces <volume (thermodynamics)> and makes this curvature negative. At <phase coexistence>, changing the <phase fraction> can change the <volume (thermodynamics)> at constant <temperature> and <pressure>; the usual finite single-phase <isothermal compressibility> then need not exist. Near a <critical point>, it can diverge. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/I_45.html#Ch45-S3[Feynman, Leighton and Sands, section 45-3: compression and phase coexistence]{ref}

= Isentropic compressibility
{parent=Compressibility}
{title2=$\kappa_S=-V^{-1}(\partial V/\partial P)_{S,N}$}

= Adiabatic compressibility
{synonym}

Isentropic compressibility is the <compressibility> at fixed <entropy>, particle number and composition. The conventional name adiabatic compressibility refers to a reversible <adiabatic process>; an irreversible <adiabatic compression> need not keep <entropy> fixed. For an <ideal gas>, it is $\kappa_S=1/(\gamma P)$, where $\gamma=C_P/C_V$ is the <heat capacity ratio>. It describes a steeper local pressure response than <isothermal compressibility> when $\gamma>1$.

= Maxwell relations
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{c}
{title2=$(\partial S/\partial V)_{T,N}=(\partial P/\partial T)_{V,N}$}
{wiki}

= Maxwell relation
{c}
{synonym}

Maxwell relations turn <pressure>, <volume (thermodynamics)> and <temperature> measurements into information about <entropy>. For example, the <pressure> rise when heating a rigid sealed vessel determines the <entropy> change when expanding the same sample isothermally:
$$
\left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial V}\right)_{T,N}
=\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial T}\right)_{V,N}.
$$
The quantities on the two sides describe different experiments, yet both are <mixed partial derivatives> of one <Helmholtz free energy>. This is the useful surprise: a mechanical measurement can reveal a thermal property. Similarly, the <coefficient of thermal expansion> gives $(\partial S/\partial P)_{T,N}=-\alpha V$. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/5-60-thermodynamics-kinetics-spring-2008/7bc33304ab1db561a64b4a6b12e25476_5_60_lecture13.pdf[MIT 5.60, Lecture 13: entropy derivatives from equations of state]{ref}

For a <simple compressible system>, hold particle number $N$ and composition fixed, and allow only <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> <work>. Write $U$ for <internal energy>, $H=U+PV$ for <enthalpy>, $F=U-TS$ for <Helmholtz free energy>, and $G=H-TS$ for <Gibbs free energy>. Their natural-variable differentials are
$$
\begin{aligned}
dU(S,V,N)&=T\,dS-P\,dV, &dH(S,P,N)&=T\,dS+V\,dP,\\
dF(T,V,N)&=-S\,dT-P\,dV, &dG(T,P,N)&=-S\,dT+V\,dP.
\end{aligned}
$$
Thus the first <partial derivatives>, with the subscript recording the variables kept fixed, are
$$
\begin{aligned}
U_S\big|_{V,N}=T,&\quad U_V\big|_{S,N}=-P, &H_S\big|_{P,N}=T,&\quad H_P\big|_{S,N}=V,\\
F_T\big|_{V,N}=-S,&\quad F_V\big|_{T,N}=-P, &G_T\big|_{P,N}=-S,&\quad G_P\big|_{T,N}=V.
\end{aligned}
$$
The <symmetry of second derivatives> produces all four standard <Maxwell relations>, including their signs:
$$
\begin{aligned}
U:\quad\left(\frac{\partial T}{\partial V}\right)_{S,N}
&=-\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\right)_{V,N},\\
H:\quad\left(\frac{\partial T}{\partial P}\right)_{S,N}
&=\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial S}\right)_{P,N},\\
F:\quad\left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial V}\right)_{T,N}
&=\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial T}\right)_{V,N},\\
G:\quad\left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial P}\right)_{T,N}
&=-\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_{P,N}.
\end{aligned}
$$
For a brief derivation of the $F$ relation, differentiate $F_T=-S$ with respect to $V$ and $F_V=-P$ with respect to $T$: $-S_V=F_{TV}=F_{VT}=-P_T$. Each other row follows in the same way. Assume continuous second <partial derivatives> of the relevant <thermodynamic potential> in the region considered. At a <phase transition>, the equilibrium potential may lose this smoothness; apply the identities within each smooth <thermodynamic phase>, and handle <latent heat> and <phase coexistence> separately. If particle numbers vary, the potential differentials acquire <chemical potential> terms, so the fixed-$N$ constraints must remain explicit. https://davidtong.org/teaching/statistical-physics/statmechhtml/S4#S4.SS4.SSS2[David Tong, Statistical Physics, section 4.4.2: the four Maxwell relations]{ref}

For an <ideal gas> with fixed $N$, the <ideal gas law> gives $P=Nk_BT/V$. At fixed <volume (thermodynamics)>, heating changes the <pressure> at rate $Nk_B/V$. Consequently an <isothermal expansion> at positive <temperature> has
$$
\Delta S=\int_{V_1}^{V_2}\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial T}\right)_{V,N}\,dV
=Nk_B\ln\frac{V_2}{V_1}.
$$
For one <mole> with $R=8.314\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$ and $V_2=2V_1$, this is $\Delta S=R\ln2\simeq5.763\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}$. At $300\,\mathrm K$, a reversible <isothermal expansion> absorbs $Q=T\Delta S\simeq1728.85\,\mathrm J$ and performs equal <work>, since its <internal energy> is unchanged. The <entropy> difference is a <state function> difference: the same equilibrium endpoints give the same $\Delta S$ in an insulated <free expansion>, although there $Q=0$ and the process is irreversible.

\Image[maxwell-entropy-pressure-derivatives.png]
{title=<Ideal gas> <entropy> slopes equal measured <pressure> responses to heating}
{description=Left: <entropy> relative to one common reference state $(T_0,V_0)$ for a classical <monatomic gas>, $[S(T,V)-S(T_0,V_0)]/(Nk_B)=\frac32\ln(T/T_0)+\ln(V/V_0)$, with $T_0=300\,\mathrm K$. Each fixed-<temperature> curve gains $\ln2$ on doubling the <volume (thermodynamics)>. Right: both <partial derivatives>, multiplied by $V_0/(Nk_B)$, equal $V_0/V$; circles are independent finite differences of <entropy>, while the line is the <pressure> response.}

The same <Maxwell relations> let thermal measurements determine the <difference between constant-pressure and constant-volume heat capacities>. In a smooth single <thermodynamic phase>,
$$
dS=\frac{C_V}{T}\,dT+\frac{\alpha}{\kappa_T}\,dV
=\frac{C_P}{T}\,dT-\alpha V\,dP,
$$
using <heat capacity at constant volume>, <heat capacity at constant pressure>, <coefficient of thermal expansion> and <isothermal compressibility>. Along fixed <pressure>, $dV=\alpha V\,dT$, so
$$
C_P-C_V=\frac{TV\alpha^2}{\kappa_T}.
$$
Thus measured expansion and compression responses determine a <heat capacity> difference without measuring both <heat capacities> separately. They do not determine the absolute <heat capacities> or absolute <entropy> by themselves; an independent thermal measurement supplies that missing information. For an <ideal gas>, $\alpha=1/T$ and $\kappa_T=1/P$ reduce the result to $C_P-C_V=PV/T=Nk_B$, which is <Mayer's relation>.

When the necessary higher <partial derivatives> exist and commute, differentiating the <Maxwell relations> once more also gives
$$
\left(\frac{\partial C_V}{\partial V}\right)_{T,N}
=T\left(\frac{\partial^2P}{\partial T^2}\right)_{V,N},\qquad
\left(\frac{\partial C_P}{\partial P}\right)_{T,N}
=-T\left(\frac{\partial^2V}{\partial T^2}\right)_{P,N}.
$$
Integrating these slopes determines how a <heat capacity> changes with <volume (thermodynamics)> or <pressure> at fixed <temperature>. For an <ideal gas>, both right-hand sides vanish because the <ideal gas law> is linear in <temperature> at either fixed <volume (thermodynamics)> or fixed <pressure>. Consequently $C_V$ and $C_P$ depend only on <temperature> at fixed $N$, although they need not be constant in <temperature>. https://davidtong.org/teaching/statistical-physics/statmechhtml/S4#S4.SS4.SSS2[Tong, section 4.4.2: heat-capacity changes from thermal pressure and volume curves]{ref}

Historically, <James Clerk Maxwell>'s \i[Theory of Heat], first published in 1871, presents these connections using the geometry of <isothermal processes> and reversible <adiabatic processes>. His fourth edition, page 167, explicitly relates expansion on heating at fixed <pressure> to <heat> emitted during an <isothermal compression>: the modern $G$ relation gives $\delta Q_{\rm rev}=T\,dS=-T\alpha V\,dP$. This explains why a substance with positive <coefficient of thermal expansion> emits <heat> when compressed isothermally, whereas one with negative coefficient absorbs it. https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/theory-of-heat/7052E2DAA4CD862126D5105ABBFFAD0F/listing[Maxwell, Theory of Heat, publication record and chapter IX]{ref} https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Page:Theory_of_Heat,_James_Clerk_Maxwell,_Fourth_Edition.djvu/183[Maxwell, Theory of Heat, fourth edition, page 167]{ref}

= Diffusion
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{title2=$\mathbf j=-D\nabla C$}

Diffusion is net transport associated with random microscopic motion. A localized patch of a dilute <solute> can spread through a stationary <solvent>: individual <molecules> move in both directions, but more cross from the crowded side of an imaginary surface than from the less crowded side. This imbalance gives a net <diffusive flux> down the <concentration> <gradient>; the <molecules> do not need to sense which direction has lower <concentration>. At uniform <concentration>, microscopic exchanges continue even though the net <diffusive flux> vanishes. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/1-061-transport-processes-in-the-environment-fall-2008/8260e3e1577cd739ce85143510d9c165_lec_01.pdf[Heidi Nepf, MIT: random motion and Fick's law]{ref}

The distinction from <advection> is easiest to see in a dye patch. Uniform bulk motion carries its centre downstream; <diffusion> broadens it relative to that centre. Both can happen together. A moving or spreading patch by itself does not identify the mechanism: a nonuniform <velocity field> can also stretch a patch. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/1-061-transport-processes-in-the-environment-fall-2008/c96f9a75576238ac2c4e0e73b84e8072_lec_05.pdf[Nepf, MIT: advection and diffusion of a release]{ref}

\Image[molecular-diffusion-profiles.png]
{title=<Diffusion> broadens a patch; uniform <advection> translates it}
{description=Original model curves, not measured data. Each panel starts with the same <Gaussian distribution> of dye, with <standard deviation> $\sigma_0=0.50\,\mathrm{mm}$. The chosen <diffusion coefficient> is $D=0.20\,\mathrm{mm^2\,h^{-1}}$ where enabled; the chosen bulk speed is $u=0.60\,\mathrm{mm\,h^{-1}}$ where enabled. The vertical axis is <concentration> divided by its spatial integral, so every curve has area one. Pure uniform <advection> preserves width and peak height; <diffusion> lowers the peak while preserving the total dye.}

For a dilute nonreacting <solute> in a uniform medium at fixed <temperature>, <Fick's first law> models the relative <diffusive flux> as $\mathbf j=-D\nabla C$, with scalar <diffusion coefficient> $D>0$ of units $\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}$. Here $C$ is the <amount of substance> per unit <volume (thermodynamics)>, so $\mathbf j$ has units $\mathrm{mol\,m^{-2}\,s^{-1}}$. With carrier <velocity field> $\mathbf u$, the total <flux> is $C\mathbf u+\mathbf j$. The <continuity equation>, with no sources or <chemical reactions>, gives
$$
\frac{\partial C}{\partial t}+\nabla\cdot(C\mathbf u)=\nabla\cdot(D\nabla C).
$$
For constant $D$ and <incompressible flow>, this reduces to $C_t+\mathbf u\cdot\nabla C=D\nabla^2C$, where $\nabla^2$ is the <Laplacian>. In a stationary carrier it is the <diffusion equation>. These assumptions matter: variable $D$ must remain inside the <divergence>, and changing sources require extra terms. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/1-061-transport-processes-in-the-environment-fall-2008/5536a0bf7f3bd0b21fde2169b93a4b5a_lec_02.pdf[Nepf, MIT: conservation with advective and diffusive fluxes]{ref}

The useful surprise is the common mathematics: <thermal conduction> smooths <temperature> through the analogous <heat equation>, while <molecular diffusion> smooths composition. This analogy already motivated the original formulation of <Fick's laws>. https://doi.org/10.1080/14786445508641925[Fick (1855), On liquid diffusion: the analogy with heat conduction]{ref} However, the <thermal diffusivity> and the <solutal diffusivity> need not be equal. A <diffusive time scale> $t_D\sim L^2/D$ means that crossing ten times the distance requires roughly one hundred times the time. This is why <molecular diffusion> is especially effective across thin layers, and why <chaotic advection> can assist mixing by making those layers thinner. Physical <diffusion> is a transport mechanism, distinct from the broader probabilistic class of <Markov diffusion>.

= Molecular diffusion
{parent=Diffusion}

Molecular diffusion is the component of material transport caused by the random thermal motion of <molecules>, measured relative to bulk motion of their surroundings. A <diffusion coefficient> is a property of a particular substance in a particular medium under specified conditions, not a universal speed of spreading. Its dependence on the <solute>, <solvent> and <temperature> is precisely what makes a spreading experiment informative. A <random walk> idealizes this motion; the <Brownian motion> description provides a continuous approximation over sufficiently long times. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/1-061-transport-processes-in-the-environment-fall-2008/8260e3e1577cd739ce85143510d9c165_lec_01.pdf[Nepf, MIT: molecular motion and diffusivity]{ref}

\b[A motivating experiment: watch dye enter a gel.] Samprovalaki, Robbins and Fryer studied rhodamine 6G and methylene blue entering agar gel from two side reservoirs. A covered slab, $10\times5\times1\,\mathrm{mm}$, sat on a controlled thermal stage; the dye was brought to the same <temperature>. They photographed the gel every ten minutes, averaged image intensity across a strip, and fitted the changing <concentration> to a <diffusion equation> model. The cover limited evaporation and stopped dye travelling over the gel surface. Runs at $30$, $50$ and $70\,\mathrm{{}^{\circ}C}$ were repeated in triplicate. The measured spatial colour change therefore supplied a quantitative transport test, rather than merely showing that dye eventually spreads. Changing lighting also changes image intensity, so the optical response had to be checked. Importantly for interpretation, their constant-<diffusion coefficient> fit was imperfect and the gel coefficient is an effective transport coefficient, not automatically the value in free <water>. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jfoodeng.2012.03.024[Samprovalaki, Robbins and Fryer (2012), Investigation of the diffusion of dyes in agar gels, sections 2-4]{ref}

\b[A finite-pulse version to test width growth.] The following is a proposed teaching variant, not that paper's apparatus or a report of measurements. Make a long, narrow, transparent gel channel of uniform cross-section. Join a thin dyed gel band between two undyed lengths of the same gel, with good contact and no air gaps; the dye should initially fill the cross-section so the dominant <concentration> variation is along the channel coordinate $x$. After assembly, keep the covered channel horizontal at uniform <temperature>, away from vibration. The dye is a finite initial pulse: no reservoir supplies more dye. Choose a dye whose image response can be calibrated and whose interaction with the gel is small enough for an approximately constant <diffusion coefficient> to be plausible.

Photograph the whole pulse at recorded times with fixed lighting, focus and exposure, including a length scale. Use uniformly dyed standards of the same gel thickness and a blank gel to convert image intensity to <concentration> $C(x,t)$; do not assume raw brightness is proportional to <concentration>. Average across the channel width and examine several parallel strips to check the one-dimensional approximation. Measure the initial profile instead of treating the band as infinitely thin. For a finite pulse with negligible end effects, define its spatial integral $Q$, centre $\bar x$ and width $\sigma$ by
$$
Q=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}C(x,t)\,dx,\qquad
\bar x(t)=\frac1Q\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}xC(x,t)\,dx,\qquad
\sigma^2(t)=\frac1Q\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(x-\bar x(t))^2C(x,t)\,dx.
$$
Here $\sigma^2$ is the <variance> of the normalized profile $C/Q$, and $\sigma$ is its <standard deviation>. If $C$ is measured in $\mathrm{mol\,m^{-3}}$, then $Q$ has units $\mathrm{mol\,m^{-2}}$; multiplying by the channel cross-section gives the total <amount of substance>. For the unbounded one-dimensional <diffusion equation> with constant $D$, conserved $Q$, finite <variance> and sufficiently decaying tails,
$$
\sigma^2(t)=\sigma_0^2+2Dt,\qquad
D=\frac12\frac{d\sigma^2}{dt}.
$$
Thus plot width \i[squared] against time: its slope determines the <diffusion coefficient>, while its intercept includes the finite starting width. This moment law holds even for a non-<Gaussian distribution> initially; it follows by applying <integration by parts> twice to the <diffusion equation>. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/1-061-transport-processes-in-the-environment-fall-2008/68b0a4a664246168ee639298b39384b1_lec_03.pdf[Nepf, MIT: profile moments and estimating diffusivity from variance growth]{ref}

\Image[molecular-diffusion-width-experiment.png]
{title=Measuring <molecular diffusion> from the growth of width squared}
{description=Original schematic of the proposed finite-pulse gel experiment, with an illustrative calculation rather than measured data. A camera records the broadening band. The plotted points are exact samples of $\sigma^2=0.25+0.40t$ with $\sigma^2$ in $\mathrm{mm^2}$ and $t$ in hours; the slope gives the chosen $D=0.20\,\mathrm{mm^2\,h^{-1}}$. The schematic is not to scale and is not a reproduction of the published apparatus.}

For the particularly readable case of an initially <Gaussian distribution> centred at zero, uniform <advection> at speed $u$ and constant <diffusion coefficient> $D$ give the one-dimensional <concentration> profile
$$
C(x,t)=\frac{Q}{\sqrt{2\pi(\sigma_0^2+2Dt)}}\exp\!\left[-\frac{(x-ut)^2}{2(\sigma_0^2+2Dt)}\right].
$$
Its centre moves as $\bar x=ut$ and its <variance> grows as $\sigma_0^2+2Dt$. Our illustrative choice $\sigma_0=0.50\,\mathrm{mm}$ and $D=0.20\,\mathrm{mm^2\,h^{-1}}$ gives widths $0.50$, $0.81$ and $1.36\,\mathrm{mm}$ at zero, one and four hours. These are calculated values, not a measured dye coefficient. Once $2Dt$ dominates the initial <variance>, width grows as $\sqrt{2Dt}$: four times the elapsed time gives approximately twice the width. A rectangular starting band will not immediately have a <Gaussian distribution>, although the same width-squared law still applies under the stated assumptions.

\b[Controls and limits of the interpretation.] In the proposed experiment, check that $Q$ stays constant and that the centre remains stationary while the width increases. Include a blank and a uniformly dyed gel photographed over the same period: an apparent signal change there exposes lighting drift, dye fading or evaporation. Repeat with a lower starting dye <concentration> and a second initial width; agreement of the slopes is a check on constant $D$ and on preparation effects. Keep the pulse far from the channel ends and record its faint tails: truncation and background errors can strongly bias a <variance>. Poor contact, dye binding, <chemical reactions> or transport in a liquid film can invalidate the simple model. A gel suppresses bulk liquid motion, but its inferred <diffusivity> need not equal that in the pure <solvent>. A stationary centre alone does not exclude symmetric flows, and linear width-squared growth alone does not prove a molecular mechanism: <turbulence> can also produce an effective diffusive description. These are reasons to combine the profile test with the controlled setup.

= Rotational diffusion
{parent=Diffusion}
{wiki}
{title2=$D_r$}

Rotational diffusion randomizes orientation. For a unit orientation vector on a sphere, isotropic rotational diffusion contributes $D_r\nabla_p^2f$ to the <Fokker-Planck equation>, where $\nabla_p^2$ is the <Laplace-Beltrami operator>. Its coefficient has units inverse time. It differs from the translational <diffusion coefficient>, whose units are length squared per time.

= Thermal expansion
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}

Thermal expansion is a change in a body's dimensions as its <temperature> changes under a specified mechanical constraint. A heated <gas> under a freely moving loaded piston usually increases its <volume (thermodynamics)> at fixed <pressure>; heating the same <gas> in a rigid vessel instead raises its <pressure>. The <coefficient of thermal expansion> quantifies the first response. Expansion can be negative, so the name does not assert that every substance grows on heating.

For an <ideal gas> at fixed <pressure> and particle number, $V=Nk_BT/P$ gives $V_2/V_1=T_2/T_1$. Heating from $300\,\mathrm K$ to $330\,\mathrm K$ therefore increases the <volume (thermodynamics)> by exactly $10\%$ within the <ideal gas> model. For a <solid>, specify whether the measurement concerns length or <volume (thermodynamics)>: for uniform expansion in all three directions, the volumetric coefficient is three times the <linear thermal expansion coefficient>, because $V\propto L^3$. Direction-dependent expansion requires the separate dimensional responses. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/3-20-materials-at-equilibrium-sma-5111-fall-2003/e402e3f0f555e15ff3af29b5b44b4e57_notes1.pdf[G. Ceder, MIT thermodynamics notes, section 1.14: linear and volumetric expansion]{ref}

= Coefficient of thermal expansion
{parent=Thermal expansion}
{title2=$\alpha=V^{-1}(\partial V/\partial T)_{P,N}$}

= Thermal expansion coefficient
{synonym}

The volumetric coefficient of <thermal expansion> is the fractional <volume (thermodynamics)> increase per <temperature> increase at fixed <pressure> and composition:
$$
\alpha=\frac1V\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_{P,N}
=-\frac1\rho\left(\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial T}\right)_{P,N},\qquad
\rho=\frac mV.
$$
The <mass density> form requires fixed <mass> $m$. Its units are $\mathrm{K^{-1}}$; $\alpha_T$ is another notation for this coefficient. For small <temperature> changes $\theta$ at fixed <pressure>,
$$
V(T_0+\theta)\simeq V(T_0)(1+\alpha(T_0)\theta),\qquad
\rho(T_0+\theta)\simeq\rho(T_0)(1-\alpha(T_0)\theta).
$$
For a finite change in a single <thermodynamic phase>, integrate the possibly varying coefficient: $\ln(V_2/V_1)=\int_{T_1}^{T_2}\alpha(T,P,N)\,dT$. A constant-coefficient approximation gives an exponential, while the displayed linear formula additionally requires $|\alpha\theta|\ll1$.

For an <ideal gas> at fixed particle number,
$$
\alpha=\frac1T,\qquad
\alpha(300\,\mathrm K)=\frac1{300}\,\mathrm{K^{-1}}\simeq3.333\times10^{-3}\,\mathrm{K^{-1}}.
$$
Integrating $1/T$ recovers the exact fixed-<pressure> <thermal expansion> ratio $V_2/V_1=T_2/T_1$. Combining expansion with <isothermal compressibility> gives the local response of any smooth <simple compressible system> of fixed composition,
$$
\frac{dV}{V}=\alpha\,dT-\kappa_T\,dP,\qquad
\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial T}\right)_{V,N}=\frac\alpha{\kappa_T}.
$$
The ratio has units $\mathrm{Pa\,K^{-1}}$. For the one-<mole> <ideal gas> state in <isothermal compressibility>, it is $P_0/T_0=333.33\,\mathrm{Pa\,K^{-1}}$: a $1\,\mathrm K$ increase at fixed <volume (thermodynamics)> raises the <pressure> by about $333.33\,\mathrm{Pa}$. <Maxwell relations> identify this same ratio as the <entropy> slope with respect to <volume (thermodynamics)> and give $C_P-C_V=TV\alpha^2/\kappa_T$.

Under the <Boussinesq approximation>, the small fractional <mass density> change produces <buoyancy> while <mass density> is otherwise treated as constant. A positive <temperature> excess $\theta$ gives <reduced gravity> $g'=g\alpha_T\theta$ in the convention used in <natural ventilation>. The coefficient therefore converts a <heat> budget into a buoyancy budget. The same measurable expansion response supplies <entropy> changes and <heat capacity> differences through <Maxwell relations>, connecting fluid motion to equilibrium <thermodynamics>.

= Linear thermal expansion coefficient
{parent=Thermal expansion}
{title2=$\alpha_L=L^{-1}(\partial L/\partial T)_P$}

The linear thermal expansion coefficient is the fractional change in a specified <length> per change in <temperature>, at fixed external <pressure> for a sample free to expand. For small changes, $\Delta L/L\simeq\alpha_L\Delta T$. An isotropic sample with the same response in all directions has volumetric <coefficient of thermal expansion> $\alpha=3\alpha_L$, since differentiating $V\propto L^3$ gives $dV/V=3\,dL/L$. Direction-dependent materials require a specified measurement direction. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/1-3-thermal-expansion[OpenStax University Physics 2, §1.3: linear and volumetric expansion]{ref}.

= Stochastic thermodynamics
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}

Stochastic thermodynamics assigns work, heat, and entropy production to individual fluctuating trajectories while recovering ordinary thermodynamics after ensemble averaging.

= Local detailed balance
{parent=Stochastic thermodynamics}

Local detailed balance states that the logarithm of the probability ratio between a trajectory and its time reverse equals the entropy delivered to the environment in units of $k_B$. For one heat bath, $\log(P_F/P_B)=\Delta Q/(k_BT)$ when positive $\Delta Q$ flows into the bath.

= Microscopic reversibility
{parent=Local detailed balance}
{wiki}

For an equilibrium process, a path and its physically time-reversed path have equal probability after including their equilibrium starting weights. Conditional on their endpoints, this means
$$
\frac{P_F}{P_B}=\exp[-(F_2-F_1)/(k_BT)].
$$
This is the path version of <detailed balance>. A time-even <order parameter> is read backward without changing sign, while a transport flux changes sign. The <Onsager--Machlup path probability> implements this reversal in a coarse-grained stochastic description.

= Entropy production
{title2=$\Delta S_{\rm tot}$}
{parent=Stochastic thermodynamics}
{wiki}

Entropy production is the sum of the entropy changes of a system and its environment. Under local detailed balance it is represented by a forward-to-reversed path-probability logarithm and has nonnegative expectation.

= Thermodynamic entropy flux
{parent=Entropy production}
{title2=$j_\eta=q/\theta$}

Local equilibrium associates the outgoing <heat flux> $q$ with outgoing <entropy> flux $q/\theta$ at positive <temperature> $\theta$. Its <divergence> appears in the <Lagrangian entropy inequality>, with the appropriate nominal flux in reference coordinates.

= Fluctuation theorem
{parent=Entropy production}
{wiki}

A fluctuation theorem compares the <probability> of a fluctuating <sample trajectory> with that of its reverse. Positive mean <entropy production> permits negative individual realizations: what is constrained is their relative <probability>, not a prohibition on every reversal. The finite statement below compares specified forward and reverse <probability laws>; replacing the reverse law by the forward law requires an additional symmetry. The <biased-step entropy production model> exhibits that symmetry exactly, while the <Crooks fluctuation theorem> compares two different driving protocols.

= Finite trajectory entropy production
{parent=Fluctuation theorem}
{title2=$\Sigma=\Delta S_{\rm tot}/k_B$}

Consider a finite-duration <Markov process> with discrete, time-even states coupled to one <thermal reservoir> at positive <temperature> $T$. Let $P_F[\gamma]$ include the initial <probability> $\rho_0(x_0)$ of the <sample trajectory> $\gamma$. Define $P_R$ by reversing the control protocol and initializing it with the actual forward final <probability distribution> $\rho_\tau$. Write $\Theta\gamma$ for the same states visited in reverse time order. Require normalized path laws with matching <probability support>: a path has positive forward weight exactly when its reverse has positive reverse weight. This excludes irreversible forbidden transitions and missing initial states.

Under <local detailed balance>, with positive <heat> $Q_{\rm bath}$ delivered to the <thermal reservoir>, the full path-weight <logarithm> is
$$
\Sigma_F[\gamma]=\ln\frac{P_F[\gamma]}{P_R[\Theta\gamma]}
=\frac{Q_{\rm bath}[\gamma]}{k_BT}+\ln\frac{\rho_0(x_0)}{\rho_\tau(x_\tau)}
=\frac{\Delta S_{\rm tot}[\gamma]}{k_B}.
$$
The endpoint term is the change in stochastic system <entropy>; the conditional path ratio alone accounts for the <thermal reservoir>. Here $k_B$ is the <Boltzmann constant>. Both terms matter even for a short observation. <Entropy production along a stochastic trajectory and an integral fluctuation theorem by Udo Seifert>[Seifert, 2005, Eqs. (14)–(18) and (30): conditional and full path weights, endpoint distributions, total entropy]{ref}

Define the conjugate reverse functional by $\Sigma_R[\eta]=\ln(P_R[\eta]/P_F[\Theta\eta])$, so $\Sigma_R[\Theta\gamma]=-\Sigma_F[\gamma]$. Summing the path identity over paths with a given value gives the exact finite <fluctuation theorem>
$$
\frac{p_F(s)}{p_R(-s)}=e^s,
$$
for nonzero denominator, using <probability masses> or corresponding <probability density functions>. This $\Sigma_R$ is defined by the paired laws: it need not be the total <entropy production> obtained by independently restarting the thermodynamic construction for the reverse experiment. A generic driven experiment therefore does not imply $p_F(s)/p_F(-s)=e^s$.

= Integral fluctuation theorem
{parent=Finite trajectory entropy production}
{title2=$\langle e^{-\Sigma_F}\rangle_F=1$}

Normalization of the reverse law gives an exact finite identity:
$$
\left\langle e^{-\Sigma_F}\right\rangle_F
=\sum_\gamma P_F[\gamma]\frac{P_R[\Theta\gamma]}{P_F[\gamma]}=1.
$$
The sum notation describes discrete paths; the corresponding path <integral> has the same change-of-variable argument. <Jensen's inequality> for the <exponential function> then gives $e^{-\langle\Sigma_F\rangle_F}\le1$, hence $\langle\Sigma_F\rangle_F\ge0$. Equivalently this mean is the <Kullback-Leibler divergence> between the forward path law and the reverse path law pulled back by $\Theta$. Equality requires those two laws to agree. <Entropy production along a stochastic trajectory and an integral fluctuation theorem by Udo Seifert>[Seifert, 2005, Eqs. (17)–(18): integral relation and its mean inequality]{ref}

Rare negative <entropy production> contributes disproportionately to this <expectation> through $e^{-\Sigma_F}$. Missing such events in a finite sample can spoil an estimate of the identity. Matching <probability support> is also essential: reverse paths absent from the forward law cannot be recovered by cancelling forward weights. This <second law of thermodynamics> statement concerns an <ensemble average>; an individual negative value is compatible with it.

= Biased-step entropy production model
{parent=Fluctuation theorem}
{title2=$S_n=a(2K-n),\ a=\ln(p/q)$}

Take $n$ <independent> steps, each $+1$ with <probability> $p>1/2$ and $-1$ with <probability> $q=1-p>0$. Reverse a step sequence by reading it backward and changing each sign; use the same biased law for both experiments. With $K$ positive steps, the forward sequence weight is $p^Kq^{n-K}$ and its reversed weight is $p^{n-K}q^K$. Their <logarithm> ratio is
$$
S_n=(2K-n)a,\qquad a=\ln(p/q)>0,\qquad
K\sim\operatorname{Binomial}(n,p).
$$
Here $S_n$ is dimensionless <entropy production>, not an <entropy> in joules per kelvin. A <Markov chain> on a three-state ring gives a state-space realization: start uniformly, take a clockwise step with <probability> $p$ and a counterclockwise step with <probability> $q$. The uniform <stationary distribution> makes endpoint weights cancel. A physical interpretation as exchanged <heat> requires <local detailed balance> with bath <entropy> $\pm k_Ba$ per directed step; the abstract model alone specifies path <probabilities>.

For an allowed value $s=a(2k-n)$, the <binomial distribution> gives
$$
p_n(s)=\binom nkp^kq^{n-k},\qquad
\frac{p_n(s)}{p_n(-s)}
=\frac{\binom nkp^kq^{n-k}}{\binom n{n-k}p^{n-k}q^k}
=\left(\frac pq\right)^{2k-n}=e^s.
$$
This is an exact same-law finite <fluctuation theorem>, with no long-time approximation. For $p=3/4$, $n=9$ and $k=6$, $s=3\ln3$ and $p_n(s)/p_n(-s)=27$. The positive mean is $\langle S_n\rangle=na(p-q)$, although the all-negative path has nonzero <probability> $q^n$. Directly, $\langle e^{-S_n}\rangle=(pe^{-a}+qe^a)^n=(q+p)^n=1$.

\Image[biased-step-entropy-probabilities.png]
{title=Exact biased-step model: discrete entropy-production probabilities and their log ratio at nine steps}

The left panel shows exact <probability masses> for $p=3/4$, with negative values highlighted; the right panel tests the <logarithm> ratio against $s$. These are model values, without experimental measurements or simulated measurement noise.

= Negative entropy production over finite durations
{parent=Biased-step entropy production model}
{title2=$\Pr(S_n<0)$}

A single step has negative <entropy production> with <probability> $q$, independent of how long an observation lasts. A whole window is negative only if negative steps outnumber positive ones:
$$
\Pr(S_n<0)=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor}\binom nkp^kq^{n-k}.
$$
For even $n$, the tie $K=n/2$ has zero <entropy production> and is excluded. At $p=3/4$, the negative-window <probabilities> for $n=1,9,21$ are approximately $0.25$, $0.04893$, and $0.00642$. Longer windows still contain negative increments: their expected number is $nq$. Their total is usually positive because the expected number of positive increments is $np$.

\Image[negative-entropy-window-duration.png]
{title=Exact biased-step model: negative totals become rare with duration while negative increments remain frequent}

Only odd window lengths are plotted to avoid alternating tie effects. The left panel gives the exact negative-window <probability> and the upper bound $(2\sqrt{pq})^n$; the right gives expected counts $np$ and $nq$. For steps of fixed duration $\delta t$, physical duration is $n\delta t$. These are counts and <probabilities> predicted by the model, not bead traces. The bound follows from $\Pr(S_n<0)\le\langle e^{-S_n/2}\rangle=(2\sqrt{pq})^n$. Since $2\sqrt{pq}<1$ for $p\ne q$, negative totals are exponentially suppressed even though the negative-step fraction stays $q$.

= Steady-state asymptotic fluctuation theorem
{parent=Fluctuation theorem}
{title2=$I(-s)-I(s)=s$}

A <steady state> does not by itself give every fluctuating observable an <entropy production> symmetry. If the dimensionless total <entropy production> $\Sigma_t$ has the same-law finite relation and a <large deviation principle> with <rate function> $I$ for $\Sigma_t/t$, then, on its common domain,
$$
p_t(\Sigma_t/t\approx s)\asymp e^{-tI(s)},\qquad
I(-s)-I(s)=s.
$$
The first expression denotes leading exponential scaling; the second follows by taking the scaled <logarithm> of the probability ratio. A finite-state, autonomous <Markov process> with time-even states, positive bidirectional rates and a positive <stationary distribution> supplies a standard setting for a finite total-<entropy production> relation. The stochastic system <entropy> is then bounded, so its endpoint contribution does not change the long-time <rate function> of bath <entropy production>. <Stochastic thermodynamics, fluctuation theorems, and molecular machines by Udo Seifert>[Seifert, 2012, §3.3.2, p. 22, Eq. (65); §4.4.3, p. 28, Eqs. (93)–(95): finite stationary total entropy and endpoint-conditioned medium entropy]{ref}

Unbounded state spaces can have endpoint fluctuations on the scale relevant to a <large deviation principle>. Dropping system <entropy>, changing the measured observable to <heat>, or changing the reverse preparation therefore requires a fresh argument. The exact finite <integral fluctuation theorem>, a same-law finite relation, and an asymptotic <rate function> symmetry are distinct claims.

= Crooks fluctuation theorem
{c}
{parent=Fluctuation theorem}
{title2=$P_F(W)/P_R(-W)=e^{\beta(W-\Delta F)}$}

For a system coupled to a <thermal reservoir> at fixed <temperature> $T$, prepare the forward process in the initial <canonical ensemble> and the reverse process in the final control parameter's <canonical ensemble>. Reverse the protocol, assume <microscopic reversibility> and matching path <probability support>, and count $W$ as <work> done on the system. With <Helmholtz free energy> difference $\Delta F=F_{\rm final}-F_{\rm initial}$ and $\beta=1/(k_BT)$,
$$
\frac{P_F(W)}{P_R(-W)}=e^{\beta(W-\Delta F)}.
$$
The final forward distribution need not already be in <thermal equilibrium>. Its reverse preparation is prescribed equilibrium preparation, rather than the actual forward final distribution used in <finite trajectory entropy production>. Thus the paired path <logarithm> $\beta(W-\Delta F)$ is dissipated <work> in thermal units, not automatically the instantaneous total stochastic <entropy production> at the end of an arbitrary driven protocol. <The Entropy Production Fluctuation Theorem and the Nonequilibrium Work Relation for Free Energy Differences by Gavin E. Crooks>[Crooks, 1999, Eqs. (5)–(7), (9)–(11): reversal, equilibrium weights and work relation]{ref} <Entropy production along a stochastic trajectory and an integral fluctuation theorem by Udo Seifert>[Seifert, 2005, Eq. (19) and following paragraph: actual final distribution versus final equilibrium distribution]{ref}

= Jarzynski equality
{c}
{parent=Crooks fluctuation theorem}
{title2=$\langle e^{-\beta W}\rangle_F=e^{-\beta\Delta F}$}

Integrating the <Crooks fluctuation theorem> over <work> and using normalization of the reverse <probability distribution> gives
$$
\langle e^{-\beta W}\rangle_F=e^{-\beta\Delta F}.
$$
Consequently <Jensen's inequality> gives $\langle W\rangle_F\ge\Delta F$. Some realizations may nevertheless have $W<\Delta F$. This is a <work> relation with equilibrium endpoint preparations, while the <integral fluctuation theorem> for total <entropy production> uses the actual final forward distribution. <The Entropy Production Fluctuation Theorem and the Nonequilibrium Work Relation for Free Energy Differences by Gavin E. Crooks>[Crooks, 1999, Eq. (3) and derivation from Eqs. (10)–(11)]{ref}

= Dragged-bead fluctuation theorem experiment
{parent=Fluctuation theorem}

The 2002 experiment trapped a $6.3\,\mathrm{\mu m}$ bead in <water> with a $980\,\mathrm{nm}$ <laser>, a $100\times$, <numerical aperture> $1.3$ objective and a piezo-driven microscope stage. A quadrant <photodiode> tracked bead position at $1\,\mathrm{kHz}$ with approximately $500\,\mathrm{Hz}$ bandwidth and reported $15\,\mathrm{nm}$ resolution. Stage translation drove water past the fixed <optical trap> at $1.25\,\mathrm{\mu m/s}$. Weak trapping prolonged the transient; stationary recording before translation established the position reference. <Stokes drag law> calibration was checked against the bead's measured <diffusion coefficient>. <Experimental Demonstration of Violations of the Second Law of Thermodynamics for Small Systems and Short Time Scales by G. M. Wang and others>[Wang and colleagues, 2002, p. 050601-2: apparatus, calibration and trajectory protocol]{ref}

From calibrated <displacement> and calibrated <harmonic optical trap> <force>, the experimenters evaluated the dimensionless driven-bead dissipation functional
$$
\Sigma_t^{\rm drive}=\frac1{k_BT}\int_0^t\mathbf v_{\rm opt}\cdot\mathbf F_{\rm opt}(u)\,du.
$$
Among 540 trajectories, negative values became less frequent with duration. Figure 2 compared the negative/positive count ratio with $\langle e^{-\Sigma_t^{\rm drive}}\rangle_{\Sigma_t^{\rm drive}>0}$; its agreement tested the paper's integrated transient relation. The detailed log-ratio plot in Figure 4 used simulated particle <sample trajectories>, not bead measurements. Near-zero sign discrimination and stage-start transients limited the earliest points. <Experimental Demonstration of Violations of the Second Law of Thermodynamics for Small Systems and Short Time Scales by G. M. Wang and others>[Wang and colleagues, 2002, Eq. (2), Eq. (3), pp. 050601-2–4 and Figs. 1, 2 and 4]{ref}

For any continuous <probability distribution> satisfying the same-law relation, integration over $s>0$ yields
$$
\frac{\Pr(\Sigma<0)}{\Pr(\Sigma>0)}
=\left\langle e^{-\Sigma}\right\rangle_{\Sigma>0}.
$$
This conditional relation explains the comparison without equating it to the unconditional <integral fluctuation theorem>. The experiment supports a specified driven-system relation; it does not establish a universal same-law symmetry for any definition of <heat> or <entropy production>. In particular, a transient work-like functional should not silently acquire the actual-distribution endpoint term in <finite trajectory entropy production>. Negative realizations are compatible with positive mean dissipation and the <second law of thermodynamics>; they do not provide a cyclic machine with reliable <work> output from a single <thermal reservoir>.

= Fluctuation-dissipation relation for a Langevin particle
{title2=$\sigma^2=2\zeta k_BT$}
{parent=Stochastic thermodynamics}

For a Langevin particle with drag coefficient $\zeta$ and white-force covariance amplitude $\sigma^2$, detailed balance at temperature $T$ requires $\sigma^2=2\zeta k_BT$.

= Vector noise normalization in inertial Langevin dynamics
{parent=Fluctuation-dissipation relation for a Langevin particle}
{title2=$\langle\mathbf A(t)\cdot\mathbf A(s)\rangle=(6\gamma k_BT/m^2)\delta(t-s)$}

Divide the inertial <Langevin equation> by mass and let $\zeta=\gamma/m$. An isotropic three-dimensional equilibrium velocity has $\langle|\mathbf u|^2\rangle=3k_BT/m$ by the <equipartition theorem>. Its white acceleration dot-covariance amplitude is therefore $6\zeta k_BT/m$, while the amplitude per Cartesian component is $2\zeta k_BT/m$. A scalar moment formula must use the component variance rather than the trace.

= Boltzmann constant
{title2=$k_B$}
{c}
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}

The Boltzmann constant converts temperature into an energy scale.

= Boltzmann distribution
{title2=$P_i=Z^{-1}e^{-\beta E_i}$}
{c}
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}

The <Boltzmann distribution> assigns <probabilities> to individual <microstates> in a <canonical ensemble>, a <statistical ensemble> for thermal contact. The system exchanges <energy> with a <thermal reservoir> at fixed positive absolute <temperature> $T$, while its particle number $N$ and external parameters, such as <volume>, stay fixed. The <thermal reservoir> is large enough that these exchanges barely change its <temperature>; the coupling permits <thermal equilibrium> but contributes negligible interaction <energy>. All the <microstates> considered have the same $N$. No <chemical potential> term is needed in their weights; fixed $N$ does not imply that the <chemical potential> is zero.

For a finite discrete set of <microstates> with <energies> $E_i$, write
$$
p_i=\frac{e^{-\beta E_i}}{Z},\qquad
\beta=\frac1{k_BT},\qquad
Z=\sum_i e^{-\beta E_i},\qquad
\sum_i p_i=1.
$$
Here $k_B$ is the <Boltzmann constant>, $\beta$ is the <inverse temperature>, $e^{-\beta E_i}$ is a <Boltzmann factor>, and $Z$ is the <partition function>. The <Boltzmann factor> is a weight; dividing by the <partition function> makes it a <probability>. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/5-62-physical-chemistry-ii-spring-2008/ec4f40d5a0fa41df3692f076e850543d_02_562ln08.pdf[MIT 5.62 Physical Chemistry II, spring 2008, lecture 2, pages 4–7: fixed N, V, T and canonical probabilities]{ref}

The useful surprise is that a fixed <temperature> still permits <thermal fluctuations> in the system's <energy>. The <Boltzmann distribution> predicts their <probabilities> and <expected values> without tracking every microscopic exchange. Its <exponential function> also makes rare excited <microstates> sensitive to <temperature>: an <energy> gap much larger than $k_BT$ gives strong <Boltzmann suppression>, while warming increases their relative populations. A population ratio can therefore reveal a gap or serve as a <thermometer>, provided <thermal equilibrium> and the relevant <state degeneracies> are known.

Historically, <Josiah Willard Gibbs>'s \i[Elementary Principles in Statistical Mechanics] (1902) presents the <canonical ensemble> in Chapter IV. His equations 90–92 give an <exponential function> of <energy> and its normalization, in classical <phase space>. This classical formulation exhibits the same weighting principle. https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Elementary_Principles_in_Statistical_Mechanics[Gibbs (1902), title page and preface]{ref} https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Elementary_Principles_in_Statistical_Mechanics/Chapter_IV[Gibbs (1902), Chapter IV, equations 90–92]{ref}

= Boltzmann distribution ratios and energy reference
{c}
{parent=Boltzmann distribution}
{title2=$p_i/p_j=e^{-\beta(E_i-E_j)}$}

For two individual <microstates>, the <partition function> cancels:
$$
\frac{p_i}{p_j}=e^{-\beta(E_i-E_j)}.
$$
At positive <temperature>, a higher-<energy> individual <microstate> is less probable, and equal-<energy> individual <microstates> are equally probable. For example, a gap $E_i-E_j=k_BT\ln2$, using the <natural logarithm>, gives $p_i/p_j=1/2$. This comparison concerns individual <microstates>; a whole <macrostate> can contain several of them.

Changing the reference for <energy> leaves every <probability> unchanged. If all <energies> become $E_i'=E_i+C$ for the same constant $C$, then
$$
Z'=e^{-\beta C}Z,\qquad
p_i'=\frac{e^{-\beta(E_i+C)}}{e^{-\beta C}Z}=p_i.
$$
The <partition function> itself changes, but its common factor cancels in the <Boltzmann distribution>. Thus choosing a <ground state> <energy> of zero simplifies calculations without changing populations. The <expected value> of <energy> shifts by $C$, as it must when its reference changes.

= Boltzmann distribution and state degeneracy
{c}
{parent=Boltzmann distribution}
{title2=$P(E)=g(E)e^{-\beta E}/Z$}

Group <microstates> by their <energy> $E$. The <state degeneracy> $g(E)$ counts how many <microstates> have that <energy>; their aggregate <probability> is
$$
P(E)=\sum_{i:E_i=E}p_i=\frac{g(E)e^{-\beta E}}{Z},\qquad
Z=\sum_{\text{distinct }E}g(E)e^{-\beta E}.
$$
Here $P(E)$ is the <probability> of the <macrostate> retaining only <energy>, while $p_i$ is the <probability> of one individual <microstate>. For two such <macrostates>,
$$
\frac{P(E_a)}{P(E_b)}=\frac{g(E_a)}{g(E_b)}e^{-\beta(E_a-E_b)}.
$$
Counting more <microstates> can compensate for a smaller <Boltzmann factor>. Omitting the <state degeneracy> confuses a sum over <microstates> with a sum over distinct <energies>. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/5-62-physical-chemistry-ii-spring-2008/bda82786b6014d3f468798339f658629_03_562ln08.pdf[MIT 5.62, spring 2008, lecture 3, page 4: degeneracy and the aggregate energy probability]{ref}

= Boltzmann distribution in a four-state system
{c}
{parent=Boltzmann distribution}
{title2=$Z=(1+q)^2$}

Consider two distinguishable, noninteracting <two-level systems>, each with <energies> $0$ and $\Delta>0$. Their four <microstates> $(0,0),(0,1),(1,0),(1,1)$ have total <energies> $0,\Delta,\Delta,2\Delta$. Thus the three <energy> <macrostates> have <state degeneracies> $1,2,1$. This is a stipulated finite model, not a measured material.

Set $q=e^{-\beta\Delta}$. The four individual <Boltzmann factors> are $1,q,q,q^2$, so the <partition function> and aggregate <probabilities> are
$$
Z=1+2q+q^2=(1+q)^2,\qquad
\bigl(P(0),P(\Delta),P(2\Delta)\bigr)=\frac{(1,2q,q^2)}{(1+q)^2}.
$$
Each of the two middle <microstates> has <probability> $q/Z$, while their shared <energy> <macrostate> has <probability> $2q/Z$.

At the <temperature> satisfying $\Delta=k_BT\ln2$, the <Boltzmann factor> is $q=1/2$ and the <partition function> is $Z=9/4$. Therefore
$$
\bigl(p_{00},p_{01},p_{10},p_{11}\bigr)=\left(\frac49,\frac29,\frac29,\frac19\right),\qquad
\bigl(P(0),P(\Delta),P(2\Delta)\bigr)=\left(\frac49,\frac49,\frac19\right).
$$
The middle <energy> is as likely as the <ground state> <energy> because its <state degeneracy> is twice as large. Yet each individual middle <microstate> is only half as likely as the <ground state> <microstate>. The diagram places the four <energies> beside their individual <probabilities>, then shows the sums within each <macrostate>.

\Image[boltzmann-microstates-and-degeneracy.png]
{title=Four <microstates> and three <energy> <macrostates> at a gap of $k_BT\ln2$}

The <expected value> of <energy> follows directly by weighting the three possible <energies>:
$$
\langle E\rangle=0\,P(0)+\Delta P(\Delta)+2\Delta P(2\Delta)
=\frac{2\Delta q+2\Delta q^2}{(1+q)^2}
=\frac{2\Delta q}{1+q}.
$$
At $q=1/2$ this is $2\Delta/3$. An individual <energy> measurement still returns $0$, $\Delta$ or $2\Delta$; the <expected value> describes the <ensemble average>, rather than a fourth possible measured <energy>.

= Boltzmann distribution temperature limits in a finite system
{c}
{parent=Boltzmann distribution}
{title2=$x=k_BT/\Delta$}

Keep the gap $\Delta$ fixed and vary positive <temperature>. With $x=k_BT/\Delta$, the <Boltzmann factor> is $q=e^{-1/x}$. In the four-<microstate> example, as $T\to0^+$, $q\to0$: the unique <ground state> has <probability> tending to one, and the other <microstates> have <probabilities> tending to zero. If several <microstates> instead shared the lowest <energy>, their <state degeneracy> would split that limiting population equally.

As $T\to\infty$ in this fixed finite model, all four individual <Boltzmann factors> tend to one, so all four individual <microstates> have <probability> tending to $1/4$. The three aggregate <probabilities> instead approach
$$
\bigl(P(0),P(\Delta),P(2\Delta)\bigr)\longrightarrow
\left(\frac14,\frac12,\frac14\right).
$$
The equal-population limit applies to this finite set of <microstates>. An infinite collection of <microstates> cannot all have the same positive normalized <probability>, so the statement cannot be extended to an unbounded <energy> spectrum. Real systems may also acquire additional accessible <microstates> as <temperature> rises.

\Image[boltzmann-populations-versus-temperature.png]
{title=Aggregate <Boltzmann distribution> populations versus positive <temperature> for <state degeneracies> 1, 2, 1}

The plotted crossing occurs at $x=1/\ln2$, where $P(0)=P(\Delta)=4/9$. For larger positive <temperatures>, the middle <macrostate> is more probable than the lowest-<energy> <macrostate>, although the lowest-<energy> individual <microstate> remains more probable than either individual middle <microstate>. The <expected value> of <energy> rises from zero toward $\Delta$ in this finite model.

= Statistical ensemble
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}

A <statistical ensemble> describes hypothetical copies of the same system under specified constraints, with a <probability distribution> over their <microstates>. An <ensemble average> is an <expected value> across those copies. The <canonical ensemble> fixes <temperature> and particle number while allowing <energy> exchange; the <microcanonical ensemble> fixes <energy>, and the <grand canonical ensemble> also allows particle exchange. These choices specify different physical preparations. https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Elementary_Principles_in_Statistical_Mechanics[Gibbs (1902), preface: ensembles of systems with different microscopic conditions]{ref}

= Temperature
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}

Temperature labels <thermal equilibrium>: two internally equilibrated bodies have the same <temperature> when contact permitting only <heat> exchange produces no net <heat transfer>. The <zeroth law of thermodynamics> makes the comparison transitive. If bodies A and B separately reach <thermal equilibrium> with the same <thermometer> without appreciably disturbing either body, they agree with each other. This is what lets a <gas thermometer> compare a metal block with <water>, despite their different <internal energies> and <heat capacities>. Insulation alone makes no such comparison: it can prevent <heat transfer> between bodies at different <temperatures>. <University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/1.1 Temperature and Thermal Equilibrium>[Ling, Moebs and Sanny, University Physics Volume 2, section 1.1: temperature and the zeroth law]{ref}

The <zeroth law of thermodynamics> establishes equality comparisons, but does not choose a numerical scale. <Thermodynamic temperature> supplies an absolute scale consistent with <Carnot's theorem> and <entropy>. For a <simple compressible system> at <thermodynamic equilibrium> with fixed particle number $N$ and composition,
$$
T=\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial S}\right)_{V,N},\qquad
\frac1T=\left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial U}\right)_{V,N}.
$$
Here $U$ is <internal energy>, $S$ is <entropy>, and $V$ is <volume (thermodynamics)>; the second equality assumes the derivative can be inverted. Temperature is a <thermodynamic conjugate variable> to <entropy> and belongs to the <intensive and extensive thermodynamic quantities> as an intensive quantity. Its <partial derivative> tells us how much <internal energy> must change per unit change of <entropy> under the stated constraints. More general systems require their other mechanical and material variables to be held fixed too. https://davidtong.org/teaching/statistical-physics/statmechhtml/S1#S1.SS2.SSS2[David Tong, Statistical Physics, section 1.2.2: temperature from the entropy derivative]{ref}

For an infinitesimal quantity $\delta Q>0$ of <heat> transferred from A to B, with no <work> or matter exchange, the equilibrium <entropy> changes give
$$
dS_{\rm total}=\delta Q\left(\frac1{T_B}-\frac1{T_A}\right).
$$
For ordinary positive <temperatures>, spontaneous <heat transfer> from $T_A>T_B$ increases total <entropy>; <thermal equilibrium> has $T_A=T_B$. This comparison uses changes in <entropy>, rather than the total <internal energy> contained in either body. A small hot object can have less <internal energy> than a large cool one. See <entropy maximization under thermal contact> for the equilibrium criterion and <negative temperature> for the special case of bounded <energy> spectra.

Express absolute <thermodynamic temperature> $T$ in <kelvin>, and a Celsius reading $t_C$ in <degree Celsius>:
$$
\frac{t_C}{{}^\circ\mathrm C}=\frac{T}{\mathrm K}-273.15,\qquad
\frac{\Delta t_C}{{}^\circ\mathrm C}=\frac{\Delta T}{\mathrm K}.
$$
The offset is exact: $300\,\mathrm K$ corresponds to $26.85\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, and $400\,\mathrm K$ to $126.85\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$. Their absolute <temperature> ratio is $4/3$; dividing their Celsius readings gives a different number. Thus <gas thermometer> pressure ratios and <Carnot's theorem> require <thermodynamic temperature>. The modern <SI> definition of the <kelvin> fixes the <Boltzmann constant> $k_B=1.380649\times10^{-23}\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}$; a reference bath realizes or transfers the scale rather than defining that constant.<The International System of Units (SI), ninth edition, SI Brochure>[section 2.3.1: kelvin and Celsius temperature]{ref}

Temperature is not universally the <mean> <kinetic energy> of a particle. In a classical, nonrelativistic <ideal gas> at <thermal equilibrium>, the <equipartition theorem> gives the mean translational <kinetic energy> relative to the gas's bulk motion:
$$
\left\langle\frac12m|\mathbf v-\mathbf u|^2\right\rangle=\frac32k_BT.
$$
Here $m$ is particle <mass>, $\mathbf v$ its <velocity>, and $\mathbf u$ the mean flow <velocity>. In this regime, different particle <masses> at the same <temperature> have equal mean translational <kinetic energies> but different characteristic speeds. A moving gas can also carry bulk <kinetic energy> without being hotter in its rest frame. Internal molecular modes and <quantum statistics> require further care; the <equipartition theorem> has classical assumptions, whereas the equilibrium <entropy> definition applies more widely. https://davidtong.org/teaching/statistical-physics/statmechhtml/S2#S2.SS2.SSS1[Tong, Statistical Physics, section 2.2.1: classical equipartition and its limits]{ref}

= Thermometer
{parent=Temperature}
{wiki}

A thermometer measures <temperature> through a physical response whose relation to <temperature> is known. Examples include the length change from <thermal expansion>, a change in <electrical resistance>, and the <pressure> of a <gas thermometer>. If the response <function> $X=f(T)$ is an <injective function> over the working range, its measured value determines $T=f^{-1}(X)$ through the <inverse function>. <Thermometer calibration> establishes that relation for the particular instrument and measurement conditions. Two matching readings are meaningful only after calibration to a common scale and with the probes sampling comparable equilibrium states. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/1-2-thermometers-and-temperature-scales[Ling, Moebs and Sanny, University Physics Volume 2, section 1.2: physical responses used in thermometers]{ref}

For a contact thermometer, the sensing part must reach <thermal equilibrium> with the region being measured. That region may differ from the rest of the object: an immersed probe near a heater can read a local <temperature> while the surrounding <water> remains cooler. A stable reading can also be a <steady state> set by competing <heat transfer> paths to the sample and the room. Good contact, adequate immersion, mixing where appropriate, and checks for spatial differences help establish which <temperature> the reading represents. These are measurement controls inferred from the <thermal equilibrium> requirement, not a guarantee that every flat trace is equilibrium.

A thermometer also changes the sample it touches. Consider an original toy calculation with an initially uniform sample at $T_o=350\,\mathrm K$, a probe at $T_p=300\,\mathrm K$, and constant whole-body <heat capacities> $C_o=100\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}$ and $C_p=1\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}$. Suppose together they form an <isolated system>, with no <work> or <phase transition> and negligible contact <energy>. The <first law of thermodynamics> and <thermal equilibrium> give
$$
T_f=\frac{C_oT_o+C_pT_p}{C_o+C_p}=349.50495\ldots\,\mathrm K,\qquad
T_f-T_o=\frac{C_p}{C_o+C_p}(T_p-T_o).
$$
The final <thermometer> reading underestimates the sample's original <temperature> by about $0.495\,\mathrm K$, although it correctly measures their new common <temperature>. The disturbance becomes small when the participating probe <heat capacity> is small compared with the sample's. A large controlled <heat bath> is a different limit: it supplies or removes <heat> while approximately maintaining its own <temperature>.

Waiting time is a separate issue from disturbance. Under a toy <Newton's law of cooling> model for a uniform probe in a maintained <heat bath>, a constant contact conductance $G$ gives
$$
C_p\frac{dT_p}{dt}=G(T_b-T_p),\qquad
T_p(t)=T_b+[T_p(0)-T_b]e^{-t/\tau},\qquad \tau=\frac{C_p}{G}.
$$
The <time constant> $\tau$ controls this <exponential decay>. For stipulated values $T_b=350\,\mathrm K$, $T_p(0)=300\,\mathrm K$ and $\tau=5\,\mathrm s$, the probe reads about $331.61\,\mathrm K$ after one <time constant> and $347.51\,\mathrm K$ after three. These are calculated values, not an instrument specification or measured data. Real probes may have multiple relaxation times and additional <heat transfer> paths; choose an equilibration criterion from the required accuracy, rather than assuming that a fixed waiting time works universally.

= Resistance thermometer
{parent=Thermometer}
{wiki}

A <thermometer> that infers <temperature> from a calibrated <electrical resistance>. Measuring current can heat its sensing element; extrapolation toward zero current controls that bias in the <saturation-pressure equilibrium measurement>.

= Platinum resistance thermometer
{parent=Resistance thermometer}

A <resistance thermometer> using <platinum> as its sensing material. It provides the <temperature> readout in the <saturation-pressure equilibrium measurement>.

= Gas thermometer
{parent=Thermometer}
{title2=$P\propto T\quad(N,V\text{ fixed})$}

A gas thermometer infers <temperature> from the <equation of state> of a confined <gas>. The constant-<volume (thermodynamics)> version measures <pressure>; the constant-<pressure> version measures <volume (thermodynamics)>. For an <ideal gas>,
$$
PV=Nk_BT=nRT,\qquad
\frac{T}{T_0}=\frac{P}{P_0}\quad(N,V\text{ fixed}),\qquad
\frac{T}{T_0}=\frac{V}{V_0}\quad(N,P\text{ fixed}).
$$
Here $N$ is particle number, $n$ is <amount of substance>, $k_B$ is the <Boltzmann constant>, and $R$ is the <molar gas constant>. The reference and test states must be internally equilibrated, and $T_0$ must be known on the absolute <thermodynamic temperature> scale. A pressure ratio cancels the unknown gas inventory and bulb size in the ideal constant-volume limit. This cancellation is the method's appeal: a mechanical measurement transfers an absolute <temperature> scale between different materials. The <Maxwell speed distribution> and <ideal gas pressure from wall collisions> explain the microscopic origin of the response. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/2-1-molecular-model-of-an-ideal-gas[OpenStax, University Physics Volume 2, section 2.1: the ideal gas law]{ref}

For an original ideal-model calculation, choose $T_0=300\,\mathrm K$, $P_0=120\,\mathrm{kPa}$ and $P=160\,\mathrm{kPa}$. Then $T=400\,\mathrm K$. These are stipulated values, not observations. Both <pressures> must be absolute, referred to vacuum. If the surrounding <atmospheric pressure> were $100\,\mathrm{kPa}$, the corresponding <gauge pressures> would be $20$ and $60\,\mathrm{kPa}$; their ratio is three, which would incorrectly predict $900\,\mathrm K$. A pressure difference from the atmosphere cannot be substituted into the <ideal gas law>.

\Image[temperature-thermometry-gas-apparatus.png]
{title=An original schematic of constant-volume <gas thermometer> measurements}
{description=Simplified measurement layout inspired by the NBS apparatus, not a replica or a report of measurements. A controlled bath thermalizes the bulb, separate calibrated <thermometers> check its surroundings, and a diaphragm transmits <pressure> to an absolute-pressure standard without mixing gases. The connecting tube contains gas at other <temperatures>; its inventory belongs in the <gas thermometer dead-space correction>.}

\b[A motivating calibration experiment.] Schooley's account of the NBS/NIST work describes a <helium>-filled metal bulb, a capillary, a <capacitance diaphragm gauge>, and a mercury pressure standard. Circulating bath liquid passed the bulb assembly and platinum resistance <thermometers>. The resistance probes checked the surrounding <temperature>; gas <pressure> supplied the independent thermodynamic comparison. In an early series, workers filled the cleaned bulb with purified <helium> at the highest test <temperature>, checked <pressure> over three hours, then measured at lower <temperatures> and near a reference at $0\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$. Six filling pressures allowed extrapolation toward zero pressure. https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/jres/095/jresv95n3p255_A1b.pdf[J. F. Schooley (1990), NBS/NIST Gas Thermometry From 0 to 660 °C, sections 4–5]{ref}

To turn this principle into a readable measurement protocol, use the following controls. These steps are a proposed teaching interpretation of the cited apparatus, rather than additional reported NBS observations:
* \b[Define the inventory.] Evacuate and clean the bulb and connecting tube, fill with a known pure <gas>, close the filling valve, and keep the same total particle number during each reference/test comparison. Monitor <pressure> at a maintained <temperature> before and after a run. A leak changes $N$; a slow drift can also reflect contamination, sorption, or imperfect equilibration, so pressure stability alone does not identify its cause.
* \b[Measure the reference.] Place the bulb in a controlled environment with independently assigned $T_0$. Record $P_0$ after thermalization, together with the independent <thermometer> readings and their spread. A reference near $0\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$ has an absolute <temperature> near $273.15\,\mathrm K$, rather than zero <temperature>. Repeating reference readings tests whether the inventory or apparatus changed during the comparison.
* \b[Measure the test state.] Change the bath setting, allow the bulb and <gas> to settle, and record $P$ only when the surrounding <thermometers> and <pressure> are sufficiently stable for the intended accuracy. Compare readings at different positions and vary the settling time. Record the connecting tube's <temperature> profile too: uniform bath <temperature> does not make the entire connecting tube isothermal.
* \b[Measure the volume changes.] Determine the bulb's <thermal expansion> and its deformation from differences between inside and outside <pressure>. Measure the connecting tube's dimensions and the effective valve volume. “Constant volume” is the ideal model; a real vessel expands. These measurements supply corrections, even though the absolute bulb volume cancels in the simplest pressure ratio.
* \b[Measure pressure against a reference.] For a liquid-column pressure standard, measure the vertical column height, the liquid's <temperature>-dependent <mass density>, local gravitational acceleration, and the reference-side <pressure>. Include diaphragm offsets and the gas's <hydrostatic pressure> change between the sensor and bulb. The pressure needed in the <equation of state> is at the bulb, not automatically at the remote gauge.

The corresponding NBS apparatus used a protective case with an outer <helium> layer held close to the bulb <pressure>, reducing pressure deformation while aiding thermal contact. <Thermal expansion> was measured on bulb material. Gas in the capillary remained partly outside the hot region, requiring a measured tube <temperature> profile and geometry. Pure working <gas> also mattered: contaminants that sorb on surfaces can change the freely circulating inventory. These are physical effects to measure or bound, rather than scatter that disappears by averaging. https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/IR/nbsir85-3133.pdf[J. F. Schooley, Thermometry, NBS IR 85-3133, chapter V, gas thermometry and the NBS experiment]{ref}

For illustration, neglect connecting-tube gas and describe a uniform bulb with a dilute <virial expansion> in particle <number density> $\rho_N=N/V$:
$$
PV=Nk_BT\left[1+B_2(T)\rho_N+O(\rho_N^2)\right],\qquad
\frac{T}{T_0}=\frac{P}{P_0}\frac{V(T)}{V(T_0)}\frac{Z(T_0)}{Z(T)}.
$$
Here $Z=PV/(Nk_BT)$ and $B_2$ is the <second virial coefficient> in the particle-density convention; a molar-density convention rescales the coefficient. The second relation follows exactly from the definition of $Z$ for the same $N$, and shows the signs of the corrections. If the bulb expands by $0.1\%$ and $Z=1$ at both states, the toy pressure ratio above gives $400.4\,\mathrm K$, rather than $400\,\mathrm K$. Improved expansion and <helium> nonideality models really changed the temperatures inferred from the 1989 NIST data: Harvey's 2023 reanalysis found expansion updates of roughly $1$–$3\,\mathrm{mK}$ and nonideality updates of roughly $-0.1$ to $-0.5\,\mathrm{mK}$. Those are reported corrections to historical results, not the much larger illustrative correction just calculated. https://tsapps.nist.gov/publication/get_pdf.cfm?pub_id=956523[Allan H. Harvey (2023), Small Corrections to 1989 NIST Constant-Volume Gas Thermometry Data, sections 2–3]{ref}

With equal corrected bulb <volumes>, negligible or corrected connecting-tube gas, fixed reference and test <temperatures>, and a dilute classical single gas phase,
$$
\frac{P}{P_0}=\frac{T}{T_0}\left[1+\bigl(B_2(T)-B_2(T_0)\bigr)\rho_N+O(\rho_N^2)\right],\qquad
\lim_{\rho_N\to0}\frac{P}{P_0}=\frac{T}{T_0}.
$$
Repeat the reference/test comparison for several sealed fills, reducing $N$ between comparisons but keeping it fixed within each pair. The extrapolation varies density at fixed endpoint <temperatures>; it does not cool one fill toward zero <temperature>. Real gases have different finite-density corrections but share the classical <ideal gas> intercept under these assumptions. The limit does not remove leaks, bulb expansion, gauge offsets, or the <gas thermometer dead-space correction>. At very low <pressures>, <thermal transpiration> can make remote sensor and bulb <pressures> differ through a nonisothermal capillary; this must be corrected too. The NBS work measured this pressure correction using a separate comparison of narrow and wider tubes. https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/jres/095/jresv95n3p255_A1b.pdf[Schooley (1990), sections 7.3–7.6: pressure, volume, dead space and gas nonideality]{ref}

\Image[temperature-thermometry-pressure-extrapolation.png]
{title=Ideal pressure ratios and a dilute-limit <gas thermometer> comparison in two toy gas models}
{description=Left: the stipulated ideal constant-volume calibration $P=0.4\,\mathrm{kPa\,K^{-1}}T$. Right: apparent <temperature> $T_{\rm app}=300\,\mathrm K\,P/P_0$ for invented dimensionless fill $x=\rho_N/\rho_*$, with $P/P_0=(4/3)(1+0.03x)/(1+0.01x)$ for model A and $(4/3)(1-0.02x)/(1+0.01x)$ for model B. Both approach the stipulated $400\,\mathrm K$ intercept; neither curve represents a named gas or measured data.}

At $x=1$, the toy models give $T_{\rm app}=407.92\,\mathrm K$ and $388.12\,\mathrm K$, respectively, although their imposed true test <temperature> is the same. Their zero-density intercept is the reusable quantity. Measuring several low-density points permits a fit and a check for curvature; asserting a straight line from one finite-density point supplies neither. A practical fit must balance nonideality against increasing relative pressure error and the capillary corrections, and keep its extrapolation uncertainty.
