= Gas thermometer dead-space correction
{parent=Gas thermometer}

Gas outside the uniform-temperature bulb of a <gas thermometer> contributes to its conserved particle inventory. An idealized calculation divides the connecting tube into small regions with <volumes> $V_j$ and local <temperatures> $T_j$. Neglect <hydrostatic pressure> offsets and <thermal transpiration>, assume a common <pressure> $P$ and a local <ideal gas law> in each region, and let the bulb have <volume (thermodynamics)> $V_b$ and <temperature> $T_b$. Adding the particle inventories gives
$$
N=\frac{P}{k_B}\left(\frac{V_b}{T_b}+\sum_j\frac{V_j}{T_j}\right).
$$
Thus even an <ideal gas> in a perfectly rigid sealed apparatus need not obey $P\propto T_b$: some of its particles remain in regions with other <temperatures>. For a stipulated bulb of $100\,\mathrm{cm^3}$, a connecting volume of $1\,\mathrm{cm^3}$ maintained at $300\,\mathrm K$, and bulb reference and test <temperatures> of $300$ and $400\,\mathrm K$, conservation of $N$ gives
$$
\frac{P}{P_0}=\frac{100/300+1/300}{100/400+1/300}
=\frac{101}{76}\approx1.328947,\qquad
T_{\rm app}=300\,\mathrm K\frac{P}{P_0}\approx398.684\,\mathrm K.
$$
These are toy dimensions and a calculated bias, not measured data. Reducing $N$ reduces both <pressures> proportionally and leaves this ratio unchanged: extrapolating away gas nonideality does not eliminate the connecting-volume error. Actual apparatus also requires the tube's <thermal expansion>, a measured <temperature> profile, and the pressure corrections omitted in this model.

= Thermometer calibration
{parent=Thermometer}

<Thermometer> calibration compares an instrument's response with assigned reference <temperatures> under specified conditions. In a proposed two-reference procedure, thermalize the sensing part at $T_0$ and $T_1$, record responses $X_0$ and $X_1$, and, only if a linear response is justified over that interval, use
$$
T(X)=T_0+(T_1-T_0)\frac{X-X_0}{X_1-X_0}.
$$
Additional reference <temperatures> test curvature; returning to the first reference tests drift. A <gas thermometer> instead obtains its response from an <equation of state>, with a reference and corrections for its apparatus. In either case, uncertainty in the reference propagates to the inferred <temperature>. Calibration determines the response relation; it cannot by itself remove the finite-sample disturbance or incomplete <thermal equilibrium> described in <thermometer>. A reference established on a practical scale can also differ slightly from true <thermodynamic temperature>; the BIPM distinguishes the International Temperature Scale of 1990 readings from their thermodynamic counterparts.<The International System of Units (SI), ninth edition, SI Brochure>[section 2.3.1: practical and thermodynamic temperature]{ref}

= Kelvin
{parent=Temperature}
{title2=$\mathrm K$}

The kelvin is the SI unit of <thermodynamic temperature>, with symbol $\mathrm K$. Its definition fixes the numerical value of the <Boltzmann constant>; it is written without a degree sign.https://www.bipm.org/en/si-base-units/kelvin[BIPM, SI unit kelvin]{ref}

= Degree Celsius
{parent=Temperature}
{title2=${}^\circ\mathrm C$}

The degree Celsius has the same size as the <kelvin>, with a shifted temperature origin: $t/{}^\circ\mathrm C=T/\mathrm K-273.15$. Thus a change of one degree Celsius is a change of one <kelvin>, but temperature ratios in <Carnot's theorem> must use absolute <thermodynamic temperature>.<The International System of Units (SI), ninth edition, SI Brochure>{ref}

= Inverse temperature
{parent=Temperature}
{title2=$\beta=1/(k_BT)$}

<Inverse temperature> is $\beta=1/(k_BT)$, where $k_B$ is the <Boltzmann constant> and $T$ is the absolute <temperature>. A <canonical ensemble> weights an energy $E$ by its <Boltzmann factor> $e^{-\beta E}$. Calling couplings dimensionless means absorbing this factor $\beta$ into their energy coefficients. The alternative convention $k_B=1$ makes <inverse temperature> $1/T$; physical and dimensionless couplings must not be mixed.

In a thermal <Euclidean worldline path integral> with $\hbar=1$, $\beta$ is also the circumference of the imaginary-time circle.

= Thermal equilibrium
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}

Two internally equilibrated systems are in thermal equilibrium if bringing them into contact that permits only <heat> exchange causes no net <heat transfer>. In ordinary nonrelativistic <thermodynamics> without appreciable gravitational temperature shifts, their <thermodynamic temperatures> are equal. Merely preventing <heat transfer> with insulation does not establish <thermal equilibrium>: two insulated bodies can have different <temperatures>. <University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/1.1 Temperature and Thermal Equilibrium>[Samuel J. Ling, William Moebs and Jeff Sanny, OpenStax University Physics Volume 2, section 1.1]{ref}

A <thermometer> illustrates the definition. Its own <temperature> settles to the object's <temperature> after thermal contact, provided its effect on the object is negligible. The existing <zeroth law of thermodynamics> makes this comparison transitive: two objects separately in <thermal equilibrium> with the same <thermometer> are in <thermal equilibrium> with each other. This lets one calibrated scale compare different materials.

Equal <temperatures> do not imply equal <internal energies> or equal <heat capacities>. Nor does <thermal equilibrium> by itself imply <mechanical equilibrium> or <chemical equilibrium>; these require additional allowed exchanges. There is also a distinction between no net exchange in <thermal equilibrium> and a constant <temperature> maintained by equal incoming and outgoing <heat>. The latter can be a <steady state> with ongoing <entropy production>. Microscopic exchanges can continue at <thermal equilibrium>, but their opposing contributions balance on average.

Historically, OpenStax credits the name “zeroth law” to Ralph Fowler in the 1930s: the already numbered laws needed a logically earlier statement about <thermal equilibrium>. <University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/1.1 Temperature and Thermal Equilibrium>[Ling, Moebs and Sanny, section 1.1: naming the zeroth law]{ref}

= Thermal equilibration of two finite bodies
{parent=Thermal equilibrium}
{title2=$T_f=(C_1T_{1,0}+C_2T_{2,0})/(C_1+C_2)$}

Consider two rigid bodies forming an <isolated system>, with fixed composition and constant whole-body <heat capacity at constant volume> values $C_1=1\,\mathrm{kJ\,K^{-1}}$ and $C_2=3\,\mathrm{kJ\,K^{-1}}$. Their initial <temperatures> are $T_{1,0}=300\,\mathrm K$ and $T_{2,0}=400\,\mathrm K$. Assume each body remains internally uniform, their contact stores negligible <energy>, no <phase transition> occurs and no <work> is done or extracted. These are stipulated model values, not material measurements.

Because $dU_i=C_i\,dT_i$, write $U_i=a_i+C_iT_i$ with arbitrary fixed reference constants $a_i$. The conserved relative <internal energy> is
$$
E=U_1+U_2-a_1-a_2=C_1T_1+C_2T_2=1500\,\mathrm{kJ}.
$$
This is an <energy> reference convention, not a claim about actual <heat capacities> down to zero <temperature>. At <thermal equilibrium> the two <temperatures> equal $T_f$, so the <first law of thermodynamics> gives
$$
T_f=\frac{C_1T_{1,0}+C_2T_{2,0}}{C_1+C_2}=375\,\mathrm K,\qquad
\Delta U_1=75\,\mathrm{kJ},\qquad \Delta U_2=-75\,\mathrm{kJ}.
$$
The larger <heat capacity> pulls the common <temperature> closer to its initial value: body 1 warms by $75\,\mathrm K$, while body 2 cools by $25\,\mathrm K$.

\Image[thermal-equilibrium-two-bodies.png]
{title=Two finite bodies approach <thermal equilibrium> at $375\,\mathrm K$}
{description=Ideal model with $C_1=1$ and $C_2=3\,\mathrm{kJ\,K^{-1}}$ and contact conductance $G=0.1\,\mathrm{kJ\,s^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. The weighted <temperature> stays at $375\,\mathrm K$ and the relative <internal energy> stays at $1500\,\mathrm{kJ}$; the plotted curves are model predictions.}

To predict a timescale, stipulate a constant contact conductance $G=1/R_{\rm th}=0.1\,\mathrm{kJ\,s^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$, the reciprocal of the contact's <thermal resistance>. The rate of <heat transfer> into body 1 is $G(T_2-T_1)$, giving
$$
C_1\dot T_1=G(T_2-T_1),\qquad C_2\dot T_2=-G(T_2-T_1).
$$
Adding proves conservation of $E$. Subtracting gives <exponential decay> of the <temperature> difference, with <time constant>
$$
\tau=\frac{C_1C_2}{G(C_1+C_2)}=7.5\,\mathrm s,\qquad
T_2-T_1=100\,\mathrm K\,e^{-t/\tau},
$$
and therefore
$$
T_1(t)=375\,\mathrm K-75\,\mathrm K\,e^{-t/\tau},\qquad
T_2(t)=375\,\mathrm K+25\,\mathrm K\,e^{-t/\tau}.
$$
Exact equality is approached as $t\to\infty$ in this ideal model. Changing $G$ changes the rate but not $T_f$: equilibrium and the speed of reaching it are separate predictions. Real bodies can have internal <temperature gradients>, changing <heat capacities> and contact losses, so the plotted $7.5\,\mathrm s$ is an assumed model timescale.

= Tolman–Ehrenfest relation
{c}
{parent=Thermal equilibrium}
{title2=$T\sqrt{-k^2}=\mathrm{constant}$}

= Tolman temperature law
{c}
{synonym}

In static <thermal equilibrium>, the local temperature times the stationary lapse is constant. Energy measured locally redshifts to conserved Killing energy by that same lapse; requiring a single equilibrium Boltzmann exponent gives the relation. The <Killing vector field> normalization fixes the constant but not the ratio of temperatures at different locations.

= Boltzmann suppression
{parent=Thermal equilibrium}
{c}
{title2=$e^{-m/T}$}

<Boltzmann suppression> is the exponential reduction of an equilibrium population when creating it costs energy much greater than the temperature. For a nonrelativistic species with negligible <chemical potential>, the leading factor is $e^{-m/T}$, multiplied by its momentum phase-space volume. It concerns the equilibrium abundance; freeze-out can preserve a larger population than the instantaneous equilibrium value at later times.

= Boltzmann suppression of a nonrelativistic relic
{parent=Boltzmann suppression}
{c}
{title2=$n_{\rm eq}\simeq g(mT/2\pi)^{3/2}e^{-m/T}$}

A nonrelativistic species with $g$ states and negligible <chemical potential> has equilibrium density $g(mT/(2\pi))^{3/2}e^{-m/T}$. If particle-antiparticle annihilation maintains equilibrium after the species becomes massive, its eventual freeze-out population can be much smaller than a relativistically decoupled population. The nonrelativistic annihilation rate and any asymmetry determine the final abundance.

= Kinetic equilibrium
{parent=Thermal equilibrium}

Momentum-exchange collisions maintain a common <temperature> and an equilibrium-shaped momentum <probability distribution>. Particle abundances and <chemical potentials> need not have their <chemical equilibrium> values: elastic scattering alone conserves particle number. Thus kinetic equilibrium does not by itself imply either zero <chemical potential> or conserved comoving <entropy>.

= Heat bath
{parent=Thermal equilibrium}
{wiki}

= Thermal reservoir
{synonym}

A heat bath, or thermal reservoir, is an idealized system so large that exchanging a finite amount of <heat> does not appreciably change its <temperature>.

= Entropy maximization under thermal contact
{parent=Thermal equilibrium}
{title2=$\partial S_{\rm tot}/\partial E_1=1/T_1-1/T_2$}

At fixed <volumes> and material inventories, two internally equilibrated bodies that exchange only <heat> within an <isolated system> have fixed total <internal energy> $E=E_1+E_2$. Their total <entropy> is
$$
S_{\mathrm{tot}}(E_1)=S_1(E_1)+S_2(E-E_1).
$$
Using $1/T_i=(\partial S_i/\partial E_i)_{V_i,N_i}$, an interior stationary point obeys
$$
\frac{dS_{\rm tot}}{dE_1}=\frac1{T_1}-\frac1{T_2}=0.
$$
Thus <entropy> stationarity requires matching <thermodynamic temperatures>. Positive values of <heat capacity at constant volume> make this a strict local maximum because
$$
\frac{d^2S_{\rm tot}}{dE_1^2}=-\frac1{C_1T_1^2}-\frac1{C_2T_2^2}<0.
$$
For bodies with positive <heat capacities> throughout the accessible range, this <concavity> gives at most one interior maximum. This is the stability check behind the equal-<temperature> condition; see <entropy concavity and positive heat capacity>. https://davidtong.org/pdfs/teaching/statistical-physics/statphys1.pdf[David Tong, Statistical Physics, sections 1.2.1–1.2.2: entropy maximization, temperature and heat capacity]{ref}

For <thermal equilibration of two finite bodies>, <entropy from heat capacity> gives
$$
\Delta S_{\rm tot}=C_1\ln\frac{T_f}{T_{1,0}}+C_2\ln\frac{T_f}{T_{2,0}}
=\left(\ln\frac{375}{300}+3\ln\frac{375}{400}\right)\mathrm{kJ\,K^{-1}}
\simeq0.02952799\,\mathrm{kJ\,K^{-1}}>0.
$$
Body 1 gains about $0.22314355\,\mathrm{kJ\,K^{-1}}$ of <entropy>, while body 2 loses about $0.19361556\,\mathrm{kJ\,K^{-1}}$. Total <energy> is conserved even though total <entropy> increases. The <entropy> calculation uses reversible comparison paths between the endpoint states; actual exchange across a finite <temperature> difference is irreversible.

\Image[thermal-equilibrium-entropy-maximum.png]
{title=At fixed total <energy>, matching <temperatures> maximize total <entropy>}
{description=The curve is total <entropy> relative to the initial $300\,\mathrm K$, $400\,\mathrm K$ state. Every point satisfies $C_1T_1+C_2T_2=1500\,\mathrm{kJ}$. Only $250\le T_1/\mathrm K\le500$ is shown; the mathematical model's positive-<temperature> domain is $0<T_1/\mathrm K<1500$.}

To construct the plot, vary $T_1$ while enforcing the <energy> constraint, rather than holding $T_2$ fixed. In numerical units of <kelvin>, $T_2/\mathrm K=(1500-T_1/\mathrm K)/3$, and the plotted function is
$$
\mathcal S(T_1)=C_1\ln\frac{T_1}{300\,\mathrm K}+C_2\ln\frac{T_2(T_1)}{400\,\mathrm K},\qquad
\frac{dT_2}{dT_1}=-\frac{C_1}{C_2}.
$$
Here $\mathcal S$ means $S_{\rm tot}-S_{\rm tot,initial}$, so its zero is a reference choice. The <chain rule> yields
$$
\mathcal S'(T_1)=\frac{C_1}{T_1}-\frac{C_1}{T_2},\qquad
\mathcal S''(T_1)=-\frac{C_1}{T_1^2}-\frac{C_1^2}{C_2T_2^2}<0.
$$
The unique maximum is at $T_1=T_2=375\,\mathrm K$. The physical trajectory starts at $T_1=300\,\mathrm K$ and moves toward that maximum; the rest of the curve compares other possible <energy> partitions, not later times in the trajectory.

The contact model also proves the direction of change directly:
$$
\dot S_{\rm tot}=G(T_2-T_1)\left(\frac1{T_1}-\frac1{T_2}\right)
=\frac{G(T_2-T_1)^2}{T_1T_2}\ge0.
$$
For $G>0$ and positive <temperatures>, equality holds exactly at <thermal equilibrium>. The appealing link is that a dynamical <heat transfer> law moves the system uphill on the constrained <entropy> curve, while conservation of <energy> keeps it on that curve. This predicts both why the endpoint is stable and why the reverse separation into hot and cold bodies cannot happen spontaneously in the <isolated system>.

= Entropy
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{title2=$S$}
{wiki}

Entropy is an <extensive quantity> and a <state function> of an equilibrium system, measured in $\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}$. Differences between equilibrium states are calculated along a <reversible thermodynamic process>:
$$
S_2-S_1=\int_1^2\frac{\delta Q_{\rm rev}}{T}.
$$
Here <heat> entering the system is positive and $T$ is its absolute <thermodynamic temperature>. The reversible path used for calculation need not be the path actually taken. Dividing the actual <heat> by a single <temperature> generally fails for an irreversible process.

For example, put two rigid bodies, each with constant <heat capacity> $C=100\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}$, at $600\,\mathrm K$ and $300\,\mathrm K$ in an otherwise <isolated system>. Assume each body has a uniform internal <temperature>, no <phase transition> occurs, and no <work> is extracted. Their final <thermal equilibrium> is at $450\,\mathrm K$, because the <internal energy> lost by one equals that gained by the other. Using <entropy from heat capacity>,
$$
\Delta S_h=C\log(450/600)=-28.77\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}},\qquad
\Delta S_c=C\log(450/300)=40.55\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}},
$$
so $\Delta S_{\rm tot}=C\log(9/8)=11.78\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}$. One body's <entropy> decreases while the total increases. https://web.mit.edu/16.unified/www/FALL/thermodynamics/notes/node41.html[MIT, Entropy changes and the hot-brick problem]{ref}

\Image[heat-exchange-entropy.png]
{title=Thermal equilibration of two finite bodies conserves energy and increases total entropy}

In <statistical mechanics>, <Boltzmann's entropy formula> counts equally likely <microstates> compatible with a <macrostate>; <Gibbs entropy> handles a specified <probability distribution> over <microstates>. If the number of equally likely <microstates> doubles, <entropy> increases by $k_B\log2$. The <logarithm> makes multiplicative counts additive for independent systems. These are precise statements about states and probabilities, not a universal measure of visible messiness. Calling <entropy> merely "disorder" obscures the thermal calculation above and hides which <microstates> are being counted. https://web.mit.edu/16.unified/www/FALL/thermodynamics/notes/node57.html[MIT, Statistical entropy and randomness]{ref}

The usefulness of <entropy> is that measurable <heat capacities> determine its changes, and the <second law of thermodynamics> turns those changes into constraints on processes. It also connects macroscopic measurements to microscopic descriptions without requiring us to track every particle.

= Specific entropy
{title2=$s=S/M$}
{parent=Entropy}

Specific entropy is <entropy> per unit mass. For a <perfect gas> with constant <specific heat capacity> $c_v$ at constant volume and <specific-heat ratio> $\gamma$, differences obey
$$
s_2-s_1=c_v\log\left[\frac{p_2}{p_1}\left(\frac{\rho_1}{\rho_2}\right)^\gamma\right],
$$
where $p$ is <pressure> and $\rho$ is <mass density>. Thus a <homentropic flow> has constant $p/\rho^\gamma$.

= Boltzmann's entropy formula
{title2=$S=k_B\log\Omega$}
{c}
{parent=Entropy}
{wiki}

= Boltzmann entropy
{c}
{synonym}

Why does a <gas> spread through an available container? One part of the answer is counting: a nearly uniform <macrostate> can be realized by vastly more <microstates> than a <macrostate> with every particle crowded into one corner. <Boltzmann's entropy formula> turns that multiplicity into <entropy>. The useful insight is that a large <entropy> measures how many microscopic realizations fit a specified macroscopic description, rather than how untidy a picture looks.

For a specified <macrostate> $M$ containing $\Omega(M)$ accessible, equally likely <microstates>, write
$$
S(M)=k_B\ln\Omega(M),\qquad p_i=\frac1{\Omega(M)}\quad(i\text{ compatible with }M).
$$
Here $k_B$ is the <Boltzmann constant>, and $\ln$ is the <natural logarithm>, also written $\log$ in this article's title. The count $\Omega(M)$ is dimensionless; <entropy> has units of <energy> divided by <temperature>. Always specify the <macrostate>, the constraints and the model's microscopic resolution before counting. A <microcanonical ensemble> provides a familiar equilibrium setting for the equal-<probability> assumption. https://web.mit.edu/16.unified/www/FALL/thermodynamics/notes/node57.html[MIT, §7.4: equiprobable microstates and S = k ln Ω]{ref}

The <natural logarithm> compresses an enormous count into a manageable number and makes products of counts into sums of <entropies>; see <additivity of Boltzmann entropy>. With this counting convention, one allowed <microstate> has zero <Boltzmann entropy>. This zero refers to the chosen model and resolution; it does not by itself assign an absolute <entropy> to a real material.

= Boltzmann entropy of a two-box occupancy
{c}
{parent=Boltzmann's entropy formula}
{title2=$S(k)=k_B\ln\binom Nk$}

Take $N=20$ distinguishable, labeled particles and two boxes of equal <volume>. Retain only whether each particle is in the left or right box: use a <bit> $0$ for left and $1$ for right. Each length-20 string is one coarse <microstate> of this toy model. Assume the labels make <independent> side choices with equal <probability>, so the $2^{20}=1\,048\,576$ strings are equiprobable. Equal <volumes> motivate these weights for noninteracting particles with no preference for either box; equal <volumes> alone would not establish them for an arbitrary interacting <gas>.

Now retain only the number $k$ in the left box as the <macrostate>. Choosing which $k$ labels are left gives a <binomial coefficient>, so
$$
\Omega(k)=\binom{20}{k}=\frac{20!}{k!(20-k)!},\qquad
S(k)=k_B\ln\binom{20}{k},\qquad
P(k)=\frac{\binom{20}{k}}{2^{20}}.
$$
The <factorials> count assignments to labels; the <probability> of a <macrostate> sums the <probabilities> of its constituent <microstates>. This is the <binomial distribution> with parameters $20$ and $1/2$. The same <binomial coefficient> appears in the counting of distinguishable <two-level systems>, although here the <macrostate> records occupancy rather than <energy>. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/8-333-statistical-mechanics-i-statistical-mechanics-of-particles-fall-2013/0cb99318c2ed889316cdcff839759e8d_MIT8_333F13_Lec12.pdf[Mehran Kardar, MIT 8.333 (2013), lecture 12, §IV.C, equations IV.17–18: binomial microstate counts]{ref}

The largest <binomial coefficient> occurs at $k=10$, the balanced <macrostate>. All-left means $k=20$ and supplies just one string. Thus
$$
\begin{aligned}
\Omega(10)&=184\,756,& S(10)/k_B&=\ln184\,756\approx12.126791,\\
\Omega(20)&=1,& S(20)/k_B&=0,\\
P(10)&\approx0.176197,& P(20)&=2^{-20}\approx9.53674\times10^{-7}.
\end{aligned}
$$
There are $184\,756$ balanced strings for every all-left string, even though each individual string has the same <probability>. Exact balance has about $17.6\%$ of the total <probability>; equilibrium reasoning concerns the many <macrostates> near balance too, rather than certainty of exactly ten particles on each side. The diagram compares the counts with their <natural logarithms>.

\Image[boltzmann-entropy-binomial-macrostates.png]
{title=Counts and Boltzmann entropies of the occupancy macrostates of twenty labeled particles}

Four fair, <independent> coins give a smaller worked example with the same <binomial distribution>. If $k$ is the number of heads, the five <macrostates> $k=0,1,2,3,4$ contain respectively $1,4,6,4,1$ <microstates>. Their <probabilities> are respectively $1/16,4/16,6/16,4/16,1/16$. In particular, two heads is one <macrostate> containing six ordered coin sequences, with <Boltzmann entropy> $k_B\ln6$ in this counting analogy. A prescribed ordered sequence has one realization. The next diagram enumerates the same sixteen strings as left/right assignments.

= Boltzmann entropy when a restriction is removed
{c}
{parent=Boltzmann's entropy formula}
{title2=$\Delta S=k_B\ln(\Omega_f/\Omega_i)$}

In <Boltzmann entropy of a two-box occupancy>, impose an initial restriction that all labels are left. There is one allowed coarse <microstate>. Remove that restriction, allowing all occupancy values: the allowed set is now the union of every occupancy <macrostate>, containing $2^N$ strings. If the final description assigns equal <probability> to this whole set, its <Boltzmann entropy> change is
$$
\Omega_i=1,\qquad \Omega_f=\sum_{k=0}^N\binom Nk=2^N,\qquad
\Delta S=k_B\ln\frac{\Omega_f}{\Omega_i}=Nk_B\ln2.
$$
The counting identity is the <binomial theorem>. For twenty labels, $\Delta S=20k_B\ln2\approx13.862944k_B$. This final description fixes neither box occupancy; it is coarser than the particular <macrostate> $k=10$, whose <Boltzmann entropy> is $k_B\ln184\,756$. The two counts answer different questions, so substituting the balanced <macrostate> for the full allowed set would change the calculation.

For four labels, the initial all-left string is `0000`; removing the restriction makes all sixteen strings available. The $k=2$ column has only six strings. Accordingly, the whole allowed set has <Boltzmann entropy> $k_B\ln16=4k_B\ln2$, while the balanced <macrostate> has <Boltzmann entropy> $k_B\ln6$.

\Image[entropy-two-box-configuration-count.png]
{title=Sixteen configurations of four labeled particles grouped by left occupancy, compared with the all-left restriction}

Removing a barrier changes the allowed set immediately, but it does not instantaneously randomize the actual <probability distribution>. Motion and equilibration must make those configurations accessible. The counting result illustrates the configurational contribution to <free expansion>; it does not describe the relaxation time or every contribution to the <entropy> of a real <gas>.

= Additivity of Boltzmann entropy
{parent=Boltzmann's entropy formula}
{title2=$\Omega_{AB}=\Omega_A\Omega_B,\quad S_{AB}=S_A+S_B$}

For two subsystems with independently specified <macrostates>, suppose every allowed <microstate> of $A$ can be paired with every allowed <microstate> of $B$. Their allowed set is a <Cartesian product>. With equal <probability> for each pair, <Boltzmann's entropy formula> gives
$$
\Omega_{AB}=\Omega_A\Omega_B,\qquad
S_{AB}=k_B\ln(\Omega_A\Omega_B)=S_A+S_B.
$$
For example, take two <independent> copies of the toy model, each with two labels choosing between its two boxes. Each copy has four configurations and <Boltzmann entropy> $k_B\ln4$. Combining their <independent> labels gives sixteen configurations and <Boltzmann entropy> $k_B\ln16=2k_B\ln4$. This explains why <entropy> adds even though microscopic counts multiply. https://web.mit.edu/16.unified/www/FALL/thermodynamics/notes/node57.html[MIT, §7.4, equation 7.17: independent state counts multiply]{ref}

The product assumption matters. A constraint shared between the subsystems can forbid some pairs; for example, fixing only their combined <energy> generally requires summing over its possible divisions. See <entropy maximization under thermal contact> for how <thermal equilibrium> selects the dominant division. Statistical <independence> cannot be inferred merely from drawing a boundary between two parts.

= Scope of Boltzmann's entropy formula
{parent=Boltzmann's entropy formula}

The strings in <Boltzmann entropy of a two-box occupancy> are complete <microstates> only within the binary toy model. They retain a <coarse-grained variable> for each particle and omit its exact <position> within a box and its <momentum>. An actual classical <gas> instead occupies continuous <phase space>; obtaining a dimensionless count from a <phase space> volume requires a specified cell convention and the appropriate treatment of particle identity. A <quantum state> count must respect the allowed <quantum states> and <quantum statistics>. Thus $2^N$ is not a general count of <microstates> for a <gas> obeying <quantum ideal-gas statistics>. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/8-333-statistical-mechanics-i-statistical-mechanics-of-particles-fall-2013/0cdb670a825c6aaea7e229ac40776cbf_MIT8_333F13_Lec13.pdf[Kardar, MIT 8.333 (2013), lecture 13, §§IV.D–E: gas phase space and particle counting]{ref}

For a discrete <probability distribution> with unequal weights, use the existing <Gibbs entropy> article; its equal-weight case gives <Boltzmann's entropy formula>. <Shannon entropy> uses the corresponding information measure, with the logarithm base setting its units. A count alone cannot replace the specified <probability distribution>, as the <Boltzmann distribution> examples demonstrate. https://web.mit.edu/16.unified/www/FALL/thermodynamics/notes/node56.html[MIT, §7.3: statistical entropy for general microstate probabilities]{ref}

Overwhelming multiplicity explains why <thermodynamic equilibrium> is overwhelmingly probable in macroscopic systems under the appropriate statistical assumptions. It does not make low-<entropy> <thermal fluctuations> logically impossible. Nor does counting alone derive irreversible macroscopic evolution: one also needs dynamics, an initial preparation, and a reason the system samples the allowed <microstates> on the timescale of interest. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/8-333-statistical-mechanics-i-statistical-mechanics-of-particles-fall-2013/0cb99318c2ed889316cdcff839759e8d_MIT8_333F13_Lec12.pdf[Kardar, lecture 12, §§IV.A–B: statistical equilibrium and the limits of counting arguments]{ref}

Historically, Boltzmann's 1877 paper counted microscopic arrangements, called complexions, and related the <natural logarithm> of their number to <entropy>. The Sharp and Matschinsky translation appeared in \i[Entropy] 17 (2015), pages 1971–2009, DOI 10.3390/e17041971. Its notation differs from ours: Boltzmann's permutability measure $\Omega$ is already logarithmic, whereas here $\Omega$ is the count. https://gilles.montambaux.com/files/histoire-physique/Boltzmann-1877-entropy.pdf[Boltzmann (1877), translated by Kim Sharp and Franz Matschinsky (2015), §III after equation 34 and §V]{ref} <Josiah Willard Gibbs>'s \i[Elementary Principles in Statistical Mechanics] (1902) developed the <statistical ensemble> framework; Chapter XIV compares several statistical definitions of <entropy> with <thermodynamics>. https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Elementary_Principles_in_Statistical_Mechanics[Gibbs (1902), title page and preface]{ref} https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Elementary_Principles_in_Statistical_Mechanics/Chapter_XIV[Gibbs (1902), Chapter XIV: discussion of thermodynamic analogies]{ref}

= Entropy density
{title2=$s$}
{parent=Entropy}
{wiki}

For a homogeneous equilibrium system with <energy density> $\rho$, <pressure> $P$, <chemical potential> $\mu$, and <number density> $n$, the entropy density is
$$
s=\frac{\rho+P-\mu n}{T}.
$$

= Comoving entropy
{parent=Entropy density}
{title2=$S_c=a^3s$}

Comoving entropy is the <entropy density> multiplied by the physical volume factor $a^3$ for a fixed comoving region in an expanding homogeneous universe. In adiabatic expansion it is conserved. Separate decoupled radiation sectors conserve their own comoving entropy, so disappearance of massive species can heat the still-equilibrated sector relative to a decoupled species.

= Entropy density at zero chemical potential
{parent=Entropy density}
{title2=$s=(\rho+P)/T$}

For an extensive equilibrium system, the <thermodynamic Euler relation> gives $E=TS-PV+\mu N$. At zero <chemical potential>, divide by $V$ to obtain $s=(\rho+P)/T$. Equivalently, $dP=(\rho+P)dT/T$ makes
$$
d\left(\frac{(\rho+P)V}{T}\right)=\frac{d(\rho V)+P\,dV}{T}.
$$
The <first law of thermodynamics> identifies this as $dS$; the <thermodynamic Euler relation> fixes the entropy normalization. For adiabatic expansion satisfying the <cosmological perfect-fluid continuity equation>, the numerator on the right vanishes, proving <cosmological entropy conservation>.

= Nonrelativistic entropy at zero chemical potential
{parent=Entropy density at zero chemical potential}
{title2=$s\sim g m^3(2\pi)^{-3/2}x^{-1/2}e^{-x}$}

For a dilute nonrelativistic gas with $x=m/T\gg1$ and zero <chemical potential>, the <Bose-Einstein distribution> approaches the <Maxwell-Boltzmann distribution>. Expanding $E=m+p^2/(2m)+\cdots$ in the <number density> integral gives $n\sim g(mT/(2\pi))^{3/2}e^{-x}$. Since $\rho=mn+3nT/2+\cdots$ and $P=nT$, the <entropy density at zero chemical potential> gives $s=n(x+5/2+\cdots)$ and hence the displayed leading expression. This includes the internal multiplicity $g$; a single real <scalar field> has $g=1$.

= Second law of thermodynamics
{c}
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}

For an <isolated system>, the second law requires $\Delta S\ge0$. More generally, include all affected surroundings to obtain
$$
\Delta S_{\rm tot}=\Delta S_{\rm system}+\Delta S_{\rm surroundings}\ge0.
$$
Equality describes the reversible limit; irreversible macroscopic processes produce <entropy>. A <closed system> can lose <entropy> by exporting <heat>, so nondecrease does not apply to that system alone. The <Clausius statement of the second law> forbids a cyclic device whose sole effect is transferring <heat> from cold to hot. The <Kelvin-Planck statement of the second law> forbids a cyclic device whose sole effect is converting <heat> from one <thermal reservoir> completely into <work>. "Sole effect" matters: a <refrigerator> moves <heat> from cold to hot by consuming <work>. <Thermodynamics and Propulsion/5.1 Concept and Statements of the Second Law (Why do we need a second law?)>{ref}

For direct transfer of $Q=1000\,\mathrm J$ from a <thermal reservoir> at $600\,\mathrm K$ to one at $300\,\mathrm K$, with no <work> extracted,
$$
\Delta S_{\rm tot}=-\frac{1000}{600}+\frac{1000}{300}
=\frac53\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}>0.
$$
The reverse transfer alone would have negative total <entropy> change. Both directions conserve <energy>, but only the hot-to-cold direction is spontaneous. The finite-body example under <entropy> replaces these constant <temperatures> by changing ones.

<Friction>, <free expansion> and <heat transfer> across a finite <temperature> difference all introduce irreversibility. Moving arbitrarily slowly is insufficient if <friction> remains. A <reversible thermodynamic process> must permit restoration of both the system and surroundings. This gives engineers a useful ideal benchmark: irreversibility spends an opportunity to obtain <work>, even though <energy> itself is conserved. https://www.ocw.mit.edu/ans7870/16/16.unified/thermoF03/chapter_7.htm[MIT, Irreversibility and lost opportunities for work]{ref}

= Reversible thermodynamic process
{parent=Second law of thermodynamics}
{title2=$dS=\delta Q_{\rm rev}/T$}

A thermodynamically reversible process can be reversed without a net change of the system and surroundings. Its entropy change equals reversible heat transfer divided by temperature. A reversible <adiabatic process> conserves <entropy>; adiabaticity alone does not forbid internal entropy production in an irreversible evolution.

= Clausius statement of the second law
{c}
{parent=Second law of thermodynamics}

No cyclic device can have as its sole effect the transfer of <heat> from a colder <thermal reservoir> to a hotter <thermal reservoir>. The word “sole” includes the surroundings: the device must return to its starting state, and there must be no net supplied <work>, depletion of a stored resource, or accompanying change elsewhere. The statement excludes a self-running <refrigerator>, while allowing an ordinary <refrigerator> or <heat pump> supplied with <work>.<Thermodynamics and Propulsion/5.1 Concept and Statements of the Second Law (Why do we need a second law?)>[§5.1, statement 2 and Figure 5.2: sole-result restriction]{ref}

For a proposed transfer $Q>0$ from a fixed cold <thermal reservoir> at $T_c$ to a fixed hot <thermal reservoir> at $T_h>T_c>0$, with no other effect, the total <entropy> change would be
$$
\Delta S_{\rm tot}=\frac{Q}{T_h}-\frac{Q}{T_c}<0.
$$
The <first law of thermodynamics> permits this <energy> balance; the <second law of thermodynamics> forbids the negative total <entropy> change. For $Q=600\,\mathrm J$, $T_c=300\,\mathrm K$ and $T_h=600\,\mathrm K$, it is $-1\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}$. Direct <heat transfer> in the opposite direction has positive total <entropy> change. A powered <heat pump> has a different budget: it supplies <work> and delivers more <heat> to the hot side than it extracts from the cold side.

A short equivalence argument links this restriction to the <Kelvin-Planck statement of the second law>. Couple a hypothetical device that moves $600\,\mathrm J$ from cold to hot with no other effect to the <heat engine> illustrated above, which absorbs $1000\,\mathrm J$, rejects $600\,\mathrm J$, and delivers $400\,\mathrm J$. The cold-side transfers cancel; the combined cyclic device takes $400\,\mathrm J$ from the hot <thermal reservoir> and delivers exactly $400\,\mathrm J$ of <work>, violating the <Kelvin-Planck statement of the second law>. Matching transfers is an ideal composition argument, not a claim that such a device exists.<8.333 Statistical Mechanics I: Statistical Mechanics of Particles/Statistical Mechanics I: Lecture 2>[§I.D, printed pp. 7–8: equivalence by coupling devices]{ref}

= Kelvin-Planck statement of the second law
{c}
{parent=Second law of thermodynamics}

No cyclic device can have as its sole effect the extraction of <heat> from one <thermal reservoir> and its complete conversion into net <work>. A putative device absorbing $Q_h>0$ and returning to its starting state with $W=Q_h$ and no other transfer would conserve <energy>, yet violate the <second law of thermodynamics>. Rejecting <heat> to colder surroundings is an essential part of the ordinary two-<thermal reservoir> <heat engine> budget.<Thermodynamics and Propulsion/5.1 Concept and Statements of the Second Law (Why do we need a second law?)>[§5.1, statement 1 and Figure 5.1: complete conversion as the sole result]{ref}

The cyclic and sole-effect qualifications matter. A single <isothermal expansion> of an <ideal gas> can absorb <heat> and deliver equal <work> while leaving its <volume (thermodynamics)> changed; restoring the initial state must also be counted. Likewise, a device discharging stored <internal energy> or consuming fuel has another effect. Converting supplied electrical <work> completely to <heat> through <Joule heating> reverses the proposed conversion and is allowed. The statement also allows cyclic devices that exchange <heat> with one <thermal reservoir> while consuming net <work>; it forbids positive net <work> output with that single <heat> source as the only external change.

Conversely, a hypothetical complete-conversion device could take $100\,\mathrm J$ from the hot <thermal reservoir> and supply $100\,\mathrm J$ of <work> to an ideal <refrigerator> that extracts $100\,\mathrm J$ from the cold <thermal reservoir> and delivers $200\,\mathrm J$ to the hot one. At $600\,\mathrm K$ and $300\,\mathrm K$, that <refrigerator> is itself admissible: its reservoir <entropy> changes cancel. The composite would consume no external <work> and move a net $100\,\mathrm J$ from cold to hot, violating the <Clausius statement of the second law>. Together with the construction there, this proves the equivalence of the two impossibility statements.<8.333 Statistical Mechanics I: Statistical Mechanics of Particles/Statistical Mechanics I: Lecture 2>[§I.D, printed p. 8: engine driving a refrigerator]{ref}

For the two fixed positive-<temperature> <thermal reservoir> hypotheses, <Carnot's theorem> gives the sharper restriction $\eta\le1-T_c/T_h<1$. A <reversible thermodynamic process> reaches that bound in the ideal <Carnot cycle>; an ordinary device's smaller <thermal efficiency> reflects its <entropy production> and measurement boundary. An inefficient observed engine is compatible with the <second law of thermodynamics>, rather than evidence that the bound has been experimentally determined.

= Carnot's theorem
{c}
{parent=Second law of thermodynamics}
{title2=$\eta\le1-T_c/T_h$}
{wiki=Carnot's_theorem_(thermodynamics)}

= Carnot theorem
{c}
{synonym}

No cyclic <heat engine> exchanging <heat> only with two fixed <thermal reservoirs> at positive absolute <temperatures> $T_h>T_c$ can exceed the <thermal efficiency> of an engine whose cycle is a <reversible thermodynamic process> between them. All such reversible engines have the same <thermal efficiency>, regardless of their working substance. Let $Q_h>0$ be <heat> absorbed from the hot <thermal reservoir> and $Q_c>0$ the magnitude rejected to the cold one. Then
$$
W=Q_h-Q_c,\qquad
\eta=\frac W{Q_h}\le\eta_C=1-\frac{T_c}{T_h}.
$$
The <work> convention is the one in the <first law of thermodynamics>: $W>0$ is net output by the engine. Use absolute <thermodynamic temperatures>, such as values in <kelvin>; ratios of readings in <degree Celsius> are invalid because their zero is shifted.

For $T_h=600\,\mathrm K$, $T_c=300\,\mathrm K$ and $Q_h=1000\,\mathrm J$, the maximum <thermal efficiency> is $50\%$. A reversible engine delivers $W=500\,\mathrm J$ and rejects $Q_c=500\,\mathrm J$. The <thermal reservoirs> have <entropy> changes $-1000/600$ and $+500/300\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}$, which cancel. The engine's own <entropy> change is zero over its <thermodynamic cycle>.

\Image[carnot-engine-efficiency.png]
{title=Carnot engine energy transfers and the reversible cycle between 600 K and 300 K}

A short derivation now becomes transparent: the <second law of thermodynamics> gives $-Q_h/T_h+Q_c/T_c\ge0$, and the <first law of thermodynamics> gives $W=Q_h-Q_c$. Together they yield the bound and, more precisely,
$$
W=Q_h\left(1-\frac{T_c}{T_h}\right)-T_c\Delta S_{\rm tot}.
$$
At fixed $Q_h,T_h,T_c$, every extra unit of total <entropy production> reduces output by $T_c$ units of <energy>. https://web.mit.edu/16.unified/www/FALL/thermodynamics/notes/node44.html[MIT, Maximum heat-engine work]{ref}

Fixed <thermal reservoirs> are essential. Finite bodies cool and warm, so their initial <temperature> ratio cannot be used throughout an entire extraction process. As a computed contrast, the bodies with equal constant <heat capacity> in the <entropy> example would reach $T_f=\sqrt{600\times300}=424.26\,\mathrm K$ if a reversible engine extracted <work> until their <temperatures> matched. Zero total <entropy> change gives this geometric mean; the <first law of thermodynamics> then gives $W_{\max}=C(600+300-2T_f)=5147.19\,\mathrm J$. Direct contact reaches $450\,\mathrm K$ and extracts no <work>.

Carnot's 1824 book asked whether the useful output of machines driven by <heat> was fundamentally limited and whether changing the working substance could improve that limit. Its reversible comparisons are the historical setting of <Carnot's theorem>. Carnot used the then-current caloric picture, treating <heat> as conserved in its passage through an engine; the modern balance $Q_h-Q_c=W$ should not be read back into that text.<Réflexions sur la puissance motrice du feu et sur les machines propres à développer cette puissance by Sadi Carnot>[pp. 6–7 and 20–23]{ref}

= Thermodynamic temperature
{parent=Carnot's theorem}
{wiki}

Absolute thermodynamic temperature can be defined so that a reversible engine exchanging heats $Q_h$ and $Q_c$ with reservoirs at $T_h$ and $T_c$ satisfies
$$
\frac{Q_c}{Q_h}=\frac{T_c}{T_h},
\qquad
\eta=1-\frac{T_c}{T_h}.
$$
A choice of one reference temperature fixes the scale.

= Carnot cycle
{c}
{parent=Carnot's theorem}
{title2=$W=(T_h-T_c)\Delta S$}
{wiki}

A Carnot cycle realizes the reversible bound in <Carnot's theorem>. Its four steps for an <ideal gas> working substance are:

* Reversible <isothermal expansion> at $T_h$, absorbing $Q_h$ and increasing <entropy> by $\Delta S$.
* Reversible <adiabatic expansion>, doing <work> while the <temperature> falls from $T_h$ to $T_c$ and <entropy> stays fixed.
* Reversible <isothermal compression> at $T_c$, rejecting $Q_c$ and decreasing <entropy> by $\Delta S$.
* Reversible <adiabatic compression>, requiring <work> to restore $T_h$ at fixed <entropy>.

The <temperature> versus <entropy> plot in the <Carnot's theorem> figure is therefore a rectangle traversed clockwise. Because $\delta Q_{\rm rev}=T\,dS$, its width is $\Delta S=Q_h/T_h$. For the illustrated engine, $\Delta S=5/3\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}$: the upper step absorbs $600\times5/3=1000\,\mathrm J$, the lower step rejects $300\times5/3=500\,\mathrm J$, and the enclosed area is the $500\,\mathrm J$ net <work>. Geometry displays the <thermal efficiency> without solving the gas motion. The two vertical steps are <isentropic processes>, not constant-<temperature> steps. https://web.mit.edu/16.unified/www/FALL/thermodynamics/notes/node24.html[MIT, Four steps of a Carnot cycle]{ref} https://web.mit.edu/16.unified/www/FALL/thermodynamics/notes/node46.html[MIT, Temperature-entropy diagrams]{ref}

Reversible <heat transfer> is a limit of vanishing <temperature> differences; ordinary finite-rate transfer introduces <entropy production>. The <Carnot cycle> is a performance benchmark rather than a guarantee of useful <power>. Running it backward gives the ideal operation of a <refrigerator> or <heat pump>, with <work> supplied to move <heat> toward the hotter <thermal reservoir>. https://web.mit.edu/16.unified/www/FALL/thermodynamics/notes/node25.html[MIT, Reversed cycles and refrigeration]{ref}

= Isothermal process
{parent=Carnot cycle}
{wiki}

An <isothermal process> changes a <thermodynamic system> while keeping its <temperature> constant. It need not keep its <pressure>, <volume (thermodynamics)> or <internal energy> constant, and it does not generally have zero <heat> transfer. An <adiabatic process> specifies zero <heat> transfer instead. For example, a slowly expanding <ideal gas> can absorb <heat> from a <heat bath> and deliver the same amount of <work> while its <temperature> stays fixed.

For a fixed <amount of substance> and composition, <internal energy of an ideal gas> depends only on <temperature>. With $Q>0$ for <heat> entering and $W_{\rm by}>0$ for <work> delivered to the surroundings, the <first law of thermodynamics> therefore gives
$$
\Delta U=Q-W_{\rm by}=0,\qquad Q=W_{\rm by}.
$$
This equality does not determine either transfer until the path is specified. <Isothermal ideal-gas work> gives the reversible value; an insulated <free expansion> has a different value even when its equilibrium endpoints have the same <temperature>. The <closed system> convention here excludes changes in bulk <kinetic energy> and external <potential energy>.

A conducting cylinder in a large, stirred <heat bath> approximates an <isothermal process> when the piston moves slowly enough for <thermal conduction> to keep up. A <quasistatic process> keeps the <gas> close to internal <thermodynamic equilibrium>; a <reversible thermodynamic process> additionally requires negligible <friction> and vanishing driving differences in <pressure> and <temperature>. Exact equality of the <gas> and <heat bath> <temperatures> gives no finite <heat transfer> rate through a finite <thermal resistance>. The reversible description is a limit of arbitrarily small differences and arbitrarily slow motion, illustrated by the <finite-rate isothermal piston model>.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/3.4 Thermodynamic Processes>[§3.4, “Isothermal Processes”, Figure 3.9 and the discussion of removing piston weights in infinitesimal steps]{ref}

For a <real gas>, <internal energy at constant temperature> can change with <volume (thermodynamics)> because molecular interaction <potential energy> changes. In one smooth <thermodynamic phase> of a fixed-composition <simple compressible system>,
$$
\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial V}\right)_{T,n}
=T\left(\frac{\partial p}{\partial T}\right)_{V,n}-p.
$$
Thus an <isothermal process> generally has $Q=\Delta U+W_{\rm by}$, with no reason to set $\Delta U=0$. The right-hand side vanishes for the <ideal gas law>, but not generally for another <equation of state>.<Thermodynamics and Statistical Mechanics by Richard Fitzpatrick/Free Expansion of Gas>[Equations 6.151–6.154: the volume contribution to internal-energy changes]{ref}

= Isothermal ideal-gas work
{parent=Isothermal process}
{title2=$W_{\rm by,rev}=nRT\ln(V_f/V_i)$}

For a <reversible thermodynamic process> that changes the <volume (thermodynamics)> of a fixed amount of <ideal gas> at absolute <temperature> $T$, the <work> done by the <gas> is
$$
W_{\rm by,rev}=\int_{V_i}^{V_f}p\,dV
=nRT\int_{V_i}^{V_f}\frac{dV}{V}
=nRT\ln\frac{V_f}{V_i}
=nRT\ln\frac{p_i}{p_f}.
$$
Here $n$ is the <amount of substance>, $R$ the <molar gas constant>, $p$ the <pressure>, and $\ln$ the <natural logarithm>. The <ideal gas law> supplies $p=nRT/V$; the <integral> is the signed <area> under the <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> curve. <Isothermal expansion> gives positive <work>; <isothermal compression> gives negative <work>. A convention counting <work> done on the <gas> instead has $W_{\rm on}=-W_{\rm by}$.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/3.2 Work, Heat, and Internal Energy>[§3.2, equations 3.3–3.4, Figure 3.5 and the isothermal integral following Figure 3.6]{ref}

For a calculated example, let $n=1\,\mathrm{mol}$, $T=300\,\mathrm K$ and $V_i=10\,\mathrm L$, $V_f=20\,\mathrm L$. The <pressure> falls from approximately $249.4$ to $124.7\,\mathrm{kPa}$, and
$$
W_{\rm by,rev}=Q=nRT\ln2\approx1.729\,\mathrm{kJ},\qquad \Delta U=0.
$$
Traversing the same <isothermal process> backward changes both transfer signs: $W_{\rm by,rev}=Q\approx-1.729\,\mathrm{kJ}$. Neither direction changes <internal energy of an ideal gas>. These stipulated values illustrate the <energy> balance; they are not experimental measurements.

\Image[isothermal-ideal-gas-signed-work-area.png]
{title=Signed work along an ideal-gas isotherm: expansion and compression}

The shaded geometric <area> is positive in both panels; the direction of the <integral> supplies the sign of <work>. Knowing only the two equilibrium endpoints does not justify shading this curve for an irreversible process. For an idealized piston moving against a specified external resisting <pressure>, boundary <work> is $W_{\rm by}=\int p_{\rm ext}\,dV$. Substitution of the equilibrium <gas> <pressure> requires the appropriate reversible limit. In <free expansion> into <vacuum>, $p_{\rm ext}=0$ and this boundary <work> is zero.

= Finite-rate isothermal piston model
{parent=Isothermal process}

Why must a conducting piston move slowly? A simple model couples the <first law of thermodynamics> to <Newton's law of cooling>. Assume a fixed amount of <ideal gas> with uniform <temperature> $T(t)$ and constant whole-sample <heat capacity at constant volume> $C_V$, a <heat bath> maintained at $T_b$, negligible <friction>, and mechanically slow motion. Let $G>0$ be the effective thermal conductance, the reciprocal of the <thermal resistance>, in $\mathrm{W\,K^{-1}}$. Then
$$
\dot Q=G(T_b-T),\qquad p=\frac{nRT}{V},\qquad
C_V\dot T=G(T_b-T)-p\dot V.
$$
Positive <heat transfer> must offset expansion <work> to keep $T$ near $T_b$. A finite $G$ and nonzero piston speed therefore require a <temperature> difference. This uniform-<temperature> model neglects spatial <temperature gradients>, sensor response, cylinder <heat capacity> and any change of $G$ with piston position; it is an illustrative calculation, not a fit to the <isothermal gas syringe experiment>.

\Image[isothermal-piston-thermal-bath-temperature-lag.png]
{title=Conducting piston in a thermal bath and modeled temperature lag during expansion}

For a prescribed exponential <volume (thermodynamics)> change $V=V_i e^{\lambda t}$, define $\tau=C_V/G$, $s=t/\tau$ and $\epsilon=nR\lambda/G$. The dimensionless <temperature> $\theta=T/T_b$ obeys a <linear ordinary differential equation> with initial value $\theta(0)=1$:
$$
\frac{d\theta}{ds}=1-(1+\epsilon)\theta,\qquad
\theta(s)=\frac{1}{1+\epsilon}
+\frac{\epsilon}{1+\epsilon}e^{-(1+\epsilon)s}.
$$
The plotted expansion uses stipulated $C_V=(5/2)nR$ and stops at $V_f=2V_i$, at $s_f=\ln2/[(5/2)\epsilon]$. During expansion, the larger $\epsilon$ produces the larger <temperature> deficit; once the piston is held fixed, the deficit undergoes <exponential decay> as $e^{-(s-s_f)}$. Reducing $|\epsilon|$ makes the path more nearly an <isothermal process>, while increasing its duration. Reversing the piston with $\lambda<0$ produces a <temperature> excess and outward <heat transfer>; the small-$|\epsilon|$ regime is the intended near-isothermal approximation. Mechanical slowness alone does not ensure sufficient <heat transfer>.

= Boyle's law
{c}
{parent=Isothermal process}
{title2=$pV=\mathrm{constant}$}
{wiki}

= Boyle law
{c}
{synonym}

For a fixed amount of an ideal gas at constant temperature, pressure times volume is constant. Equivalently its density is proportional to pressure. In a dilute bubbly flow, this allows bubble volume and its contribution to <buoyancy> to increase as hydrostatic pressure decreases, provided gas mass is retained and dissolution is negligible.

= Isothermal compression
{parent=Isothermal process}

An <isothermal compression> reduces the <volume (thermodynamics)> of a <thermodynamic system> at fixed <temperature>. For a fixed-composition <ideal gas> compressed reversibly from $V_i$ to $V_f<V_i$, <isothermal ideal-gas work> gives
$$
W_{\rm by,rev}=nRT\ln(V_f/V_i)<0,\qquad
Q=W_{\rm by,rev}<0,\qquad \Delta U=0.
$$
The surroundings supply positive <work> $W_{\rm on}$, and the <gas> rejects the same amount of <heat> to the <heat bath>. Compressing $1\,\mathrm{mol}$ at $300\,\mathrm K$ from $20$ to $10\,\mathrm L$ requires approximately $1.729\,\mathrm{kJ}$ of <work> input and rejects $1.729\,\mathrm{kJ}$ of <heat>. If that <heat transfer> cannot keep up, the <temperature> rises: the process departs from <isothermal compression> toward <adiabatic compression>. The <finite-rate isothermal piston model> explains the required small <temperature> excess.

= Isothermal gas syringe experiment
{parent=Isothermal compression}

A <gas syringe> makes the distinction between a constant-<temperature> path and equal-<temperature> endpoints observable. The <Ideal Gas Law Apparatus (TD-8596A), Instruction Manual 012-08420B by PASCO scientific> specifies a TD-8596A <gas syringe> with a mechanical stop, an internal thermistor, an attached <pressure> sensor and data acquisition. Record the stop <volume (thermodynamics)> with the coupling disconnected, then set $40\,\mathrm{mL}$ and seal the coupling. Record <pressure> and <temperature>, compress quickly to the stop near $20\,\mathrm{mL}$, and hold until both readouts settle. The manual gives roughly $30\,\mathrm s$ for settling and about $0.5\,\mathrm s$ for the thermistor response. Releasing the plunger gives a second transient and a final <volume (thermodynamics)> to record.<Ideal Gas Law Apparatus (TD-8596A), Instruction Manual 012-08420B by PASCO scientific>[pp. 2–4: Introduction, Equipment Setup and Experiment 1 procedure]{ref}

The manual describes a <pressure> overshoot and <temperature> rise during rapid compression, followed by cooling at the held <volume (thermodynamics)>; the settled <pressure> remains higher than initially. This illustrates approximately <adiabatic compression> followed by <heat> rejection, rather than an <isothermal compression> throughout. Use the settled initial and compressed readings to test <Boyle's law>, correcting both syringe readings by the unmarked tubing <volume (thermodynamics)> $V_d$:
$$
p_i(V_{\rm read,i}+V_d)=p_f(V_{\rm read,f}+V_d).
$$
Use <absolute pressure>, not <gauge pressure>. A transient comparison of $pV/T$ must pair simultaneous <pressure> and <temperature> readings: the sensor lag can separate their peaks.<Ideal Gas Law Apparatus (TD-8596A), Instruction Manual 012-08420B by PASCO scientific>[pp. 4–5: Constant Temperature and Varying Temperature analyses; p. 8: answers 1–3]{ref}

For an additional test of the path, repeat with small compression steps, wait for stable <temperature> and <pressure> at every step, and repeat in reverse. Keep the sealed <amount of substance>, connecting tubing and ambient <temperature> fixed; prevent hand warming of the barrel and check for leaks by watching for continued <pressure> drift at held <volume (thermodynamics)> after <thermal equilibrium>. These are proposed controls, rather than reported measurements. Agreement of equilibrated points with <Boyle's law> supports the fixed-<temperature> <ideal gas law> approximation, but does not prove that the rapid segments between them were <isothermal processes>. A single local <temperature> sensor can miss <temperature gradients>; plunger <friction> can produce direction-dependent readings. The <pressure> and <volume (thermodynamics)> data estimate reversible <work> only insofar as the path approaches a <reversible thermodynamic process>; they do not directly measure <heat>. After motion stops, cooling at fixed <volume (thermodynamics)> has zero boundary <work> and $Q=\Delta U<0$, even though the complete equal-endpoint-<temperature> <ideal gas> run has $\Delta U=0$.

= Gas syringe
{parent=Isothermal gas syringe experiment}

A <gas syringe> traps a <gas> in a graduated barrel with a movable plunger, allowing its <volume (thermodynamics)> to be varied and read. In an <isothermal gas syringe experiment>, the relevant total <volume (thermodynamics)> also includes any connected tubing and sensor cavity containing the same <gas>.

= Isothermal expansion
{parent=Isothermal process}

An <isothermal expansion> increases the <volume (thermodynamics)> of a <thermodynamic system> at fixed <temperature>. For a reversible <ideal gas> path, the <gas> delivers positive <work> and absorbs positive <heat>, with $Q=W_{\rm by,rev}=nRT\ln(V_f/V_i)$ and $\Delta U=0$. The <heat bath> supplies the expansion <energy>; without sufficient <heat transfer>, doing <work> instead lowers the <temperature>. This transfer is the hot step of a <Carnot cycle>, and it is why an expanding <gas> need not cool when its surroundings continuously replenish its <internal energy>.

An insulated <free expansion> into an evacuated compartment is different. In the idealization with no <heat> exchange, no external <work> and no container <energy> change, $Q=W_{\rm by}=0$. Since <internal energy of an ideal gas> depends only on <temperature>, its final equilibrium <temperature> equals its initial value. The transient need not have a uniform <temperature> or <pressure>, so equal endpoint <temperatures> do not make it a reversible isotherm. Do not apply the logarithmic <isothermal ideal-gas work> formula to this irreversible process. Its <entropy> still increases by $\Delta S=nR\ln(V_f/V_i)$; <entropy change in free expansion> evaluates this <state function> difference using a hypothetical reversible path.<Thermodynamics and Statistical Mechanics by Richard Fitzpatrick/Free Expansion of Gas>[Equations 6.144–6.148 and the negligible-container-heat-capacity assumption]{ref}

For a <real gas>, even equal-<temperature> equilibrium endpoints need not have equal <internal energy>. An insulated <free expansion> can therefore change the <temperature>, while a controlled <isothermal expansion> must provide the <heat> needed for both its <internal energy> change and its delivered <work>. The <internal energy at constant temperature> article gives the <Van der Waals equation> example.<Thermodynamics and Statistical Mechanics by Richard Fitzpatrick/Van der Waals Gas>[Equations 6.163–6.166: attractive interactions and free-expansion cooling]{ref}

= Heat
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{title2=$Q$}
{wiki}

Heat is <energy> transferred across a <thermodynamic system>'s boundary because of a <temperature> difference. It is measured in <joules>, like <work> and <internal energy>. A hot cup transfers <heat> to cooler surroundings; its <temperature> describes its state, while the transferred <heat> describes a process. The cup stores <internal energy>, rather than a substance called <heat>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/1-4-heat-transfer-specific-heat-and-calorimetry[Ling, Moebs and Sanny, OpenStax, University Physics Volume 2, section 1.4: heat and internal energy]{ref}

For a <closed system> with unchanged bulk <kinetic energy> and <potential energy>, choose $Q>0$ for <heat> entering and $W>0$ for <work> done by the system. The <first law of thermodynamics> gives
$$
\Delta U=Q-W,\qquad dU=\delta Q-\delta W.
$$
Here $U$ is the stored <internal energy>. For example, a sealed piston cylinder receiving $20\,\mathrm{kJ}$ of <heat> and delivering $5\,\mathrm{kJ}$ of <work> gains $15\,\mathrm{kJ}$ of <internal energy>. A <closed system> retains its matter while exchanging <energy>; it need not be an <isolated system>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/3-3-first-law-of-thermodynamics[Ling, Moebs and Sanny, OpenStax, First law of thermodynamics and sign conventions]{ref}

\Image[heat-work-internal-energy-balance.png]
{title=<Heat> input and <work> output determine the <internal energy> increase}
{description=The dashed boundary encloses a <closed system> in a sealed piston cylinder. The incoming arrow carries $Q=20\,\mathrm{kJ}$ of <heat>; the outgoing arrow carries $W=5\,\mathrm{kJ}$ of <work> done by the system. With unchanged bulk <kinetic energy> and <potential energy>, $\Delta U=15\,\mathrm{kJ}$. No matter crosses the boundary.}

Unlike <internal energy>, <heat> is not a <state function>: different paths between the same equilibrium states can transfer different $Q$, with compensating differences in <work>. The symbol $\delta Q$ records this dependence on the path; there is no stored quantity whose difference is always $Q$. The two paths in <first law of thermodynamics> illustrate this directly. Supplied <heat> can raise <temperature> as <sensible heat>, change a <phase fraction> through <latent heat>, or help supply expansion <work>. Measuring <temperature> alone therefore does not determine transferred <heat>.

For a <reversible thermodynamic process>, $\delta Q_{\rm rev}=T\,dS$, where $T$ is absolute <temperature> and $S$ is <entropy>. This identification requires reversibility: an irreversible <adiabatic process> has $Q=0$ and can nevertheless increase <entropy>. Likewise $Q=\Delta H$, with <enthalpy> $H$, requires the particular fixed-<pressure> conditions in <enthalpy>. These distinctions make <heat> useful as a precise boundary-transfer quantity in <heat engines>, <refrigerators> and heating calculations.

Historically, James Prescott Joule's paddle experiments showed that stirring <water> with input <work> could raise its <temperature>, helping establish <conservation of energy>. The lesson is still useful: a <temperature> rise can result from <work> even without incoming <heat>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/1-4-heat-transfer-specific-heat-and-calorimetry[Ling, Moebs and Sanny, OpenStax, section 1.4: Joule's paddle experiment]{ref}

= Sensible heat
{parent=Heat}
{title2=$Q_{\rm sens}$}
{wiki}

Sensible heat is transferred <heat> associated with a <temperature> change within a single <thermodynamic phase>. By contrast, <latent heat> can change the <phase fraction> while the <temperature> stays fixed during equilibrium <phase coexistence>. Neither name denotes an additional stored substance: both describe how transferred <heat> participates in a process.

For fixed <mass> $m$ and composition, use the <heat capacity> appropriate to the heating constraint. Under the <quasistatic process> assumptions in <heat capacity>, with only <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> <work> and unchanged bulk <kinetic energy> and <potential energy>, fixed-<pressure> heating gives
$$
Q_{\rm sens}=m\int_{T_1}^{T_2}c_p(T)\,dT,
\qquad Q_{\rm sens}=mc_p(T_2-T_1)\quad\text{if }c_p\text{ is constant}.
$$
Here $c_p$ is <specific heat capacity at constant pressure>; fixed-<volume (thermodynamics)> heating instead uses <specific heat capacity at constant volume> $c_V$. A cooling step has negative $Q_{\rm sens}$ with the sign convention in <heat>. A <temperature> difference has the same numerical value in units of the <kelvin> or <degree Celsius>. The <integral> follows by adding the small transfers $\delta Q=mc_p(T)\,dT$, rather than assuming a constant <heat capacity>.

As a stylized <ice>-to-<water> example for a fixed-composition <closed system> at fixed <pressure> $1\,\mathrm{atm}$, take $m=0.5\,\mathrm{kg}$, an initial <temperature> of $-10\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, <melting> at $0\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, and a final <temperature> of $20\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$. Assume constant <specific heat capacity at constant pressure> values $c_{\rm ice}=2.1\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$ and $c_{\rm water}=4.18\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$, and <latent heat of fusion> $L_f=334\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}}$. These are stipulated, rounded model values; ignore the container and <heat> losses, retain all the material, and assume only <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> <work> under the <quasistatic process> conditions above. The three steps are
$$
\begin{aligned}
Q_{\rm ice}&=0.5\times2.1\times10=10.5\,\mathrm{kJ},\\
Q_{\rm melt}&=0.5\times334=167\,\mathrm{kJ},\\
Q_{\rm water}&=0.5\times4.18\times20=41.8\,\mathrm{kJ},\\
Q_{\rm total}&=10.5+167+41.8=219.3\,\mathrm{kJ}.
\end{aligned}
$$
Only the first and third terms are <sensible heat>; the middle term is <latent heat>. The plateau begins after $10.5\,\mathrm{kJ}$ and ends after $177.5\,\mathrm{kJ}$ of total supplied <heat>. At $94\,\mathrm{kJ}$ total input, half the <mass> is <liquid> <water> and the <temperature> remains $0\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$. The equilibrium <phase transition> <temperature> depends on <pressure>; this example stipulates a single fixed <pressure>. <University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/1.5 Phase Changes>{ref}

\Image[sensible-and-latent-heating-curve.png]
{title=Heating $0.5\,\mathrm{kg}$ of <ice> to <water>: <sensible heat> and <latent heat>}
{description=Illustrative fixed-<pressure> <heating curve> using the constant parameters above. The sloping portions raise <temperature>; the long $0\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$ plateau changes the <phase fraction>. Total supplied <heat> is measured from the initial <ice> state and equals the <enthalpy> change under the stated assumptions.}

This split makes heating budgets easy to interpret: <melting> consumes $167/219.3\approx76.2\%$ of the supplied <heat> in this model despite causing no <temperature> rise. Within either single <thermodynamic phase>, the <heating curve> slope is $dT/dQ=1/(mc_p)$; a larger <heat capacity> makes the slope shallower. A flat interval represents changing <phase fraction>, so it cannot be modeled by applying $Q=mc_p\Delta T$ across the <phase transition>. The complementary constructed example in <latent heat> tracks that <phase fraction> explicitly.

= Heat transfer
{parent=Heat}
{wiki}

<Heat transfer> asks how quickly <heat> crosses a boundary and how the <temperature> varies along its path. Its principal mechanisms are <thermal conduction>, <convection> and <thermal radiation>. A warm wall can conduct <heat> through its interior while exchanging <heat> with moving air and emitting <thermal radiation> at its surfaces. Identifying these paths lets us estimate heating requirements and cooling times.

Distinguish the accumulated <heat> $Q$ from its rate $\dot Q=dQ/dt$, a <power> measured in $\mathrm W$. The <heat flux> $q$ is the rate per unit <area>, measured in $\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$: a uniform normal <heat flux> across <area> $A$ gives $\dot Q=Aq$. The steady bar in <thermal conduction> carries $q=400\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$ through $A=0.01\,\mathrm{m^2}$, hence $\dot Q=4\,\mathrm W$; maintained for $60\,\mathrm s$, it transfers $Q=240\,\mathrm J$. This distinction makes a surface's intensity and a whole object's heating load easy to compare.

= Heat exchanger
{parent=Heat transfer}
{wiki}

A <heat exchanger> transfers <heat> between a <fluid> and another <fluid> or its surroundings. In <measuring the Joule-Thomson effect with a porous plug>, an upstream coil restores the inlet <temperature> before the measurement throttle; it belongs outside the approximately insulated measurement <control volume>.

= Nusselt number
{c}
{parent=Heat transfer}
{title2=$\mathrm{Nu}$}
{wiki}

The <Nusselt number> is the ratio of total <heat transfer> to conductive <heat transfer> across the same length scale: $\mathrm{Nu}=qh/(k_T\Delta T)$, where $q$ is heat flux and $k_T$ is <thermal conductivity>.

= Thermal inertia
{parent=Heat transfer}
{title2=$\Gamma=\sqrt{k\rho c_p}$}

For a homogeneous material, thermal inertia is $\Gamma=\sqrt{k\rho c_p}$, where $k$ is <thermal conductivity>, $\rho$ is density and $c_p$ is specific <heat capacity>. It measures the resistance of the surface temperature to periodic heating and controls the phase lag relevant to the <Yarkovsky effect>.

= Radiative cooling
{parent=Heat transfer}
{wiki}

A body loses <internal energy> by emitting <thermal radiation> faster than it absorbs radiation or receives other heating. In a gravitating gas this can remove pressure support and allow further contraction.

= Compton cooling by the cosmic microwave background
{parent=Radiative cooling}
{title2=$t_C\propto(1+z)^{-4}/x_e$}

Hot electrons can transfer energy to <cosmic microwave background> photons through <Compton scattering>. For a fixed electron fraction $x_e$ and electron temperature well above the radiation temperature, the <optically thin gas cooling time> scales approximately as $(1+z)^{-4}/x_e$. Its volume loss scales with electron density, not with $n_H^2$, because the radiation density is independent of local gas density. The process tends toward the radiation temperature; it heats rather than cools electrons colder than that radiation.

= Dust thermal emission
{parent=Radiative cooling}

Dust grains emit thermal radiation after absorbing energy from radiation or collisions. Collisions can transfer thermal energy from dense gas to grains, whose radiation then cools the gas. This channel requires dust and does not operate in strictly pristine hydrogen-helium material without prior enrichment.

= Inverse Compton cooling
{parent=Radiative cooling}
{title2=$P_e=4\sigma_TcU_\gamma k_B(T_e-T_\gamma)/(m_ec^2)$}

Thermal electrons can transfer energy to lower-energy radiation photons through <Compton scattering>. In the nonrelativistic Thomson regime, the net cooling power per electron is $4\sigma_TcU_\gamma k_B(T_e-T_\gamma)/(m_ec^2)$ for a blackbody photon bath. A sufficiently hot electron population therefore loses energy; a colder population is heated instead. The <cosmic microwave background> radiation energy density grows as $(1+z)^4$, making this channel more important for high-redshift diffuse ionized gas.

= Astrophysical cooling function
{parent=Radiative cooling}
{title2=$\mathcal C=n_H^2\Lambda(T)$}

= Hydrogen-density-squared cooling function
{synonym}

An <astrophysical cooling function> packages radiative losses of a plasma into a temperature-dependent coefficient. In the hydrogen-density-squared convention, the volume energy loss is $n_H^2\Lambda(T)$, so the coefficient has units $\mathrm{erg\,cm^3\,s^{-1}}$. Its dependence on <ionization> state, chemical abundances and radiation field must be specified; a low-density collisional-equilibrium curve is not universal.

= Molecular line cooling
{parent=Astrophysical cooling function}

Radiation from rotational and vibrational transitions of molecules removes gas thermal energy. In primordial gas below the atomic threshold, <molecular hydrogen> is a principal coolant; <hydrogen deuteride> can matter in suitable chemical conditions. Molecular formation, photodissociation, optical depth and density-dependent level populations control the rate. This allows some small systems below the atomic <virial temperature> threshold to form stars.

= Cooling suppression by photoionization
{parent=Astrophysical cooling function}

<Photoionization> changes the ion population, often removing bound-state coolants at temperatures where collisional equilibrium would retain them. It can therefore suppress primordial line-cooling peaks. It also supplies <photoionization heating>. The net loss is cooling minus heating and depends on radiation spectrum, intensity, shielding and gas density. A universal density-independent function of temperature alone is not generally sufficient for irradiated gas.

= Optically thin gas cooling time
{parent=Astrophysical cooling function}
{title2=$t_{\rm cool}=3\chi k_BT/(2n_H\Lambda)$}

= Radiative gas cooling time
{synonym}

For a nonrelativistic ideal gas with $n_{\rm tot}$ thermal particles per volume, divide its thermal energy $3n_{\rm tot}k_BT/2$ by the optically thin volume radiation loss. In the $n_H^2$ cooling convention this becomes $3\chi k_BT/(2n_H\Lambda)$, where $\chi=n_{\rm tot}/n_H$. Density scaling and ionization-dependent particle count must be kept consistent.

= Cooling-to-free-fall equality curve
{parent=Optically thin gas cooling time}
{title2=$n_{H,\rm crit}=\frac{32Gm_p}{3\pi Xf_g}\left(\frac{3\chi k_BT}{2\Lambda}\right)^2$}

Equating the optically thin cooling time to the uniform-sphere free-fall time gives a critical gas density proportional to $T^2/\Lambda^2$. With <hydrogen mass fraction> $X$, particle ratio $\chi$ and gravitating gas fraction $f_g$, it is $[32Gm_p/(3\pi Xf_g)][3\chi k_BT/(2\Lambda)]^2$. At fixed temperature, gas above this density cools faster than free fall. A smaller gravitating gas fraction moves the boundary upward because gravity is faster at a fixed gas density.

= Primordial atomic cooling curve
{parent=Astrophysical cooling function}
{title2=$\Lambda_{\rm H,He}(T)$}

The <primordial atomic cooling curve> describes hydrogen-helium gas without heavy elements. Near the <hydrogen> excitation threshold it rises sharply, then shows <hydrogen> and <helium> excitation/<ionization> features. Bound-state losses weaken when both elements become highly ionized. At sufficiently high temperatures <thermal bremsstrahlung> gives a slowly rising tail. Composition, density convention and <ionization> assumptions fix the numerical normalization.

= Atomic line cooling
{parent=Astrophysical cooling function}
{title2=$\mathcal C_{\rm line}=\sum n_en_iq_{ij}(T)\,E_{ij}$}

<Atomic line cooling> removes gas kinetic energy by <collisional excitation> followed by photon escape. It is strongest where atoms or ions have bound <electrons> and thermally accessible transitions. If photons are trapped, or if the relevant atoms are fully ionized, the simple optically thin line-cooling rate no longer gives the same loss.

= Metal-line cooling
{parent=Atomic line cooling}
{title2=$\Lambda(T,Z)$}

Heavy elements provide many ionic transitions at temperatures where <hydrogen> and <helium> have lost their most effective line emitters. <Metal-line cooling> can therefore increase the plasma cooling function substantially and broaden its temperature features. Low-energy fine-structure transitions also permit cooling below the <hydrogen> atomic threshold when the relevant ions and <electrons> are present.

= Heat flux density
{title2=$\mathbf q$}
{parent=Heat}

= Heat flux
{synonym}

<Heat flux density> is a <vector> $\mathbf q$ describing the rate of <heat transfer> per unit <area>, measured in $\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$. Through a planar face of <area> $A$ with uniform normal component $q$, the signed rate is $\dot Q=Aq$ in $\mathrm W$; positive $q$ means <heat> travels in the chosen positive normal direction. <Fourier's law> determines the conductive contribution. In a bar with a constant cross-sectional <area>, steady <thermal conduction> without internal sources or side losses makes both $q$ and $\dot Q$ constant along the bar. Constant $\dot Q$ need not mean constant $q$ when the cross-sectional <area> varies.

= Thermal conduction
{parent=Heat}
{wiki}

= Heat conduction
{synonym}

<Thermal conduction> transfers <heat> through microscopic interactions without bulk transport of matter. To predict the heating load through a bar, combine <Fourier's law> with <conservation of energy> and the imposed end <temperatures>. In the <isotropic> model with positive <thermal conductivity> $k$, the conductive <heat flux> points from high to low <temperature>, opposite to the <temperature gradient>. A spoon warming along its handle and <heat> escaping through a wall are familiar examples.

Consider a bar of <length> $L=0.10\,\mathrm m$, constant cross-sectional <area> $A=0.010\,\mathrm{m^2}$ and constant <thermal conductivity> $k=2\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. Maintain its ends at $T_h=40\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$ and $T_c=20\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, with $x$ increasing toward the colder end. Assume a <steady state>, one-dimensional <thermal conduction>, no internal sources and no <heat transfer> through the sides. The <temperature> falls by $20\,\mathrm K$ over $0.10\,\mathrm m$, giving
$$
\frac{dT}{dx}=-200\,\mathrm{K\,m^{-1}},\qquad
q=-k\frac{dT}{dx}=400\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}},\qquad
\dot Q=Aq=4\,\mathrm W.
$$
The difference between the two readings in <degree Celsius> units is the same $20$-<kelvin> difference. The positive <heat flux> carries <heat> toward increasing $x$.

\Image[fourier-conduction-steady-bar.png]
{title=Steady <thermal conduction> through a bar: declining <temperature>, constant <heat flux>}
{description=An <isotropic> bar with stipulated constant <thermal conductivity> $k=2\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$, <length> $0.10\,\mathrm m$ and cross-sectional <area> $0.010\,\mathrm{m^2}$. Its ends remain at $40$ and $20\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, and its sides exchange no <heat>. The lower panel distinguishes $q=400\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$ from $\dot Q=4\,\mathrm W$.}

The general solution under these assumptions is
$$
T(x)=T_h-\frac{T_h-T_c}{L}x,\qquad
q=\frac{k(T_h-T_c)}{L},\qquad
\dot Q=\frac{kA(T_h-T_c)}{L}.
$$
A proof outline is enough to explain the straight line: <conservation of energy> in any small slice, with no accumulation, sources or side losses, gives $d\dot Q/dx=0$. Constant cross-sectional <area> gives $dq/dx=0$; constant <thermal conductivity> and <Fourier's law> then give $d^2T/dx^2=0$. Two <integrations> and the end <boundary conditions> determine the displayed <affine function>. This is why a visually simple <temperature> profile can encode a continuous flow of <energy>. https://web.mit.edu/16.unified/www/FALL/thermodynamics/notes/node117.html[E. M. Greitzer, Z. S. Spakovszky and I. A. Waitz, MIT Thermodynamics and Propulsion, section 16.3: steady one-dimensional conduction]{ref}

Doubling the <length> halves the rate, while doubling the cross-sectional <area> doubles it, at fixed end <temperatures> and <thermal conductivity>. This explains why a long narrow support can limit unwanted <heat transfer>, while a short broad path can help remove <heat>. Joining materials leads to <thermal resistance> in series. During initial warming, <internal energy> can accumulate and the <temperature> profile need not be a straight line; the time dependence is described by the <heat equation> and <thermal diffusivity>.

Now join two layers, each of <length> $L_1=L_2=0.10\,\mathrm m$, with constant cross-sectional <area> $A=0.010\,\mathrm{m^2}$ and stipulated constant <thermal conductivities> $k_1=1$ and $k_2=4\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. Keep the outer ends at $40$ and $20\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$. Assume a <steady state>, one-dimensional <thermal conduction>, no internal sources or side losses, and perfect contact: the <temperature> is continuous at the interface, with no extra contact drop. For each layer, use the area-specific <thermal resistance> $r_i=L_i/k_i$ and $R_i=r_i/A$. The series calculation gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
r_1+r_2=\frac{L_1}{k_1}+\frac{L_2}{k_2}
=0.100+0.025=0.125\,\mathrm{m^2\,K\,W^{-1}},\\
R_1+R_2=\frac{r_1+r_2}{A}=12.5\,\mathrm{K\,W^{-1}},\\
q=\frac{20\,\mathrm K}{0.125\,\mathrm{m^2\,K\,W^{-1}}}
=160\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}},\qquad \dot Q=Aq=1.6\,\mathrm W,\\
\Delta T_1=qr_1=16\,\mathrm K,\qquad
\Delta T_2=qr_2=4\,\mathrm K,\qquad
T_{\rm interface}=40\,{}^\circ\mathrm C-16\,\mathrm K=24\,{}^\circ\mathrm C.
\end{aligned}
$$

\Image[layered-thermal-conduction.png]
{title=Two layers in series: continuous <heat flux>, different <temperature gradients>}
{description=Each layer is $0.10\,\mathrm m$ long with the same cross-sectional <area> $0.010\,\mathrm{m^2}$ and perfect contact. Stipulated constant <thermal conductivities> are $1$ and $4\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. The <temperature> drops by $16\,\mathrm K$ and $4\,\mathrm K$, reaching $24\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$ at the interface, while $q=160\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$ and $\dot Q=1.6\,\mathrm W$ throughout.}

The <heat flux> is continuous: otherwise <energy> would accumulate at the interface. Yet <Fourier's law> gives different <temperature gradients>, $dT/dx=-q/k_1=-160\,\mathrm{K\,m^{-1}}$ and $dT/dx=-q/k_2=-40\,\mathrm{K\,m^{-1}}$.
