Lower <thermal conductivity> requires a steeper <temperature gradient> to carry the same <heat flux>; equal <lengths> therefore give drops in the ratio $4:1$. A kink in the <temperature> profile records the change of material while perfect contact keeps the <temperature> itself continuous.

= Thermal resistance
{parent=Thermal conduction}
{title2=$R_{\rm th}=\Delta T/\dot Q$}

<Thermal resistance> measures the <temperature> drop required to drive a given rate of <heat transfer>. For the steady one-dimensional bar model in <thermal conduction>,
$$
R_{\rm th}=\frac{T_h-T_c}{\dot Q}=\frac{L}{kA}
\quad[\mathrm{K\,W^{-1}}],\qquad
r=AR_{\rm th}=\frac{L}{k}
\quad[\mathrm{m^2\,K\,W^{-1}}],\qquad
q=\frac{T_h-T_c}{r}.
$$
Here $r$ relates the <temperature> drop to <heat flux>, while $R_{\rm th}$ relates it to the rate of <heat transfer>. The single bar above has $r=0.050\,\mathrm{m^2\,K\,W^{-1}}$ and $R_{\rm th}=5\,\mathrm{K\,W^{-1}}$, so its $20\,\mathrm K$ drop drives $4\,\mathrm W$.

To see why series <thermal resistances> add, apply <Fourier's law> in each layer to obtain $\Delta T_i=\dot Q R_i$. With no accumulation or side losses the rate of <heat transfer> is the same through both, so adding the drops gives $T_h-T_c=\dot Q(R_1+R_2)$. This lets us size layered walls without solving a new <differential equation> for every layer. The structure matches <Ohm's law>: <temperature> difference plays the role of <electric potential difference>, the rate of <heat transfer> plays the role of <electric current>, and <thermal resistance> plays the role of <electrical resistance>. https://web.mit.edu/16.unified/www/FALL/thermodynamics/notes/node118.html[E. M. Greitzer, Z. S. Spakovszky and I. A. Waitz, MIT Thermodynamics and Propulsion, section 16.4: series thermal resistances and the electrical analogy]{ref}

= Temperature dipole around an insulating sphere
{parent=Thermal conduction}
{title2=$T=T_0+(\mathbf H\cdot\mathbf r)(1+a^3/(2r^3))$}

In the absence of heat advection, an insulating <sphere> of radius $a$ in a uniform remote <temperature> gradient $\mathbf H$ has the displayed exterior <harmonic function> as its temperature field. The reflected dipole term makes $\partial_rT=0$ at $r=a$. The surface temperature is $T_0+(3/2)a\mathbf H\cdot\mathbf n$, so the tangential gradient is amplified by $3/2$. If the sphere has <thermal conductivity> ratio $K$ relative to its surroundings, temperature and heat-flux continuity instead give surface gradient factor $3/(2+K)$.

= Isobaric conductive cooling in mass coordinates
{parent=Thermal conduction}
{title2=$T_\tau+\Lambda(T)/T-\lambda_0\partial_m(T^{\alpha-1}T_m)=0$}

With fixed <pressure> $p$, let $A=\mu p/\mathcal R$, so $\rho=A/T$. A material mass coordinate has $\partial_x=\rho\partial_m$ and the material time derivative becomes $\partial_t|_m$. The energy and continuity equations then give the displayed equation with $\tau=Ct$ and $C=(\gamma-1)(\mu/\mathcal R)^2p/\gamma$. A mass integral from a fixed origin is material only when the <mass flux> there vanishes; otherwise subtract its time-integrated flux to label parcels. Uniform <pressure> is a low-Mach approximation when sound crossing is faster than thermal evolution.

= Error-function isobaric cooling front
{parent=Isobaric conductive cooling in mass coordinates}
{title2=$T=T_0\operatorname{erf}\!\left(m/(2\sqrt{\lambda_0\tau})\right)$}

For conductivity $\lambda=\lambda_0T$, no cooling in the hot phase and a zero-<temperature> boundary at $m=0$, the mass-coordinate equation is linear diffusion. Its similarity profile solves $f''+\xi f'/2=0$, $f(0)=0$, $f(\infty)=1$, giving the displayed <error function>. The thermal layer broadens as the square root of time. Conducted energy enters the cold radiating reservoir at rate per area $L=\lambda_0A T_m(0)=A T_0\sqrt{\lambda_0}/\sqrt{\pi\tau}$, hence $L\propto t^{-1/2}$.

= Temperature gradient
{title2=$\nabla T$}
{parent=Thermal conduction}

The temperature gradient is the <gradient> $\nabla T$ of the <temperature> field. It points in the direction of fastest temperature increase.

= Fourier's law
{title2=$\mathbf q=-k\nabla T$}
{c}
{parent=Thermal conduction}
{wiki}

<Fourier's law> predicts the local conductive <heat flux> from the local <temperature gradient>. In a <continuum approximation> with <isotropic> <thermal conductivity> $k>0$, its <vector> form and one-dimensional form are
$$
\mathbf q=-k\nabla T,\qquad
q=-k\frac{dT}{dx},\qquad
\dot Q=Aq\quad\text{for uniform normal flux over area }A.
$$
The <heat flux> $q$ has units $\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$, the <temperature gradient> has units $\mathrm{K\,m^{-1}}$ and the <thermal conductivity> has units $\mathrm{W\,m^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. The rate of <heat transfer> $\dot Q$ has units $\mathrm W$. These units distinguish a local material response from the total <power> crossing a surface.

The minus sign makes <heat> flow toward lower <temperature>: for $k>0$, $\mathbf q\cdot\nabla T=-k|\nabla T|^2\leq0$. In the worked bar in <thermal conduction>, $dT/dx=-200\,\mathrm{K\,m^{-1}}$ therefore gives $q=+400\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-2}}$. Reversing the end <temperatures> reverses the <heat flux>. A zero <temperature gradient> gives zero conductive <heat flux> in this model.

<Fourier's law> is a constitutive relation expressing measured material behavior; it is not a consequence of <conservation of energy> alone. It applies locally and does not require a <steady state>. The straight-line bar solution additionally assumes constant <thermal conductivity>, constant cross-sectional <area>, no sources or side losses, and fixed end <temperatures>. <Anisotropic> materials require the directional response described by the <thermal conductivity tensor>. The <scalar> $k$ used in the examples expresses the stated <isotropy> assumption. https://web.mit.edu/16.unified/www/FALL/thermodynamics/notes/node116.html[E. M. Greitzer, Z. S. Spakovszky and I. A. Waitz, MIT Thermodynamics and Propulsion, section 16.2: Fourier's law and thermal conductivity]{ref}

Historically, <Joseph Fourier>'s \i[Théorie analytique de la chaleur] was published in 1822. In Chapter II, article 112, he writes the <heat> crossing a spherical surface as the product of <thermal conductivity>, elapsed time, surface <area> and the negative radial <temperature gradient>; article 113 balances the incoming and outgoing <heat> to obtain the <heat equation> for a <sphere>. The enduring idea is this connection between a local flow rule and a global prediction of changing <temperature>. https://fr.wikisource.org/wiki/Théorie_analytique_de_la_chaleur[Joseph Fourier, Théorie analytique de la chaleur, Firmin Didot, 1822]{ref} https://fr.wikisource.org/wiki/Théorie_analytique_de_la_chaleur/Chapitre_2[Fourier, 1822, Chapter II, articles 112–113]{ref}

= Thermal conductivity
{title2=$k$}
{parent=Fourier's law}
{wiki}

<Thermal conductivity> is the material response coefficient in <Fourier's law>. For an <isotropic> material it is a <scalar> $k$, with units $\mathrm{W\,m^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$; larger positive $k$ means a larger conductive <heat flux> for the same <temperature gradient>. It can depend on <temperature> and composition. Direction-dependent response in an <anisotropic> material is described by a <thermal conductivity tensor>. The two worked examples use stipulated positive constants, rather than measured values for named materials.

<Thermal conductivity> describes material response, whereas <thermal resistance> also includes geometry: for the steady bar, $R_{\rm th}=L/(kA)$. Doubling $k$ at fixed <length>, cross-sectional <area> and end <temperatures> halves <thermal resistance> and doubles the rate of <heat transfer>. Doubling <length> changes <thermal resistance> without changing the material's <thermal conductivity>. This distinction lets a designer improve cooling either by choosing a higher <thermal conductivity> or by shortening and widening the path. It also explains why the less conductive layer in <thermal resistance> takes the greater <temperature> drop.

<Thermal diffusivity> answers a different question: how rapidly a <temperature> disturbance spreads. Its usual model $\kappa=k/(\rho c_p)$ also involves <mass density> $\rho$ and <specific heat capacity> $c_p$, so equal <thermal conductivities> need not imply equal rates of warming.

= Thermal conductivity tensor
{parent=Thermal conductivity}
{title2=$\mathbf q=-a\nabla T$}

The thermal conductivity tensor maps a temperature gradient to the negative heat-flux density. In passive reciprocal conduction it is symmetric positive definite. This permits direction-dependent transport while ensuring positive dissipated energy.

= Thermal diffusivity
{title2=$\kappa$}
{parent=Thermal conduction}
{wiki}

<Thermal diffusivity> measures how rapidly <thermal conduction> smooths a <temperature> difference inside a material. <Thermal conductivity> measures the <heat flux> driven by a specified <temperature gradient>; <thermal diffusivity> also accounts for the <heat capacity> that must be warmed. A material can carry substantial <heat> yet change <temperature> slowly because it stores substantial <internal energy> per <kelvin>.

Write $\alpha=\kappa$ for <thermal diffusivity>, reserving $k$ for <thermal conductivity>. In a homogeneous <isotropic> material,
$$
\alpha=\frac{k}{\rho c}=\frac{k}{C_{\mathrm{vol}}},\qquad
[\alpha]=\frac{\mathrm{W\,m^{-1}\,K^{-1}}}{\mathrm{J\,m^{-3}\,K^{-1}}}
=\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}.
$$
Here $\rho$ is <mass density>, $c$ is <specific heat capacity> for the relevant mechanical constraint, and $C_{\mathrm{vol}}=\rho c$ is <volumetric heat capacity>. The familiar $k/(\rho c_p)$ uses <specific heat capacity at constant pressure>. For ordinary <solids> with negligible thermal expansion, the difference between the two constrained <specific heat capacities> is often neglected. A rigid fixed-<volume (thermodynamics)> model instead uses <specific heat capacity at constant volume>; a compressible moving <fluid> requires the accompanying <work> and transport terms. Thus the constraint is part of the approximation, not a choice made solely from the units. <Light and Laser Flash Analyzers by NETZSCH Analyzing and Testing>[NETZSCH, Principle of the LFA Method: conductivity equals diffusivity times specific heat capacity times density]{ref}

Assume a stationary material in the <continuum approximation>, local thermal equilibration on times shorter than those resolved, no internal heating, no <phase transition>, and negligible <convection> and mechanical <work>. Combining <conservation of energy> with <Fourier's law> gives the one-dimensional balance
$$
\rho c\frac{\partial T}{\partial t}=-\frac{\partial q}{\partial x}
=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(k\frac{\partial T}{\partial x}\right).
$$
If $k,\rho,c$ are constant over the region and <temperature> excursion, this becomes the <heat equation> $\partial_tT=\alpha\partial_x^2T$. If $k$ varies, retain the differentiated product; replacing it with $k\partial_x^2T$ loses a term. <Anisotropic> materials need a <thermal conductivity tensor>. The scalar constant-$\alpha$ examples below deliberately exclude these complications. <Rear-surface integral method for calculating thermal diffusivity from laser flash experiments by Elliot J. Carr>[Carr, section 1: homogeneous isotropic constant-property assumptions; section 2, equations (1)–(3): insulated one-dimensional heat equation]{ref}

For an original comparison, stipulate two model materials with equal <thermal conductivity> $k=2\,\mathrm{W\,m^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$ and equal <mass density> $\rho=1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, but <specific heat capacities> $c_A=1000$ and $c_B=4000\,\mathrm{J\,kg^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. Then
$$
\alpha_A=2\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}},\qquad
\alpha_B=5\times10^{-7}\,\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}.
$$
At the same <temperature gradient>, both have the same instantaneous <heat flux> by <Fourier's law>. At the same initial <temperature> profile and <boundary conditions>, material B takes four times as long to smooth a given spatial pattern. With fixed end <temperatures>, identical geometry and no sources, both eventually have the same steady profile and <heat flux>. The larger <heat capacity> changes the transient, not this <steady state>. These are stipulated parameters, not measurements of named substances.

= Prandtl number
{c}
{parent=Thermal diffusivity}
{title2=$\operatorname{Pr}=\nu/\kappa_T$}
{wiki}

The <Prandtl number> is the ratio of <kinematic viscosity> to <thermal diffusivity>. It compares the rates at which momentum and <temperature> variations diffuse. In thermal-diffusion units the <Boussinesq equations> have momentum inertia multiplied by $\operatorname{Pr}^{-1}$; large, rather than small, <Prandtl number> supplies the usual instantaneous <Stokes flow> limit.

= Thermal diffusion time
{title2=$L^2/\kappa$}
{parent=Thermal diffusivity}

The <thermal diffusion time> $t_D=L^2/\alpha$, with $\alpha=\kappa$ the <thermal diffusivity>, is a characteristic scale for smoothing a <temperature> variation over <length> $L$. It is the thermal instance of the <diffusive time scale>. Rescaling $x=L\widehat x$ and $t=(L^2/\alpha)\widehat t$ removes $L$ and $\alpha$ from the constant-coefficient <heat equation>. Doubling <length> therefore multiplies comparable internal equilibration times by four; increasing <thermal diffusivity> by four divides them by four.

This scale is not a universal time of complete <thermal equilibrium>. A nonzero <Fourier mode> decays exponentially and never becomes exactly zero at finite time. The geometry, <boundary conditions>, initial pattern and accepted residual determine the numerical factor. For example, an insulated bar's slowest nonuniform <Fourier mode> has decay time $L^2/(\pi^2\alpha)$, and reducing its amplitude to a fraction $\varepsilon$ requires
$$
t_\varepsilon=\frac{L^2}{\pi^2\alpha}\log\frac1\varepsilon,
\qquad 0<\varepsilon<1.
$$
External <heat transfer> can impose a longer cooling time even when internal <temperature> differences smooth quickly. <Thermal diffusivity> alone does not determine cooling to the room's <temperature>.

= Thermal diffusion rate of a Fourier mode
{parent=Thermal diffusion time}
{title2=$\beta=\xi k^2$}

With <thermal diffusivity> $\xi$, a <Fourier mode> of angular <wavenumber> $k$ decays at rate $\beta=\xi k^2$. To avoid confusing this $k$ with <thermal conductivity>, use $\alpha=\xi$ and angular <wavenumber> $K$ below: the same rate is $\beta=\alpha K^2$, with units $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$.

Consider an insulated bar $0\leq x\leq L$ with constant <thermal diffusivity>, no sources and no <heat flux> through either end or the sides. An original initial <temperature> profile and its exact evolution are
$$
T(x,0)=\overline T+A_0\cos\frac{n\pi x}{L},\qquad
T(x,t)=\overline T+A_0e^{-\alpha K_n^2t}\cos\frac{n\pi x}{L},\qquad
K_n=\frac{n\pi}{L},\quad n=1,2,\ldots.
$$
The <cosine> has zero <temperature gradient> at both ends, satisfying the insulated <Neumann boundary conditions>. Its spatial mean is zero, so <conservation of energy> leaves the mean <temperature> $\overline T$ unchanged while the nonuniform part disappears. A short derivation substitutes $T=\overline T+A(t)\cos(K_nx)$ into the <heat equation>: the second spatial <derivative> supplies $-K_n^2$, hence $A'=-\alpha K_n^2A$. The <exponential decay> time is $\tau_n=1/(\alpha K_n^2)$; doubling angular <wavenumber> quarters this time. If cycles-per-<length> <spatial frequency> $f_x$ is used instead, $K=2\pi f_x$ and the rate is $4\pi^2\alpha f_x^2$.

\Image[thermal-diffusivity-insulated-fourier-mode-decay.png]
{title=Insulated-bar <Fourier mode> decay: equal <thermal conductivity>, different <heat capacities>}
{description=Original constant-property model with $L=0.010\,\mathrm m$ and $n=1$. The normalized initial <temperature> profile is identical for both stipulated materials in <thermal diffusivity>. The larger <volumetric heat capacity> gives a smaller <thermal diffusivity> and four times the decay time. Profiles at $t=10\,\mathrm s$ and amplitude histories are analytical predictions, not measured records.}

For the two model materials in <thermal diffusivity> and $L=0.010\,\mathrm m$, $\tau_{1,A}\approx5.07\,\mathrm s$ and $\tau_{1,B}\approx20.3\,\mathrm s$. After $10\,\mathrm s$, the remaining amplitude fractions are approximately $0.139$ and $0.610$. A narrow hot-and-cold pattern disappears faster than a broad one because its <temperature> curvature, and hence the imbalance in conductive <heat flux>, is larger.

= Thermal diffusion length
{parent=Thermal diffusion time}
{title2=$\ell(t)=\sqrt{\alpha t}$}

The <thermal diffusion length> $\ell(t)=\sqrt{\alpha t}$ is the characteristic <length> over which <thermal conduction> changes a <temperature> distribution during time $t$ in the constant-<thermal-diffusivity> model. It is a scale, not a sharp front or distance travelled at a constant speed: the <heat equation> has <infinite propagation speed> mathematically. Different definitions of penetration depth introduce numerical factors. In particular, the normalized one-dimensional <heat kernel> has spatial <variance> $2\alpha t$, so its root-mean-square width is $\sqrt{2\alpha t}$.

\Image[thermal-diffusivity-length-time-scaling.png]
{title=<Thermal diffusion length> grows as the square root of time; <thermal diffusion time> grows as <length> squared}
{description=Original predictions for the two stipulated materials in <thermal diffusivity>. Left: $\ell=\sqrt{\alpha t}$, with four times the time needed to double <length>. Right: $t_D=L^2/\alpha$ on logarithmic axes. Equal <thermal conductivity> with four times the <volumetric heat capacity> halves the reached <length> at a given time and quadruples the time at a given <length>. The scale $t_D$ differs from the insulated-bar decay time by the factor $\pi^2$.}

At $t=10\,\mathrm s$, the model materials have <thermal diffusion lengths> $4.47$ and $2.24\,\mathrm{mm}$. For $L=10\,\mathrm{mm}$ their <thermal diffusion times> are $50$ and $200\,\mathrm s$. These values describe the same <heat equation> as the <thermal diffusion rate of a Fourier mode>; the different numerical factors come from the definition of the scale.

= Laser flash analysis
{parent=Thermal diffusivity}
{wiki}

<Laser flash analysis> measures <thermal diffusivity> by heating one face of a specimen briefly and recording the opposite face's <temperature> rise. The original 1961 experiment used a flash tube rather than a <laser>: a blackened thin metal specimen sat in an opaque ceramic holder, with <thermocouple> wires pressed against its rear face. An amplifier, oscilloscope and camera recorded the signal. Uniform front illumination and the black coating promoted uniform absorption; the opaque low-<thermal-conductivity> holder blocked direct rear illumination and reduced edge losses. The delayed rear response reveals internal <thermal conduction> rather than direct heating of the sensor. <Flash Method of Determining Thermal Diffusivity, Heat Capacity, and Thermal Conductivity by W. J. Parker and others>[Parker, Jenkins, Butler and Abbott, pp. 1682–1683, Experimental Procedure and figure 3: holder, flash, thermocouple and recording chain]{ref}

A modern version uses a <laser> or flash lamp, a specimen of measured thickness $L$, controlled initial <temperature>, and a rear-facing <infrared> detector with a synchronized recorder. The readout is a <temperature> rise versus time, starting from a pre-pulse baseline. <Thermal conductivity> additionally requires <mass density> and <specific heat capacity>; it is not obtained from timing alone. <Light and Laser Flash Analyzers by NETZSCH Analyzing and Testing>[NETZSCH, Principle of the LFA Method: opposite-face infrared readout and conductivity product]{ref}

Under ideal one-dimensional, insulated, constant-property assumptions, instantaneous uniform absorption in a very thin front layer gives the normalized rear rise
$$
R(t)=\frac{T(L,t)-T_0}{T_\infty-T_0}
=1+2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-1)^n
\exp\left(-\frac{n^2\pi^2\alpha t}{L^2}\right),\qquad t>0.
$$
Here $T_0$ is the initial uniform <temperature> and $T_\infty$ is the final uniform <temperature> in the insulated model. Fitting this curve estimates <thermal diffusivity>; each term has the same $\alpha K_n^2$ decay as the insulated-bar <Fourier modes>. Equivalently, the half-rise time $t_{1/2}$ solves $R(t_{1/2})=1/2$. Writing $s=\alpha t/L^2$, the series gives $s_{1/2}\approx0.13879$, hence
$$
\alpha\approx0.13879\frac{L^2}{t_{1/2}}.
$$
This coefficient comes from the stated ideal <heat equation> solution, not from an empirical sensor calibration. It has been independently checked by solving the series equation. Absolute absorbed pulse <energy> cancels from the normalized curve; determining <heat capacity> from its absolute height needs additional information. <Rear-surface integral method for calculating thermal diffusivity from laser flash experiments by Elliot J. Carr>[Carr, section 2, equations (4)–(7): rear-face series, half-rise time and corrected dimensionless constant 1.370]{ref}

Parker and colleagues identify finite flash duration, <heat> loss, nonuniform illumination, recording bandwidth and electrical interference as practical limits. A specimen too thin responds during the pulse; one too thick allows losses before the rear rise finishes. They shielded the electrical chain and recorded a baseline; their reported tests were preliminary metal measurements near ambient and moderately elevated <temperatures>. <Flash Method of Determining Thermal Diffusivity, Heat Capacity, and Thermal Conductivity by W. J. Parker and others>[Parker and colleagues, pp. 1682–1684: signal distortion, shielding, thickness tradeoff and Conclusions]{ref}

Useful additional controls are to measure thickness at several points, verify the recorder's time scale and detector response independently, record a no-pulse baseline, and repeat at different pulse <energies> while avoiding <phase transitions>. Compare different thicknesses of the same homogeneous material: ideal half-rise times scale as $L^2$. Failure of these checks can expose finite-pulse effects, sensor delay, optical transmission, coating storage or edge losses; a thickness error is especially consequential because the inferred <thermal diffusivity> depends on $L^2$. These are proposed diagnostic controls, not invented measurements. When the pulse is not short or the sample exchanges appreciable <heat> with its surroundings, fit a model that includes those effects instead of applying the ideal half-rise coefficient unchanged.

= Stefan–Boltzmann law
{title2=$F=\epsilon\sigma T^4$}
{c}
{parent=Heat}
{wiki}

The Stefan–Boltzmann law gives the thermal-radiation flux emitted by a surface as $F=\epsilon\sigma T^4$, where $\epsilon$ is its <emissivity> and $\sigma$ is the Stefan–Boltzmann constant.

= Stefan-Boltzmann constant
{parent=Stefan–Boltzmann law}
{c}
{title2=$\sigma$}

The <Stefan-Boltzmann constant> $\sigma$ is the proportionality constant in the blackbody flux $F=\sigma T^4$. The blackbody <radiation pressure> is $p_{\rm rad}=4\sigma T^4/(3c)$.

= Emissivity
{title2=$\epsilon$}
{parent=Stefan–Boltzmann law}
{wiki}

Emissivity is the ratio of the thermal radiation emitted by a surface to that emitted by an ideal black body at the same <temperature>.

= Radiative equilibrium
{parent=Stefan–Boltzmann law}
{wiki}

A body is in radiative equilibrium when absorbed radiative power equals emitted radiative power. A surface absorbing flux $F$ and emitting as a grey body has equilibrium temperature $T=(F/(\epsilon\sigma))^{1/4}$.

= Heat capacity
{parent=Heat}
{title2=$C_X=(\delta Q/dT)_X$}
{wiki}

Heat capacity measures the <heat> supplied per small <temperature> increase along a specified process:
$$
C_X=\left(\frac{\delta Q}{dT}\right)_X.
$$
The subscript $X$ records the constraint, such as fixed <volume (thermodynamics)> or fixed <pressure>. The units are $\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}$. The symbol $\delta Q$ emphasizes that <heat> is a transfer along a path, rather than a <state function> with a differential $dQ$. Consequently, an unspecified <heat capacity> does not determine the required <heat>.

For a <closed system> of fixed composition undergoing a <quasistatic process>, assume that only <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> <work> occurs, with no <friction> and no change in bulk <kinetic energy> or <potential energy>. The <first law of thermodynamics> and $H=U+pV$, where $U$ is <internal energy> and $H$ is <enthalpy>, then give
$$
\delta Q=dU+p\,dV=dH-V\,dp,\qquad
C_V=\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial T}\right)_V,\qquad
C_p=\left(\frac{\partial H}{\partial T}\right)_p.
$$
These <partial derivatives> define <heat capacity at constant volume> and <heat capacity at constant pressure>, with the amount and composition also held fixed. Integrating along the selected constraint gives $Q=\int_{T_1}^{T_2}C_X(T)\,dT$ within a single <thermodynamic phase>; $Q=C_X\Delta T$ requires constant $C_X$. <Statistical Physics by David Tong/4 Classical Thermodynamics>{ref}

For a computed example, take a <monatomic gas> in the classical <ideal gas> regime, with <amount of substance> $n=1\,\mathrm{mol}$ and stipulated <molar gas constant> $R=8.314\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. The <equipartition theorem> gives $U=3nRT/2$, and the <ideal gas law> gives $pV=nRT$. Thus
$$
C_V=\frac32nR=12.471\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}},\qquad
C_p=\frac52nR=20.785\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}.
$$
For the same <temperature> increase $\Delta T=10\,\mathrm K$, the two processes require
$$
Q_V=124.71\,\mathrm J,\qquad Q_p=207.85\,\mathrm J,\qquad
\Delta U_V=\Delta U_p=124.71\,\mathrm J,\qquad
W_V=0,\quad W_p=p\Delta V=nR\Delta T=83.14\,\mathrm J.
$$
Here <work> done by the <gas> is positive. At fixed <pressure>, the extra <heat> supplies expansion <work>; the <internal energy> increase is the same because <internal energy of an ideal gas> depends only on <temperature>. Their final <volume (thermodynamics)> values differ. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/3-5-heat-capacities-of-an-ideal-gas[OpenStax, Heat capacities of an ideal gas]{ref}

\Image[heat-capacity-constraints.png]
{title=Required <heat> depends on the constraint and the amount of material}
{description=Left: one <mole> of classical <monatomic gas> with the stipulated <molar gas constant> above; the shaded difference is expansion <work> at fixed <pressure>. Right: the illustrative fixed-<volume (thermodynamics)> body from <specific heat capacity>, with <mass> $2\,\mathrm{kg}$ and $c_V=400\,\mathrm{J\,kg^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. Each curve starts at zero accumulated <heat>, rather than zero absolute <internal energy>.}

The practical value of <heat capacity> is that a measured <heat> input and <temperature> response can determine it. Once its constraint and <temperature> dependence are known, it predicts heating requirements and supplies <entropy> changes through <entropy from heat capacity>. https://davidtong.org/teaching/statistical-physics/statmechhtml/S1[Tong, Statistical Physics, heat capacity as a measurable response]{ref}

= Molar heat capacity
{parent=Heat capacity}
{title2=$C_{\mathrm m}=C/n$}

Molar heat capacity is <heat capacity> divided by <amount of substance> $n$, with units $\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. Its constraint must still be specified: $C_{V,\mathrm m}=C_V/n$ and $C_{p,\mathrm m}=C_p/n$. For the classical <monatomic gas> example in <heat capacity>, these are $3R/2$ and $5R/2$, where $R$ is the <molar gas constant>. Some textbooks use $C_V$ and $C_p$ for these per-<mole> quantities; the convention here reserves them for the whole sample.

= Heating curve
{parent=Heat capacity}

A heating curve records <temperature> against accumulated supplied <heat> under a specified constraint. Within one <thermodynamic phase>, its slope is $dT/dQ=1/C_X$, where $C_X$ is the constrained <heat capacity>. A <latent heat> plateau instead records changing <phase fraction> at the <phase coexistence> <temperature>. The <sensible heat> example combines two sloping intervals and a <melting> plateau in one heating budget.

= Entropy from heat capacity
{parent=Heat capacity}

Along a reversible path at fixed volume, $dS=C_V(T)dT/T$. Hence entropy differences can be measured macroscopically from
$$
S(T_2)-S(T_1)=\int_{T_1}^{T_2}\frac{C_V(T)}T\,dT,
$$
with an additional $L/T_c$ across a first-order transition of <latent heat> $L$ at $T_c$.

= Entropy concavity and positive heat capacity
{parent=Heat capacity}

For a thermodynamically stable system, the <entropy> is concave in energy, so
$$
\frac{\partial^2S}{\partial E^2}
=-\frac1{T^2}\frac{\partial T}{\partial E}<0.
$$
Consequently $\partial E/\partial T>0$: energy increases with <temperature>, and the corresponding <heat capacity> is positive.

= Specific heat capacity
{title2=$c=C/m$}
{parent=Heat capacity}
{wiki}

Specific heat capacity is <heat capacity> per unit <mass>:
$$
c=\frac Cm,\qquad c_V=\frac{C_V}{m},\qquad c_p=\frac{C_p}{m}.
$$
Its units are $\mathrm{J\,kg^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$, and the constraint remains part of the definition. In particular, <specific heat capacity at constant volume> and <specific heat capacity at constant pressure> are different responses. For homogeneous material, doubling the <mass> doubles its <heat capacity> while leaving its <specific heat capacity> unchanged. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/1-4-heat-transfer-specific-heat-and-calorimetry[OpenStax, Specific heat and heating calculations]{ref}

As an illustrative material model, give a fixed-<volume (thermodynamics)> body <mass> $m=2\,\mathrm{kg}$ and constant <specific heat capacity at constant volume> $c_V=400\,\mathrm{J\,kg^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. With no <phase transition> and no other <work>, increasing its uniform <temperature> by $10\,\mathrm K$ requires
$$
C_V=mc_V=800\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}},\qquad
Q=mc_V\Delta T=8000\,\mathrm J=\Delta U.
$$
These stipulated values describe no measured substance. The right panel of the diagram in <heat capacity> shows the straight line $Q=mc_V\Delta T$: a <temperature> increase of $5\,\mathrm K$ takes $4000\,\mathrm J$ of <heat>. If the <specific heat capacity at constant volume> depends on <temperature>, use $Q=m\int_{T_1}^{T_2}c_V(T)\,dT$ instead. The corresponding fixed-<pressure> calculation uses <specific heat capacity at constant pressure> and gives $Q=\Delta H$ under the assumptions in <heat capacity>.

Choosing between <specific heat capacity>, <molar heat capacity> and <volumetric heat capacity> is choosing whether to compare equal <mass>, equal <amount of substance> or equal <volume (thermodynamics)>. A small sample with large <specific heat capacity> can still have less total <heat capacity> than a much larger sample.

= Volumetric heat capacity
{parent=Specific heat capacity}
{title2=$C_{\mathrm{vol}}=C/V=\rho c$}
{wiki}

Volumetric heat capacity is <heat capacity> per unit <volume (thermodynamics)>, with units $\mathrm{J\,m^{-3}\,K^{-1}}$. For homogeneous material of <mass density> $\rho=m/V$, it equals $\rho c$, using <specific heat capacity> $c$ for the same constraint. It measures the <heat> required per small <temperature> increase for a chosen occupied <volume (thermodynamics)>. In a material model using <specific heat capacity at constant pressure>, <thermal diffusivity> is $\alpha=k/(\rho c_p)$, where $k$ is <thermal conductivity>.

= Specific heat capacity at constant volume
{parent=Specific heat capacity}
{title2=$c_V=(\partial u/\partial T)_\rho$}

The specific heat capacity at constant volume is the temperature derivative of <specific internal energy> at fixed density and composition: $c_V=(\partial u/\partial T)_\rho$. It is the corresponding extensive <heat capacity at constant volume> divided by material mass.

= Specific heat capacity at constant pressure
{title2=$c_P$}
{parent=Specific heat capacity}

The <specific heat capacity> at fixed <pressure> and composition is $c_P=(\partial h/\partial T)_P$, where $h$ is <specific enthalpy>. Equivalently, it is <heat capacity at constant pressure> divided by <mass>. Under the process assumptions in <heat capacity>, it includes expansion <work> as well as the <internal energy> change. The <sensible heat> example uses this response separately for <ice> and <water>.

= Pure-radiation constant-pressure heat-capacity singularity
{parent=Specific heat capacity at constant pressure}

For pure <blackbody radiation>, $P=aT^4/3$ depends only on <temperature>. Constant pressure therefore fixes temperature, so a usual finite constant-pressure temperature derivative is unavailable. In the material gas-radiation limit the <specific-heat ratio> diverges, although all three <stellar adiabatic exponents> approach $4/3$. The radiation index $4/3$ is an adiabatic pressure-volume response, not a finite heat-capacity ratio.

= Heat capacity at constant volume
{title2=$C_V$}
{parent=Heat capacity}

= Constant-volume heat capacity
{synonym}

For a fixed-composition <closed system>, the <heat capacity> at fixed <volume (thermodynamics)> is $C_V=(\partial U/\partial T)_V$, with <internal energy> $U$. Under the <quasistatic process> assumptions in <heat capacity>, only <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> <work> is permitted, so $dV=0$ gives $\delta Q=dU=C_V\,dT$. A rigid vessel therefore needs $Q=\Delta U$, or $Q=C_V\Delta T$ when $C_V$ is constant. Its per-unit-<mass> counterpart is <specific heat capacity at constant volume>.

= Dulong-Petit law
{c}
{parent=Heat capacity at constant volume}
{wiki=Dulong–Petit_law}

In the classical high-<temperature> limit, each independent <harmonic oscillator> contributes $k_B$ to the <heat capacity at constant volume>, where $k_B$ is the <Boltzmann constant>. A three-dimensional crystalline <solid> with $N$ <chemical atoms> has $3N$ vibrational <normal modes> in the bulk harmonic approximation. Its vibrational <internal energy> and <heat capacity at constant volume> therefore obey
$$
U_{\rm vib}=U_0+3Nk_BT,\qquad
C_V=\left(\frac{\partial U_{\rm vib}}{\partial T}\right)_{V,N}=3Nk_B.
$$
Here $U_0$ is independent of <temperature> at fixed <volume (thermodynamics)>. Thus the <Dulong-Petit law> predicts $C_V\simeq3Nk_B$ for a real <solid> in this regime. Per <mole> of <chemical atoms>, rather than per <mole> of a compound's formula units,
$$
C_{V,\rm mol}\longrightarrow3R\simeq24.9434\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}},\qquad R=N_Ak_B,
$$
with <molar gas constant> $R$ and <Avogadro constant> $N_A$. A formula unit containing $r$ <chemical atoms> consequently has a classical vibrational prediction of $3rR$ per <mole> of formula units.

For example, in this ideal classical model, one <mole> of <chemical atoms> heated at fixed <volume (thermodynamics)> from $300$ to $600\,\mathrm K$ gains vibrational <internal energy>
$$
\Delta U_{\rm vib}=3R(600-300)\,\mathrm K\simeq7483.02\,\mathrm J.
$$
The <heat capacity at constant volume> stays the same even though the thermal part of the <internal energy> doubles. These <temperatures> illustrate the stipulated classical model; they do not assert that every real <solid> is classical at $300\,\mathrm K$.

\Image[dulong-petit-classical-energy.png]
{title=Classical <equipartition theorem>: one <harmonic oscillator> stores $k_BT$, one <chemical atom> stores $3k_BT$}
{description=Mean <kinetic energy> and <potential energy> at $300$ and $600\,\mathrm K$, in units of $k_B(300\,\mathrm K)$. Each independent quadratic term contributes $k_BT/2$. The three-direction model has three kinetic and three potential terms per <chemical atom>; doubling <temperature> doubles both contributions.}

The proof outline is the <equipartition theorem>: one classical <harmonic oscillator> has a quadratic <kinetic energy> and a quadratic <potential energy>, each with mean $k_BT/2$. Their sum is $k_BT$, whose <derivative> with respect to <temperature> is $k_B$. Summing over $3N$ <normal modes> gives $3Nk_B$. The classical result does not require equal <angular frequencies>: each harmonic <normal mode> contributes the same amount once it is classical. Overall rigid motions give only a finite-size correction to the bulk count. This independence from the individual <angular frequencies> makes the <Dulong-Petit law> useful as a quick estimate of a <solid>'s heating requirement.

Experiments on freely expanding <solids> often measure the <heat capacity at constant pressure> $C_P$, whereas the harmonic prediction is for the <heat capacity at constant volume> $C_V$. In a smooth single <thermodynamic phase> of a simple compressible material, the <difference between constant-pressure and constant-volume heat capacities> is
$$
C_P-C_V=\frac{TV\alpha^2}{\kappa_T},
$$
where $\alpha=V^{-1}(\partial V/\partial T)_P$ is the volumetric <coefficient of thermal expansion> and $\kappa_T=-V^{-1}(\partial V/\partial P)_T$ is the <isothermal compressibility>. Using measured $C_P$ as an approximation to $C_V$ requires this correction to be small compared with $C_V$. https://davidtong.org/teaching/statistical-physics/statmechhtml/S4#S4.SS4.SSS2[David Tong, Statistical Physics, section 4.4.2: relation between the two heat capacities]{ref}

High <temperature> here means $k_BT\gg\hbar\omega$ for the relevant vibrational <normal modes>, with reduced <Planck constant> $\hbar$ and mode <angular frequency> $\omega$. At low <temperature>, excitation across a quantum <energy> spacing is suppressed: modes with $\hbar\omega\gg k_BT$ freeze out thermally, and the classical <equipartition theorem> fails. The <Einstein model of a solid> makes this loss of vibrational <heat capacity> explicit. The <Dulong-Petit law> is therefore an ideal high-<temperature> vibrational limit, not a statement that all <solids> have the same <heat capacity> at every <temperature>. Other contributions and departures from the harmonic approximation can matter even at high <temperature>. https://davidtong.org/teaching/statistical-physics/statmechhtml/S3#S3.SS3[David Tong, Statistical Physics, section 3.3: solid vibrations, the classical limit and quantum freeze-out]{ref}

Historically, Petit and Dulong's 1819 experiments compared the <specific heat capacities> of several elemental <solids> and found an approximately constant product with their relative atomic weights. Their original empirical regularity came before the classical harmonic explanation; it suggested that counting <chemical atoms> reveals a simpler pattern than comparing equal <masses>. https://webserver.lemoyne.edu/giunta/PETIT.html[Petit and Dulong, Recherches sur quelques points importants de la Théorie de la Chaleur, Annales de Chimie et de Physique 10, 395–413 (1819), contemporary English translation hosted by Carmen Giunta]{ref}

= Einstein solid
{c}
{parent=Dulong-Petit law}
{title2=$C_V/(3Nk_B)=x^2e^x/(e^x-1)^2$}
{wiki}

= Einstein model of a solid
{c}
{synonym}

The <Einstein model of a solid> represents the vibrations of $N$ <chemical atoms> by $3N$ independent one-dimensional <quantum harmonic oscillators>, all with the same positive <angular frequency> $\omega$. Equivalently, each <chemical atom> has three independent oscillator directions. The common <angular frequency> is a simplifying model assumption, not a claim about every vibration of a real <solid>. Hold the <volume (thermodynamics)>, $N$ and $\omega$ fixed. Define the characteristic <temperature> $\theta_E=\hbar\omega/k_B$ and the dimensionless ratio $x=\theta_E/T$. The vibrational <internal energy> is
$$
U=3N\hbar\omega\left[\frac12+\frac{1}{e^x-1}\right],\qquad
x=\frac{\hbar\omega}{k_BT}=\frac{\theta_E}{T}.
$$
The first term is the <zero-point energy> $3N\hbar\omega/2$. It is constant at fixed $\omega$, so it contributes no <heat capacity at constant volume>. The <temperature>-dependent term gives
$$
\frac{C_V}{3Nk_B}=\frac{x^2e^x}{(e^x-1)^2}
=\frac{x^2e^{-x}}{(1-e^{-x})^2}.
$$
This is the vibrational <heat capacity at constant volume> of the model, with no expansion or other contributions included. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/3-012-fundamentals-of-materials-science-fall-2005/d3fa883afa8df7cf7aa0432fcaadde8f_lec22t.pdf[MIT 3.012, Lecture 22, pages 9–13: Einstein-solid partition function, energy and heat capacity]{ref}

For a worked comparison, choose the model parameter $\theta_E=300\,\mathrm K$. The normalized <heat capacity at constant volume> depends only on $T/\theta_E$:

|| $T/\theta_E$
|| <Temperature> $T$ in $\mathrm K$
|| $C_V/(3Nk_B)$

| $0.1$
| $30$
| $0.00454$

| $0.5$
| $150$
| $0.72406$

| $1$
| $300$
| $0.92067$

| $2$
| $600$
| $0.97942$

At $T=\theta_E$, one <mole> of <chemical atoms> therefore has vibrational <heat capacity at constant volume> $3R(0.92067)\simeq22.965\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$, using the unrounded model value. Even at twice the characteristic <temperature>, the model remains about $2.06\%$ below the <Dulong-Petit law> limit. These are calculations for the ideal <Einstein solid>, not material measurements.

\Image[einstein-solid-heat-capacity.png]
{title=<Einstein solid> vibrational <heat capacity> approaches the <Dulong-Petit law> limit}
{description=The model ratio $C_V/(3Nk_B)$ over $0.02\leq T/\theta_E\leq3$, with the four computed cases marked. The dashed classical line is $1$; quantum freeze-out suppresses the low-<temperature> response. All <quantum harmonic oscillators> have the same stipulated <angular frequency>.}

A short derivation uses the <thermal partition function of a quantum harmonic oscillator>. The <energy eigenvalues> $E_n=\hbar\omega(n+1/2)$ and their <Boltzmann factors> form a <geometric series>, giving the one-oscillator <canonical partition function> $z=e^{-x/2}/(1-e^{-x})$. The mean occupation is $1/(e^x-1)$, hence the displayed $U$ after summing over $3N$ independent <quantum harmonic oscillators>. Taking its <derivative> with $dx/dT=-x/T$ gives the displayed <heat capacity at constant volume>. https://davidtong.org/pdfs/teaching/statistical-physics/omg1.pdf[David Tong, Statistical Physics, Example Sheet 1 (January 2012), problem 5: oscillator energy, the Einstein model and its limits]{ref}

The two limits explain the shape. At high <temperature>, a <Taylor series> in small $x$ gives
$$
\frac{C_V}{3Nk_B}=1-\frac{x^2}{12}+O(x^4)\longrightarrow1
\qquad(T\gg\theta_E).
$$
At low <temperature>, $1-e^{-x}\to1$, so
$$
\frac{C_V}{3Nk_B}\sim x^2e^{-x}\longrightarrow0
\qquad(T\ll\theta_E).
$$
The <zero-point energy> remains even when the thermal response is tiny: freeze-out means that changing <temperature> scarcely changes the occupations, not that the <chemical atoms> have zero <energy>. This is the useful lesson of the <Einstein model of a solid>: the classical counting rule reappears when many levels are accessible, while discrete excitation spacings explain its failure upon cooling.

The exponential low-<temperature> decay is also the model's limitation. Real three-dimensional crystalline <solids> have <acoustic phonon branches> with arbitrarily small <angular frequencies> in the bulk limit. The <Debye model> includes these collective <normal modes> and predicts a low-<temperature> vibrational contribution $C_V\propto T^3$, which the single-frequency <Einstein solid> cannot reproduce. This qualification concerns the vibrational contribution; additional excitations can change a material's total <heat capacity>. https://davidtong.org/teaching/statistical-physics/statmechhtml/S3#S3.SS3[David Tong, Statistical Physics, section 3.3: acoustic phonons and the Debye low-temperature law]{ref}

Einstein introduced the quantum treatment of <solid> vibrations in his 1907 paper \i[Die Plancksche Theorie der Strahlung und die Theorie der spezifischen Wärme]. Its historical importance is that quantum excitation energies explained why the classical <Dulong-Petit law> could fail; the modern <quantum harmonic oscillator> formulation above includes the <zero-point energy> explicitly. https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/andp.19063270110[A. Einstein, Annalen der Physik 327, 180–190 (1907), original-paper publication metadata]{ref}

= Heat capacity at constant area
{title2=$C_A$}
{parent=Heat capacity}

For a two-dimensional system of fixed area $A$, the heat capacity at constant area is $C_A=(\partial E/\partial T)_{N,A}$ when the particle number and other conserved quantities are also fixed. It is the two-dimensional analogue of <heat capacity at constant volume>.

= Third law of thermodynamics
{c}
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}

The third law implies that the entropy of a system with a nondegenerate ground state approaches zero as its temperature approaches absolute zero. In particular, ordinary equilibrium heat capacities approach zero.

= Pressure
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{title2=$P=F_\perp/A$}
{wiki}

Pressure measures the compressive <normal force> per unit <surface area>. For a uniformly loaded surface of <surface area> $A$, the magnitude of the perpendicular <force> is $F_\perp=PA$; locally, $P=dF_\perp/dA$. Its <SI> unit is the pascal, $1\,\mathrm{Pa}=1\,\mathrm{N\,m^{-2}}$. A <force> parallel to the surface contributes shear <stress>, rather than <pressure>. In a <gas>, the steady macroscopic <force> comes from many microscopic transfers of <momentum>; <ideal gas pressure from wall collisions> makes this connection explicit. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/2-2-pressure-temperature-and-rms-speed[OpenStax University Physics Volume 2, section 2.2: microscopic origin of pressure]{ref}

For a computed example, a uniform <pressure> of $100\,\mathrm{kPa}$ on a piston of <surface area> $10\,\mathrm{cm^2}=10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m^2}$ produces a perpendicular <force> of $100\,\mathrm N$. If the opposite face is exposed to $80\,\mathrm{kPa}$, the net <force> due to the two <pressures> is $(100-80)\times10^3\times10^{-3}=20\,\mathrm N$, directed toward the lower-<pressure> side. The <pressure> on either face and their difference answer different questions; <gauge pressure> records a difference from a specified reference, often <atmospheric pressure>.

A scalar <pressure> describes a <gas> in local <thermodynamic equilibrium> with <isotropic> random particle <velocities>: every wall orientation has the same normal <force> per unit <surface area>. Without this <isotropy>, normal <stresses> can depend on direction and a <pressure tensor> is needed. Thus random motion alone is insufficient; a preferred direction matters.

In <thermodynamics>, <pressure> and <volume (thermodynamics)> are <thermodynamic conjugate variables>. For a <simple compressible system>, reversible expansion delivers <work> $\delta W=P\,dV$, and at fixed <entropy> $S$ and particle number $N$,
$$
P=-\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial V}\right)_{S,N},
$$
where $U$ is <internal energy>. A large <pressure> therefore means both a large wall <force> per unit <surface area> and a large amount of expansion <work> per small increase in <volume (thermodynamics)>.

= Atmospheric pressure
{parent=Pressure}
{wiki}
{title2=$p_{\rm atm}$}

<Atmospheric pressure> often provides the reference for a <gauge pressure> measurement: $p_g=p_{\rm abs}-p_{\rm atm}$. The absolute <pressure> is measured relative to an ideal <vacuum>, whereas zero <gauge pressure> means equality to the chosen reference. The actual <atmospheric pressure> can vary; it must be specified when converting between these descriptions. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-1/pages/14-2-measuring-pressure[OpenStax: atmospheric, gauge and absolute pressure]{ref}.

= Kinematic pressure
{parent=Pressure}
{title2=$p/\rho_0$}

Dividing a <pressure> disturbance by the constant reference <mass density> $\rho_0$ gives a quantity whose <gradient> enters the <Boussinesq equations> directly as an <acceleration>. Its units are squared <velocity>. Distinguish this convention from physical <pressure> when calculating <energy flux> or comparing <wave polarization> formulae.

= Pressure tensor
{parent=Pressure}
{title2=$P_{ij}$}

In the local rest frame of a gas, the <pressure tensor> is the directional <momentum flux> $P_{ij}=\int p_iv_jf(\mathbf p)\,d^3p$, where $f$ is the <phase-space distribution function> per unit <momentum> volume and $\mathbf v$ is particle velocity. For an isotropic distribution, $P_{ij}=P\delta_{ij}$, recovering the <kinetic pressure of an isotropic gas>. An anisotropic distribution can exert different normal <pressures> along different directions.

= Gas pressure
{title2=$p_{\rm gas}$}
{parent=Pressure}

Gas pressure is the mechanical pressure contributed by material particles. For a classical ideal gas, $p_{\rm gas}=nk_BT$.

= Radiation pressure
{title2=$p_{\rm rad}$}
{parent=Pressure}
{wiki}

Radiation pressure is momentum flux carried by electromagnetic radiation. Isotropic blackbody radiation has $p_{\rm rad}=a_{\rm rad}T^4/3$.

= Equation of state
{parent=Pressure}
{wiki}

= Equations of state
{synonym}

An equation of state is a relation among thermodynamic state variables such as pressure, volume, temperature, energy, and particle number.

= Barotropic stellar equation of state
{parent=Equation of state}
{title2=$p=p(\rho)$}

= Stellar barotropic equation of state
{synonym}

A <perfect fluid in general relativity> stellar model in which pressure depends only on energy density, $p=p(\rho)$. A positive derivative makes this relation locally invertible and turns outward-decreasing pressure into outward-decreasing density. This general relation need not have the constant ratio $p/\rho$ used for a constant cosmological fluid parameter.

= Clausius gas
{parent=Equation of state}
{c}

A Clausius gas with covolume parameter $b$ obeys $P(V-Nb)=Nk_BT$. It is the repulsive-volume part of a <Van der Waals equation> without the attractive term.

= Van der Waals equation
{c}
{parent=Equation of state}
{wiki}

The <Van der Waals equation> is a simple <real gas> <equation of state> that can also describe a <liquid-gas critical point>. With particle count $N$ fixed, write the <volume per particle> as $v=V/N$:
$$
p=\frac{k_BT}{v-b}-\frac{a}{v^2},\qquad v>b,\quad a>0,\quad b>0,
$$
or equivalently $(p+aN^2/V^2)(V-Nb)=Nk_BT$. Here $p$ is <pressure>, $T$ is absolute <temperature>, and $k_B$ is the <Boltzmann constant>. In <SI>, $v$ and $b$ have units $\mathrm{m^3}$ per particle, $a$ has units $\mathrm{Pa\,m^6}=\mathrm{J\,m^3}$, and $k_B$ has units $\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}$; the particle count is dimensionless. The pole at $v=b$ excludes smaller <volume (thermodynamics)> values from this model.

The $b$ correction represents <excluded volume>; the attraction term lowers <pressure> at fixed <temperature> and particle <number density> $1/v$. This is a <mean-field theory>, replacing detailed intermolecular structure by two parameters. At $v\gg b$ with $a/v^2\ll k_BT/v$, it approaches the <ideal gas law> $pv=k_BT$. It supplies a useful qualitative route from a dilute <gas> to <phase coexistence>, without a claim of quantitative accuracy for every <fluid>.<Thermodynamics and Statistical Mechanics by Richard Fitzpatrick/Van der Waals Gas>[opening explanation and equation 6.156: excluded volume and attractive pressure correction; Fitzpatrick uses molar volume]{ref}

Do not mix this particle convention with the <molar volume> convention elsewhere in this article. With <amount of substance> $n_{\rm mol}=N/N_A$, <Avogadro constant> $N_A$, and <molar gas constant> $R=N_Ak_B$, one has $v_m=N_Av$, $b_m=N_Ab$, and $a_m=N_A^2a$, so $p=RT/(v_m-b_m)-a_m/v_m^2$. The molar parameters have units $\mathrm{m^3\,mol^{-1}}$ and $\mathrm{Pa\,m^6\,mol^{-2}}$, respectively.

<Johannes Diderik van der Waals> introduced this <equation of state> in 1873, connecting molecular attraction with departures from the dilute <ideal gas law>. Its historical significance is the unified treatment of <gases> and <liquids>, rather than a universally exact fit.<Johannes Diderik van der Waals, Facts by Nobel Prize Outreach>[Nobel Prize Outreach, Johannes Diderik van der Waals, Work: equation introduced in 1873 and molecular attraction]{ref}

= Van der Waals law of corresponding states
{c}
{parent=Van der Waals equation}

The <Van der Waals equation> becomes independent of its material parameters when <pressure>, <temperature> and molecular <volume> are divided by their critical values: $\bar p=8\bar T/(3\bar v-1)-3/\bar v^2$. This explains the common reduced isotherms within this model, without claiming exact agreement for all real fluids.

= Van der Waals isotherm
{c}
{parent=Van der Waals equation}
{title2=$p(v,T)$}

A <Van der Waals isotherm> is the <pressure>–particle <volume (thermodynamics)> curve at a fixed <temperature>. Put $x=v/v_c$, $t=T/T_c$, and $q=p/p_c$, using the <Van der Waals critical point> values below. Then the existing <Van der Waals law of corresponding states> becomes
$$
q(x,t)=\frac{8t}{3x-1}-\frac{3}{x^2},\qquad x>\frac13.
$$
All curves in the figure are calculations in this reduced model.

\Image[van-der-waals-reduced-isotherms.png]
{id=van-der-waals-reduced-isotherms}
{title=<Van der Waals isotherms> below, at and above the <Van der Waals critical point>}
{description=The dashed subcritical homogeneous continuation contains an unstable red segment. The green <Maxwell construction> plateau describes equilibrium <phase coexistence>; the two shaded areas are equal. Curves are model calculations, not <carbon dioxide> measurements.}

For a uniform <thermodynamic phase>, the <isothermal compressibility> is
$$
\kappa_T=-\frac1v\left(\frac{\partial v}{\partial p}\right)_T
=-\frac{1}{v(\partial p/\partial v)_T}.
$$
Thus the upward-sloping portion of a subcritical <Van der Waals isotherm> has $\kappa_T<0$: compression would reduce <pressure>, allowing a density disturbance to grow. It is mechanically unstable. The <spinodal points> bound that portion; setting the <derivative> $\partial q/\partial x$ to zero gives their curve $t=(3x-1)^2/(4x^3)$. Negative slope alone guarantees only local mechanical stability: portions between the <spinodal> and the <phase coexistence> endpoints have <metastability>. Equilibrium replaces the entire interval between those endpoints, including these metastable portions, by a <Maxwell construction> plateau.<Statistical Physics by David Tong>[§5.1, equation 5.3 and §5.1.1, Maxwell Construction and Meta-Stable States: unstable middle branch and coexistence versus spinodal]{ref}

= Van der Waals critical point
{c}
{parent=Van der Waals equation}
{title2=$(v_c,T_c,p_c)$}

The <liquid-gas critical point> of the <Van der Waals equation> occurs where its two subcritical stationary points merge into a horizontal <inflection point>. The conditions are $(\partial p/\partial v)_T=(\partial^2p/\partial v^2)_T=0$. The short calculation is
$$
-\frac{k_BT_c}{(v_c-b)^2}+\frac{2a}{v_c^3}=0,\qquad
\frac{2k_BT_c}{(v_c-b)^3}-\frac{6a}{v_c^4}=0.
$$
Dividing these relations gives $2/(v_c-b)=3/v_c$, hence
$$
v_c=3b,\qquad T_c=\frac{8a}{27bk_B},\qquad p_c=\frac{a}{27b^2}.
$$
Substitution gives the <Van der Waals law of corresponding states> and the parameter-independent ratio $p_cv_c/(k_BT_c)=3/8$. That ratio belongs to this <equation of state>; reducing a real <fluid>'s variables does not enforce it.<Statistical Physics by David Tong>[§5.1.3, equations 5.7–5.8: critical values, reduced equation and compressibility ratio]{ref}

An exact rearrangement of the reduced critical <Van der Waals isotherm> makes its flatness visible:
$$
q(x,1)-1=-\frac{3(x-1)^3}{x^2(3x-1)}.
$$
The first nonzero departure near $x=1$ is $-3(x-1)^3/2$. On the critical particle <volume (thermodynamics)> approached from $t>1$, $(\partial q/\partial x)_{x=1}=-6(t-1)$ and
$$
\kappa_T=\frac{1}{6p_c(t-1)}.
$$
The divergent <isothermal compressibility> and cubic flattening explain why the critical region is mechanically soft. These are <mean-field theory> predictions; <thermal fluctuations> limit their use for real <fluids> very close to a <thermodynamic critical point>.

= Van der Waals liquid-gas coexistence
{c}
{parent=Van der Waals equation}

Below $T_c$, the equilibrium <liquid> and <gas> have the same <temperature>, <pressure> $p_*$, and per-particle <chemical potential>. For macroscopic bulk <thermodynamic phases>, neglect interface contributions. The <Maxwell equal-area construction> determines $v_l$ and $v_g$ by
$$
p(v_l,T)=p(v_g,T)=p_*,\qquad
\int_{v_l}^{v_g}[p(v,T)-p_*]\,dv=0.
$$
It expresses equality of <chemical potentials>, since $d\mu=v\,dp$ at fixed <temperature>. Equivalently, the equilibrium <Helmholtz free energy> per particle follows a common <tangent line> between the two endpoint states, lying below the homogeneous continuation between them. The construction uses that mathematical continuation to compare endpoints; it is not a physical compression path through negative <isothermal compressibility> states.<Statistical Physics by David Tong>[§5.1.1, equation 5.5, Maxwell Construction and figures 36–37: chemical-potential equality and flat coexistence intervals]{ref}

Here is a reproducible calculated example at $t=0.9$. With $q_*=p_*/p_c$, the homogeneous intersections solve the <cubic equation>
$$
3q_*x^3-(q_*+8t)x^2+9x-3=0.
$$
The <Maxwell construction> gives
$$
q_*\simeq0.646998352,\qquad
(x_l,x_m,x_g)\simeq(0.603401903,\ 1.090526633,\ 2.348842376).
$$
All three <roots of a polynomial> satisfy $x>1/3$. Only the smallest and largest are the <phase coexistence> endpoints; the middle one has negative <isothermal compressibility>. Equal <pressures> at three admissible values of <volume (thermodynamics)> therefore do not imply three stable <thermodynamic phases>. An analytic area check is
$$
\frac{8t}{3}\ln\frac{3x_g-1}{3x_l-1}
+3\left(\frac1{x_g}-\frac1{x_l}\right)-q_*(x_g-x_l)=0.
$$
At full numerical precision, the residual is below $10^{-11}$ in reduced units; independent numerical integration gives the same cancellation. At $t=0.9$, the <spinodal points> are instead $x\simeq0.718597189$ and $1.528504964$.

\Image[van-der-waals-critical-coexistence.png]
{id=van-der-waals-critical-coexistence}
{title=<Phase coexistence> and <spinodal> curves meet at the <Van der Waals critical point>}
{description=The horizontal coordinate is reduced <number density> $n/n_c=1/x$, not <molar volume>. The green region is equilibrium <liquid>–<gas> coexistence, bounded by the <binodal>. Hatching marks unstable homogeneous states, and the strips between solid and dashed boundaries mark homogeneous <metastability>. This is a model <phase diagram>, not measured data.}

The equilibrium plateau changes the <phase fraction> rather than the endpoint densities. For a gas particle fraction $f_g=N_g/N$ and mean particle <volume (thermodynamics)> $\bar v=V/N$, the <lever rule> reads
$$
\bar v=(1-f_g)v_l+f_gv_g,\qquad
f_g=\frac{\bar v-v_l}{v_g-v_l}.
$$
Thus $\bar v=v_c$ at $t=0.9$ gives $f_g\simeq0.227219$: this is a particle fraction, not the <volume fraction> occupied by <gas>. Differentiating equality of <chemical potentials> along this model's <phase coexistence curve> yields the same <Clapeyron equation> explained below, $dp_*/dT=\ell/[T(v_g-v_l)]$, with <latent heat> $\ell$ per particle. Both endpoint differences vanish as the <Van der Waals critical point> is approached; the equation's critical limit must be taken as a ratio.

= Andrews's carbon dioxide experiment
{c}
{parent=Van der Waals equation}

The 1869 <The Bakerian Lecture: On the Continuity of the Gaseous and Liquid States of Matter by Thomas Andrews> describes dried <carbon dioxide> trapped above <mercury (element)> in a sealed <capillary tube>. A steel screw pressurized <water> in a strong <copper> tube; movable <mercury (element)> transmitted the load. A second <capillary tube> held air as a <pressure> gauge. Flowing <water> around the visible <capillary tubes> controlled <temperature>; a <thermometer> read the bath. A <cathetometer> read column positions, converted to <volume (thermodynamics)> readings using weighed-<mercury (element)> calibration.<The Bakerian Lecture: On the Continuity of the Gaseous and Liquid States of Matter by Thomas Andrews>[Andrews, pp. 576–578 and Plate LXIII, figures 3 and 5–7: sample preparation, screw, coupled capillaries, water jackets and optical readout]{ref}

Below the critical <temperature>, compression produced a visible <liquid>–<gas> boundary; above it, large density changes occurred without that boundary. Three trials gave $30.92\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$ as his critical <temperature>. Purity checks detected residual air; <mercury (element)> height corrections accounted for unequal <hydrostatic pressures>. He explicitly left air nonideality and pressure-induced tube-capacity corrections unresolved, so his air-gauge <pressures> were approximate. These readouts support a <liquid-gas critical point>; they do not measure a homogeneous unstable loop or establish universal quantitative accuracy of the later <Van der Waals equation>.<The Bakerian Lecture: On the Continuity of the Gaseous and Liquid States of Matter by Thomas Andrews>[Andrews, pp. 576, 578–580 and 583: residual air, approximate air-gauge pressure, hydrostatic corrections and three critical-temperature trials]{ref}

= Equation of state of an ideal ultrarelativistic gas
{parent=Equation of state}

An isotropic ideal gas whose particles obey the ultrarelativistic dispersion $\varepsilon=pc$ satisfies
$$
pV=\frac E3.
$$

= Adiabatic equation of state
{parent=Equation of state}

An adiabatic equation of state relates pressure and volume along a process with no heat transfer. It commonly has the form $pV^\gamma=\text{constant}$.

= Volume
{disambiguate=thermodynamics}
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}

Thermodynamic volume is the <volume> occupied by a thermodynamic system, treated as an <extensive variable>.

= Molar volume
{parent=Volume (thermodynamics)}
{title2=$v_m=V/n$}
{wiki}

The <volume (thermodynamics)> per <amount of substance>, with $n$ measured in <moles>. For a uniform pure substance, $v_m=M/\rho$, where $M$ is its <molar mass> and $\rho$ its <mass density>. The <Clapeyron equation> pairs a difference of <molar volumes> with <latent heat> per <mole>.

= Volume per particle
{parent=Volume (thermodynamics)}
{title2=$v=V/N$}

The <volume (thermodynamics)> divided by the positive number of particles $N$. The <Van der Waals equation> uses this convention for $v$, whereas <specific volume> divides by <mass> and <molar volume> divides by the <amount of substance>. With the <Avogadro constant> $N_A$, the <molar volume> is $v_m=N_Av$.

= Specific volume
{parent=Volume (thermodynamics)}
{title2=$v=V/m=1/\rho$}
{wiki}

The <volume (thermodynamics)> per unit <mass>; for a uniform substance it is the reciprocal of <mass density>. The <Clapeyron equation> pairs a difference of <specific volumes> with <latent heat> per unit <mass>.

= Internal energy
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}
{title2=$U$}

Internal energy $U$ is the <energy> stored in the microscopic <degrees of freedom> of a <thermodynamic system>: random molecular <kinetic energy>, internal molecular excitations, and interaction <potential energy>. Its value requires a specified system and an <energy> reference. In <thermodynamic equilibrium>, <internal energy> is a <state function>; it records the present state, without remembering whether a previous change came from stirring, compression or <heat> transfer. This makes <internal energy> the common accounting quantity connecting mechanical experiments to thermal ones.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/3.2 Work, Heat, and Internal Energy>[§3.2, “Internal Energy”, equation 3.5 and the discussion of molecular motion]{ref}

<Heat> and <work> describe <energy> crossing the system boundary during a process. They are not separately stored contents of the system. For a <closed system> whose bulk <kinetic energy> and external <potential energy> have unchanged endpoint values, the <first law of thermodynamics> gives
$$
\Delta U=Q_{\rm in}+W_{\rm on}=Q_{\rm in}-W_{\rm out}.
$$
Here $Q_{\rm in}$ is positive for incoming <heat>, and $W_{\rm on}=-W_{\rm out}$ is positive for net <work> done on the system. Two routes between the same equilibrium states have the same $\Delta U$, although their <heat> and <work> can differ. The infinitesimal notation $dU=\delta Q_{\rm in}+\delta W_{\rm on}$ distinguishes a <state function> change from path-dependent transfers.<Statistical Physics by David Tong/4 Classical Thermodynamics>[§4.2, equation 4.156 and “Quasi-Static Processes”: state energy versus heat and work; Tong uses work positive inward]{ref}

For a calculated example, take one <mole> of a classical <monatomic gas> in the <ideal gas> regime, at fixed <volume (thermodynamics)>, from $300$ to $310\,\mathrm K$. With stipulated <molar gas constant> $R=8.314\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$, its <internal energy> change is $3nR\Delta T/2=124.71\,\mathrm J$. A purely thermal route supplies $Q_{\rm in}=124.71\,\mathrm J$ with $W_{\rm on}=0$. An ideal insulated stirring route supplies $W_{\rm on}=124.71\,\mathrm J$ with $Q_{\rm in}=0$, measured between equilibrium endpoints after the motion dies away. The same stored increment has two different transfer histories; neither history defines a separate kind of stored <energy>.

\Image[internal-energy-storage-and-transfers.png]
{id=internal-energy-storage-and-transfers}
{title=<Internal energy> is stored; <heat> and <work> are ways to change it}
{description=Original schematic and calculated <ideal gas> example with the stipulated parameters above; no experimental data. The two routes end at the same <temperature> and <volume (thermodynamics)>. Microscopic <kinetic energy> and interaction <potential energy> belong inside $U$; whole-body motion and external elevation are accounted for separately under the stated convention.}

The familiar <internal energy of an ideal gas> is a useful special case. Neglect intermolecular interactions, hold the <amount of substance> $n$ and composition fixed, and suppose that $f$ independent quadratic terms per particle obey the classical <equipartition theorem>. Choosing the constant reference contribution as zero gives
$$
U=\frac f2nRT,\qquad C_V=\frac f2nR.
$$
Here $T$ is absolute <temperature>, $R$ is the <molar gas constant>, and $C_V$ is the whole sample's <heat capacity at constant volume>. A <monatomic gas> has $f=3$ translational terms. A rigid diatomic <molecule> has $f=5$ when its two rotational terms are active and vibration is frozen; activating one <harmonic oscillator> vibration adds two terms, one <kinetic energy> and one <potential energy>, giving $f=7$. The counting is of quadratic contributions, so a vibrational coordinate is not just one contribution.<Statistical Physics by David Tong/2 Classical Gases>[§§2.2.1 and 2.4, equations 2.57–2.58 and the rotation/vibration energy counting]{ref}

This constant-$f$ formula applies only over a regime with those same active terms, classical translational statistics, nonrelativistic motion and unchanged composition. Quantum freezing, <quantum degeneracy>, <chemical reactions> or appreciable intermolecular interactions invalidate that simple counting. More generally, at fixed amount and composition an <ideal gas> can still have $U=U(T)$ with varying <heat capacity at constant volume>, so use $\Delta U=\int_{T_1}^{T_2}C_V(T)\,dT$. A nonzero constant reference adds $U_*$ to $fnRT/2$ without altering $\Delta U$.

= Internal energy and system boundaries
{parent=Internal energy}

For an ordinary stationary laboratory sample, a useful convention is
$$
E_{\rm total}=U+K_{\rm bulk}+\Phi_{\rm ext},\qquad
\Delta U+\Delta K_{\rm bulk}+\Delta\Phi_{\rm ext}
=Q_{\rm in}+W_{\rm on}.
$$
The bulk <kinetic energy> $K_{\rm bulk}$ includes organized whole-body motion; for pure translation it is $Mv_{\rm cm}^2/2$, with total <mass> $M$ and <center of mass> speed $v_{\rm cm}$. The external <potential energy> $\Phi_{\rm ext}$ includes elevation in an imposed <gravitational field>, approximately $Mgz$ in a uniform field. Accelerating a sealed bottle or lifting it without otherwise changing its state changes these mechanical terms, rather than its <internal energy>. Molecular <kinetic energy> is measured relative to the chosen bulk motion.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/3.2 Work, Heat, and Internal Energy>[§3.2, “Internal Energy”: molecular speeds relative to the center of mass; moving-train and roof examples]{ref}

State the convention rather than applying this separation blindly. If an experiment separately tracks a rotating apparatus or an organized flow, its mechanical <kinetic energy> must not also be counted in $U$. In <Joule's paddle-wheel experiment>, waiting for the apparatus and fluid to come to rest removes such an endpoint ambiguity. Including the vessel and paddle changes whose <internal energy> is measured; including the falling weights additionally puts their changing <gravitational potential energy> inside the enlarged system's total <energy> balance.

= Internal energy at constant temperature
{parent=Internal energy}

Constant <temperature> does not generally mean constant <internal energy>. At fixed amount and composition, within one smooth <thermodynamic phase> of a <simple compressible system>, an equilibrium identity is
$$
dU=C_V\,dT+\left[T\left(\frac{\partial p}{\partial T}\right)_V-p\right]dV.
$$
The bracket vanishes for the <ideal gas law> $pV=nRT$. It need not vanish for a <real gas>: changing the <volume (thermodynamics)> changes the average interaction <potential energy>, even when the random-motion contribution stays at the same <temperature>. The identity follows by writing $dU=T\,dS-p\,dV$ and using a <Maxwell relation>; it does not require the actual process to be reversible. <Thermodynamics and Statistical Mechanics by Richard Fitzpatrick/Free Expansion of Gas>{ref}

For a <Van der Waals equation> model with <volume (thermodynamics)> per <mole> $v=V/n$, $p=RT/(v-b)-a/v^2$ gives $(\partial U/\partial V)_{T,n}=a/v^2$. Thus an <isothermal expansion> from $v_1$ to $v_2>v_1$, at fixed <amount of substance> $n$, increases <internal energy> by
$$
\Delta U=na\left(\frac1{v_1}-\frac1{v_2}\right)>0.
$$
Separating attractive particles makes their interaction <potential energy> less negative. For stipulated $n=1\,\mathrm{mol}$, $a=0.10\,\mathrm{Pa\,m^6\,mol^{-2}}$, $b=10^{-5}\,\mathrm{m^3\,mol^{-1}}$, $T=300\,\mathrm K$, $v_1=0.010$ and $v_2=0.020\,\mathrm{m^3\,mol^{-1}}$, the calculated increase for this one-<mole> sample is $5\,\mathrm J$, despite $\Delta T=0$. This dilute stable-branch model example is not a measurement of a named <gas>. <Thermodynamics and Statistical Mechanics by Richard Fitzpatrick/Van der Waals Gas>{ref}

A <phase transition> gives another reason. During equilibrium vaporization of a pure substance at fixed <pressure>, the <temperature> can stay at its <phase coexistence> value while the <phase fraction> changes. Molecular separation changes interaction <potential energy>.<University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/1.5 Phase Changes>[§1.5, figure 1.18 and equations 1.7–1.8: molecular attractions and constant-temperature phase conversion]{ref} If the <closed system> does only <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> <work>, its <latent heat> input obeys
$$
Q_{\rm in}=\Delta H,\qquad
\Delta U=\Delta H-p\Delta V.
$$
Here $H=U+pV$ is <enthalpy>. A vaporization <latent heat> therefore is not automatically the <internal energy> increment: some input supplies expansion <work>. A fixed <temperature> reading alone cannot tell how much <internal energy> changed.

= Joule's paddle-wheel experiment
{c}
{parent=Internal energy}

Joule's paper <On the Mechanical Equivalent of Heat> (1850) describes a <copper> vessel holding <water> and a brass paddle with stationary vanes. Descending <lead> weights drove its shaft through strings and pulleys; the vanes opposed simple co-rotation. A boxwood shaft segment reduced <thermal conduction>. He measured fall distances and initial/final <temperatures>, repeating twenty descents per run and disconnecting the drive for rewinding. Calibrated <thermometers> read the <water> and room.<On the Mechanical Equivalent of Heat by James Prescott Joule>[pp. 64–66 and Plate VII: thermometer calibration, water apparatus and operating sequence]{ref}

\Image[joule-paddle-wheel-calorimeter.png]
{id=joule-paddle-wheel-calorimeter}
{title=<Joule's paddle-wheel experiment>: mechanical input and thermal readout}
{description=Original explanatory schematic, not a scale reconstruction or a plot of measurements. The outlined system includes <water>, vessel and immersed paddle, but excludes the weights and external drive. The symbolic balance separates mechanical losses outside this boundary from <heat> exchange with the room. Rewinding occurs with the drive disconnected.}

His matched resting trials retained the <water> amount, duration, surroundings and reading procedure, omitting paddle motion to estimate environmental <heat> exchange. A screen reduced <thermal radiation> from his body. His calculation included the vessel and paddle <heat capacities>, external pulley/string losses, descent <kinetic energy> and string elasticity; it acknowledged residual vibration and sound.<On the Mechanical Equivalent of Heat by James Prescott Joule>[pp. 66, 69–70 and 82: matched controls, apparatus water-equivalent capacity, mechanical corrections and residual losses]{ref}

The measured observable is a corrected <temperature> rise, interpreted through the participating <heat capacity>; it is not a direct view of molecular motion. The experiment tests whether measured mechanical input accounts quantitatively for thermal storage. Frictional warming by itself would establish neither the conversion factor nor the absence of <heat> exchange. A modern authored demonstration at UCSB instead measures crank turns and a cylinder's <temperature> with an electrical <thermometer>: the apparatus differs, but again both mechanical input and thermal response must be measured. <UCSB Physics Lecture Demonstrations/52.33 -- Mechanical equivalent of heat>{ref}

= Paddle-wheel calorimetry energy balance
{parent=Joule's paddle-wheel experiment}

Choose the <water>, vessel and immersed paddle as the <closed system>, with fixed outer <volume (thermodynamics)> and negligible endpoint changes in bulk mechanical <energy>. Between common equilibrium <temperatures> $T_1$ and $T_2$,
$$
\Delta U_{\rm assembly}=\int_{T_1}^{T_2}C_{\rm eff}(T)\,dT
=W_{\rm shaft,on}+Q_{\rm room,in}.
$$
The effective <heat capacity> $C_{\rm eff}$ includes every component that participates in the common <temperature> change. For nearly constant $C_{\rm eff}$, the readout is $C_{\rm eff}\Delta T$. <Heat> transferred between <water> and paddle stays inside this boundary; it must not be added again as external input. The drive supplies shaft <work> even though the vessel has no expansion <work>.

For a simplified modern model, let $M$ be the total descending <mass>, let each of $N$ descents cover height $h$, and let $W_{\rm loss,out}$ be <energy> diverted by the external drive. With negligible remaining drive <kinetic energy> and elastic storage,
$$
W_{\rm shaft,on}=NMgh-W_{\rm loss,out},\qquad
NMgh=C_{\rm eff}\Delta T+W_{\rm loss,out}+Q_{\rm room,out}.
$$
The signed $Q_{\rm room,out}=-Q_{\rm room,in}$ may be negative if the room warms the assembly. This model omits the small endpoint and elastic corrections explicitly considered in the historical analysis. It does not equate all of the weights' lost <gravitational potential energy> with the <water>'s gained <internal energy>.

As a proposed modern protocol, measure the falling <masses> and distances, calibrate the <thermometer> and $C_{\rm eff}$ independently, record room <temperature> and resting drift before and after each run, and wait for fluid motion to decay before the final equilibrium reading. Repeat with different fall budgets and run durations. A stable ratio of corrected input to corrected thermal storage supports the <first law of thermodynamics> over the tested range. Resting controls only estimate leakage if their thermal conditions represent the driven run; sensor lag, nonuniform <temperature>, unmeasured drive losses, evaporation or changing <heat capacity> can spoil the inference. Those are limits of the measurement, rather than evidence that <heat> was stored as a separate substance.

= Specific internal energy
{parent=Internal energy}
{title2=$e$}

Specific <internal energy> is <internal energy> per unit mass. For a <perfect gas> with constant <specific-heat ratio>, $e=p/[(\gamma-1)\rho]$. Its material balance is $\rho De/Dt=-p\nabla\cdot\mathbf u+Q$, where $Q$ is <heat> supplied per unit volume per unit time.

= Thermodynamic potential
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}

A <state function> used to describe a <thermodynamic system> with a chosen set of natural variables. <Internal energy>, <enthalpy>, <Helmholtz free energy>, and <Gibbs free energy> use different variables and serve different environmental constraints.

= Thermodynamic free energy
{parent=Thermodynamic potential}
{wiki}

= Free energy
{synonym}

A <free energy> is a <thermodynamic potential> that compares possible states under specified environmental constraints. For a <closed system> of fixed composition, treated as a <simple compressible system> with only <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> <work>, stable <thermodynamic equilibrium> minimizes <Helmholtz free energy> at fixed <temperature> and <volume (thermodynamics)>, or <Gibbs free energy> at fixed <temperature> and <pressure>. The choice follows the experiment: a rigid sealed vessel and a sealed cylinder with a freely moving loaded piston impose different constraints. Both may exchange <heat> with a <thermal reservoir>. See https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/3-00-thermodynamics-of-materials-fall-2002/14c95abd6fac023d84bd33c5d74d787d_lecture_02_oneside.PDF[W. C. Carter, MIT thermodynamics notes, sections 2-1.14 and 2-1.19].

\Image[thermodynamic-potential-constraints.png]
{title=Choose <Helmholtz free energy> for fixed <temperature> and <volume (thermodynamics)>, or <Gibbs free energy> for fixed <temperature> and <pressure>}
{description=Original schematic of two closed vessels in contact with a <thermal reservoir>. Composition is fixed, and only <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> <work> is allowed. The right piston permits the <volume (thermodynamics)> to change.}

The useful insight is that <internal energy> alone cannot rank these alternatives: exchanging <heat> also changes the surroundings' <entropy>. Subtracting $TS$ packages that accounting into a property of the chosen system. Adding $pV$ additionally accounts for displacing the surroundings at fixed <pressure>. A <free energy> is a <state function>; supplied <heat> describes a transfer during a process. Under the stated constraints, supplied <heat> is $Q=\Delta U$ at fixed <volume (thermodynamics)> or $Q=\Delta H$ at fixed <pressure>, whereas neither $\Delta F$ nor $\Delta G$ generally equals $Q$.

A lower <free energy> identifies a thermodynamic preference, without specifying a timescale. A barrier can trap a system in a <local minimum>, producing <metastability> although a lower <global minimum> exists. The <enzyme> example illustrates the corresponding distinction for <chemical reactions>: changing the route can change the <reaction rate> while preserving the endpoint <Gibbs free energy> difference and <chemical equilibrium>.

= Free-energy functional
{parent=Thermodynamic free energy}
{title2=$\mathcal A[m]$}

A <free-energy functional> assigns a <free energy> to an entire spatial <order parameter> configuration rather than only to one uniform value. In <Landau-Ginzburg theory>, a <local derivative expansion> gives $\mathcal A[m]=\int d^Dx\{\kappa(\nabla m)^2/2+V(m)\}$, with appropriate boundary terms and regular backgrounds. The <Landau approximation> minimizes this functional; a fluctuating <statistical field theory> instead integrates its <Boltzmann weight> over configurations.
