These procedures need not give identical <critical exponents>.

= Helmholtz free energy
{title2=$F$}
{parent=Thermodynamic free energy}
{c}
{wiki}

= Helmholtz energy
{c}
{synonym}

The <Helmholtz free energy> is
$$
F=U-TS,
$$
where $U$ is <internal energy>, also denoted $E$ elsewhere here, $T$ is absolute <temperature>, and $S$ is <entropy>. For the <closed system> of fixed composition and only <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> <work> described above, spontaneous relaxation at fixed <temperature> and <volume (thermodynamics)> cannot increase $F$. Stable <thermodynamic equilibrium> is a <global minimum> over the permitted alternatives, including different <thermodynamic phases>. A rigid vessel fixes the total <volume (thermodynamics)>; it does not require each coexisting <thermodynamic phase> to retain its own <volume (thermodynamics)>.

For a constructed comparison at $300\,\mathrm K$, suppose one candidate has $1.5\,\mathrm{kJ}$ more <internal energy> but $6\,\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}$ more <entropy> than another at the same <temperature> and <volume (thermodynamics)>. Its difference is
$$
\Delta F=1500-300(6)=-300\,\mathrm J.
$$
Thus the candidate with higher <internal energy> has lower <Helmholtz free energy>. This is the practical reason for retaining the <entropy> term when selecting a state.

For equilibrium states within a smooth branch of fixed composition, the <first law of thermodynamics> gives $dU=T\,dS-p\,dV$, hence
$$
dF=-S\,dT-p\,dV,\qquad
S=-\left(\frac{\partial F}{\partial T}\right)_V,\qquad
p=-\left(\frac{\partial F}{\partial V}\right)_T.
$$
These <derivatives> extract <entropy> and <pressure>; the differential is not an evolution equation for relaxation. If <chemical species> amounts $n_i$ vary, add $\sum_i\mu_i\,dn_i$, with molar <chemical potentials> $\mu_i$. A <closed system> can still change <chemical species> amounts through <chemical reactions>; the fixed-composition simplification then needs the conserved-inventory constraints of <chemical equilibrium>. See https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/5-60-thermodynamics-kinetics-spring-2008/e763a06aa3b5de09cd6bfea6280f219c_5_60_lecture14.pdf[MIT 5.60, Lecture 14, fundamental equations and chemical potentials].

The minimum principle has a short explanation. At fixed <volume (thermodynamics)>, no boundary <work> occurs, so $Q=\Delta U$. A <thermal reservoir> at positive <temperature> $T$ loses <entropy> $Q/T$. Consequently
$$
\Delta S_{\rm total}=\Delta S-\frac{\Delta U}{T}=-\frac{\Delta F}{T}\geq0,
$$
by the <second law of thermodynamics>. Here both endpoint states share the imposed <temperature>. See https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/5-60-thermodynamics-kinetics-spring-2008/64efc6f359b20c43dab61fc77791d744_5_60_lecture12.pdf[MIT 5.60, Lecture 12, criteria for spontaneous change].

The microscopic counterpart is $F=-k_BT\ln Z$ in the <canonical ensemble>, with <Boltzmann constant> $k_B$ and <partition function> $Z$. The <partition function> examples compute the balance between <internal energy> and <entropy>; the <chemical equilibrium> example already displays an ideal-mixture <Helmholtz free energy> minimum.

= Gibbs free energy
{parent=Thermodynamic free energy}
{c}
{title2=$G$}
{wiki}

= Gibbs energy
{c}
{synonym}

The <Gibbs free energy> is
$$
G=U-TS+pV=H-TS,\qquad H=U+pV,
$$
where $H$ is <enthalpy>. For a <closed system> of fixed composition, with only <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> <work>, spontaneous relaxation at fixed <temperature> and <pressure> cannot increase $G$; stable <thermodynamic equilibrium> minimizes it over the allowed states. A loaded piston can move while maintaining the imposed <pressure>, so the <enthalpy> term includes the displacement cost. Along a smooth equilibrium branch of fixed composition,
$$
dG=-S\,dT+V\,dp.
$$
Thus its <temperature> slope at fixed <pressure> is $-S$, while its <pressure> slope at fixed <temperature> is $V$. See https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/5-60-thermodynamics-kinetics-spring-2008/7bc33304ab1db561a64b4a6b12e25476_5_60_lecture13.pdf[MIT 5.60, Lecture 13, Gibbs free energy and its derivatives].

Consider two illustrative <thermodynamic phases>, A and B, of the same substance at one fixed <pressure>. Write B minus A, using quantities per <mole>:
$$
\Delta H=12\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}},\qquad
\Delta S=40\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}=0.040\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}.
$$
Treat these differences as approximately constant over a limited <temperature> window. Then
$$
\frac{\Delta G(T)}{\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}}=12-0.040\frac{T}{\mathrm K},\qquad
T_* =\frac{\Delta H}{\Delta S}=300\,\mathrm K.
$$
At $280\,\mathrm K$, $\Delta G=+0.8\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, so A is preferred. At $320\,\mathrm K$, $\Delta G=-0.8\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, so B is preferred. At $300\,\mathrm K$, both have equal molar <Gibbs free energy>, permitting <phase coexistence> in this bulk model. B's greater <entropy> outweighs its greater <enthalpy> above the crossing.

\Image[free-energy-temperature-crossing.png]
{title=Illustrative molar <Gibbs free energy> difference crosses zero at $300\,\mathrm K$}
{description=Constructed B-minus-A values: $+0.8\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ at $280\,\mathrm K$ and $-0.8\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ at $320\,\mathrm K$. The constants approximate a limited <temperature> window and represent no measured substance.}

Each plotted point compares alternatives at the same <temperature>; moving along the curve changes the imposed <temperature>. The model predicts preference, not instantaneous conversion: <nucleation> barriers can sustain <metastability>. Real <enthalpy> and <entropy> differences generally vary with <temperature> through <heat capacities>, bending this line outside the approximation.

At fixed <pressure>, the <first law of thermodynamics> gives $Q=\Delta H$ under the stated <work> restriction, so the same <thermal reservoir> accounting yields $\Delta S_{\rm total}=-\Delta G/T\geq0$. Variable composition restores $\sum_i\mu_i\,dn_i$ to $dG$; <constrained Gibbs minimization for chemical equilibrium> handles those permitted changes.

The historical foundation is Gibbs's https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Scientific_Papers_of_Josiah_Willard_Gibbs,_Volume_1/Chapter_IIIa[On the Equilibrium of Heterogeneous Substances, first part completed in 1876, continued in 1878]{ref}. Its equations (87) and (91) introduce the combinations now called <Helmholtz free energy> and <Gibbs free energy>, connecting <entropy> criteria, <chemical potentials> and <phase coexistence> within one framework.

= Gibbs free energy of an ideal-gas mixture
{c}
{parent=Gibbs free energy}

For a single-phase <ideal gas> mixture with molar amounts $N_i$, common <temperature> $T$, and <pressure> $P$, extensivity and ideal mixing give
$$
G=\sum_iN_i\mu_i=N\sum_i x_i\left[\mu_i^\circ(T)+\mathcal RT\log\left(\frac{x_iP}{P^\circ}\right)\right].
$$
Here $N=\sum_iN_i$, $x_i$ is the <mole fraction>, $\mathcal R$ is the molar gas constant, and $P^\circ$ is the standard <pressure>. Integrating $\partial\mu_i/\partial P=\mathcal RT/P$ and including the ideal mixing <entropy> proves $\mu_i=\mu_i^\circ+\mathcal RT\log(x_iP/P^\circ)$. Reactions can change $N$, so fractions alone do not set the extensive <Gibbs free energy>. Its composition Hessian is $\mathcal RT(\delta_{ij}/N_i-1/N)$; it is positive semidefinite by the weighted <Cauchy-Schwarz inequality>.

= Gibbs free-energy Maxwell relation
{c}
{parent=Gibbs free energy}

Equality of mixed derivatives of $dG=-S\,dT+V\,dP$ gives
$$
\left(\frac{\partial S}{\partial P}\right)_T
=-\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_P.
$$

= Chemical-potential balance for a reaction
{parent=Gibbs free energy}

For a reaction with stoichiometric changes $\nu_i$, varying the reaction extent at fixed temperature and pressure gives
$$
dG=\left(\sum_i\nu_i\mu_i\right)d\xi.
$$
Equilibrium requires $\sum_i\nu_i\mu_i=0$.

= Thermochemical equilibrium
{parent=Chemical-potential balance for a reaction}

Thermochemical equilibrium requires every chemical reaction to have zero Gibbs free-energy change. For ideal reactants, the equilibrium constant is $K=\exp[-\Delta_rG^\circ/(RT)]$ after standard-state activity factors are included.

= Constrained Gibbs minimization for chemical equilibrium
{parent=Thermochemical equilibrium}

At fixed <temperature> and <pressure>, a closed reacting system reaches <thermochemical equilibrium> by minimizing <Gibbs free energy> over nonnegative species amounts subject to conserved elemental abundances. For an allowed <stoichiometric vector> $\nu$, an interior minimum has $\sum_i\nu_i\mu_i=0$. More generally the <chemical potential> vector lies in the row span of the elemental conservation matrix at positive concentrations, with the appropriate one-sided inequalities for absent species. The <first law of thermodynamics> gives the thermodynamic differential, while the <second law of thermodynamics> supplies the minimization direction.

= Reverse reaction rate from equilibrium thermodynamics
{parent=Thermochemical equilibrium}

For an elementary reversible reaction, detailed balance gives $k_f\prod_r n_r^{\nu_r}=k_r\prod_p n_p^{\nu_p}$ at equilibrium. Hence the reverse rate coefficient is the forward coefficient divided by the equilibrium constant, with units fixed by the reaction stoichiometry.

= Phase coexistence curve
{parent=Gibbs free energy}

Two phases coexist where their molar Gibbs free energies agree. Differentiating that equality along the coexistence curve relates its slope to the entropy and volume jumps.

= Triple point
{parent=Phase coexistence curve}

= Vapor pressure
{parent=Phase coexistence curve}
{title2=$p_{\rm sat}(T)$}
{wiki}

= Saturation pressure
{synonym}

The equilibrium <pressure> of a vapor in contact with its <liquid> or <solid> at a specified <temperature>. For pure <liquid>–<gas> <phase coexistence> with negligible interface effects, it is the <pressure> on their <phase coexistence curve>. In a <gas> <mixture>, distinguish the component's <partial pressure> from the total <pressure>; the pure-substance <integrated Clausius-Clapeyron relation> does not describe the total <pressure> of arbitrary humid air.

= Maxwell construction
{c}
{parent=Phase coexistence curve}

= Maxwell equal-area construction
{c}
{synonym}

At fixed <temperature>, choose a coexistence <pressure> $p_*$ such that the gas and liquid have equal <chemical potentials>. Because $d\mu=v\,dp$, this gives $\int_{v_l}^{v_g}v\,dp=0$, or equivalently $\int_{v_l}^{v_g}[p(v,T)-p_*]\,dv=0$. The equal areas replace the unstable part of an isotherm by a horizontal coexistence segment.

= Phase diagram
{parent=Phase coexistence curve}
{wiki}

A phase diagram partitions a space of thermodynamic variables into regions occupied by different equilibrium phases and records the coexistence curves between them.

= Solidus
{parent=Phase diagram}
{title2=$T_S(C)$}

The solidus bounds the entirely solid equilibrium region of a <phase diagram>. For a linear <liquidus> $T_L(C_l)=-mC_l$ and a constant <segregation coefficient> $C_s=k_D C_l$, the corresponding solidus is $T_S(C_s)=-mC_s/k_D$. The <concentration> argument is the solid <concentration>, not the neighboring liquid <concentration>.

= Lever rule
{parent=Phase diagram}
{title2=$\phi=(\overline C-C_s)/(C_l-C_s)$}
{wiki}

The lever rule finds phase fractions from a horizontal tie line on a <phase diagram>. For solid and liquid <concentrations> $C_s,C_l$ and bulk <concentration> $\overline C$, conservation gives $\overline C=(1-\phi)C_s+\phi C_l$. Thus $\phi=(\overline C-C_s)/(C_l-C_s)$ is the <liquid fraction>, provided $C_s\le\overline C\le C_l$. For pure solid, $\phi=\overline C/C_l$.

= Bicritical point
{parent=Phase diagram}

A bicritical point is where two continuous transition lines and a first-order line meet, typically separating a disordered phase from two competing ordered phases.

= Liquidus
{parent=Phase diagram}
{wiki}

The liquidus is the locus in a <phase diagram> above which the equilibrium state is entirely liquid. In a dilute binary solution it is often approximated locally by a linear relation such as $T_L=T_0-mC$.

= Freezing-point depression
{parent=Liquidus}
{wiki}

Freezing-point depression is the lowering of a solvent's equilibrium freezing temperature by dissolved solute. In the linear dilute approximation, $T_L=T_0-mC$ with $m>0$.

= Eutectic system
{parent=Phase diagram}
{wiki}

An eutectic system has a composition and temperature at which a liquid is in equilibrium with two distinct solid phases. Cooling liquid of that composition through the eutectic temperature produces both solid phases together.

= Eutectic composition
{parent=Eutectic system}
{title2=$C_E$}

The eutectic composition is the liquid <concentration> at the <eutectic temperature>, where liquid and two solid phases coexist. It is a composition coordinate on a <phase diagram>, not the overall <concentration> of every sample in an <eutectic system>.

= Eutectic temperature
{title2=$T_E$}
{parent=Eutectic system}

The eutectic temperature is the temperature of simultaneous liquid–two-solid <phase coexistence> in an <eutectic system>. In directional freezing of a solution away from the eutectic composition, a <mushy layer> can end at this temperature, where its remaining liquid solidifies.

= First-order phase transition
{parent=Phase coexistence curve}

At a first-order phase transition, the Gibbs free energy is continuous while at least one first derivative, such as entropy or volume, jumps. A nonzero entropy jump produces latent heat $L=T\Delta S$.

= Two-phase scalar coexistence endpoint conditions
{parent=First-order phase transition}
{title2=$V'=V''=V^{(3)}=0,\quad V^{(4)}>0$}

A smooth scalar <Landau free energy> can end a two-phase <phase coexistence curve> by merging two minima and their intervening maximum. At a generic stable endpoint, its first three derivatives vanish and its fourth derivative is positive. A field shift and two mixed control parameters reduce the local form to a quartic <Landau free energy>; the order-parameter jump then vanishes and the <magnetic susceptibility> diverges. This conclusion assumes there is no competing third phase and that the endpoint lies inside the control-parameter domain. It is not a general theorem about every first-order line: a sextic potential with fixed negative quartic coefficient has a symmetry-related ordered coexistence line that ends at a triple point with a finite jump.

= Spin-flop transition
{parent=First-order phase transition}

= Classical nucleation theory
{parent=First-order phase transition}
{wiki}

Classical nucleation theory balances the positive surface free energy of a droplet against its negative bulk free-energy gain. In three dimensions,
$$
F(R)=4\pi\sigma R^2-\frac{4\pi}{3}\Delta R^3
$$
has critical radius $R^*=2\sigma/\Delta$ and barrier $F^*=16\pi\sigma^3/(3\Delta^2)$.

= Critical nucleus
{parent=Classical nucleation theory}

A critical nucleus is the unstable droplet at the maximum of the nucleation free energy. Smaller droplets shrink, while larger droplets lower their free energy by growing.

= Arrhenius nucleation time
{parent=Classical nucleation theory}

Thermal crossing of a nucleation barrier $F^*$ has rate proportional to $e^{-F^*/(k_BT)}$ and mean waiting time proportional to $e^{F^*/(k_BT)}$, up to a kinetic prefactor.

= Latent heat
{parent=Phase coexistence curve}
{title2=$L=T\Delta S=\Delta H$}
{wiki}

Latent heat is the <heat> absorbed or released in a <first-order phase transition> through its <entropy> jump. For an equilibrium conversion from <thermodynamic phase> $\alpha$ to <thermodynamic phase> $\beta$ at fixed <pressure>, evaluate both <thermodynamic phases> at the same <temperature> and <pressure> on their <phase coexistence curve>:
$$
L=T_{\rm tr}(S_\beta-S_\alpha)=H_\beta-H_\alpha.
$$
Use one normalization throughout: $L$, $S$ and $H$ may all be per <mole>, giving $L$ in $\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}$, or all per unit <mass>, giving $L$ in $\mathrm{J\,kg^{-1}}$. Here $S$ is <entropy> and $H$ is <enthalpy> on that basis. Equal <Gibbs free energies> of the coexisting <thermodynamic phases> give $\Delta H=T_{\rm tr}\Delta S$. Identifying this <enthalpy> difference with supplied <heat> assumes a <closed system> and an equilibrium conversion with only <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> <work>. <Statistical Physics by David Tong>[Tong, Statistical Physics, phase coexistence and latent heat]{ref}

For a pure substance with positive <latent heat> per unit <mass> $L$, let $x$ be the <phase fraction> of the higher-<enthalpy> <thermodynamic phase>, measured by <mass>. While both <thermodynamic phases> coexist at the fixed <pressure>,
$$
Q=mL(x_2-x_1),\qquad T=T_{\rm tr}.
$$
Adding <heat> changes $x$ rather than the <temperature>; this creates the horizontal portion of a <heating curve>. Once $x=1$, additional <heat> raises the <temperature> according to <specific heat capacity at constant pressure>. Reversing the conversion releases <heat>. The plateau represents changing <enthalpy>, rather than a finite single-<thermodynamic phase> <heat capacity> satisfying $Q=C_p\Delta T$. <University Physics Volume 2 by Samuel J. Ling and others/1.5 Phase Changes>{ref}

For a constructed fixed-<pressure> model, take $m=1\,\mathrm{kg}$, initial <temperature> $280\,\mathrm K$, transition <temperature> $T_{\rm tr}=300\,\mathrm K$, <solid> <specific heat capacity at constant pressure> $c_s=2\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$, <latent heat> $L=100\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}}$, and <liquid> <specific heat capacity at constant pressure> $c_l=3\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. These are stipulated model parameters, not empirical properties of a named material. Heating the <solid> to $300\,\mathrm K$ takes $40\,\mathrm{kJ}$ of <sensible heat>; converting it fully to <liquid> takes another $100\,\mathrm{kJ}$ of <heat>; heating the <liquid> to $320\,\mathrm K$ takes $60\,\mathrm{kJ}$. The total is $200\,\mathrm{kJ}$. After a total <heat> input of $90\,\mathrm{kJ}$, $x=(90-40)/100=1/2$ and the <temperature> is still $300\,\mathrm K$. The corresponding per-unit-<mass> <entropy> jump is $L/T_{\rm tr}=\frac13\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$.

\Image[sensible-and-latent-heat.png]
{title=<Sensible heat> raises <temperature>; <latent heat> changes <phase fraction>}
{description=Constructed $1\,\mathrm{kg}$ fixed-<pressure> model with total supplied <heat> starting at zero at $280\,\mathrm K$. The plateau runs from $40$ to $140\,\mathrm{kJ}$, while the <liquid> <phase fraction> by <mass> rises from zero to one. The horizontal axis measures $Q=\Delta H$, rather than absolute <internal energy>.}

A <continuous phase transition> has no <latent heat>. Its <heat capacity> can have a jump or a singularity without a finite interval in which added <heat> converts coexisting <thermodynamic phases> at a constant <temperature>. A heating plateau therefore requires the stated <phase coexistence> conditions; it is not a feature of every <phase transition>. <Statistical Physics by David Tong>[Tong, Statistical Physics, first-order and continuous transitions]{ref}

= Stefan number
{c}
{title2=$\mathrm{Ste}=c_p\Delta T/L$}
{parent=Latent heat}
{wiki}

The Stefan number compares sensible heat $c_p\Delta T$ with <latent heat> $L$ per unit mass. Some solidification literature uses its reciprocal as the Stefan parameter, so the defining formula must accompany the convention.

= Latent-to-sensible heat ratio
{title2=$S=L/(c_p\Delta T)$}
{parent=Stefan number}

= Inverse Stefan number
{synonym}

The latent-to-sensible heat ratio is the reciprocal of the <Stefan number> defined by $\mathrm{Ste}=c_p\Delta T/L$. Large $S$ means that phase change uses much more energy than changing temperature by $\Delta T$.

= Clausius-Clapeyron relation
{parent=Latent heat}
{c}
{wiki=Clausius–Clapeyron_relation}
{title2=$dp/dT=L/(T\Delta v)$}

= Clapeyron equation
{c}
{synonym}

= Clausius-Clapeyron equation
{c}
{synonym}

The <Clapeyron equation> connects the slope of a <phase coexistence curve> to the <latent heat> and the change in <volume (thermodynamics)> during a <first-order phase transition>. A steep boundary can indicate a large <latent heat>, a small change in <volume (thermodynamics)>, or both. This lets a measured boundary constrain caloric properties and predicts how changing <pressure> shifts a <melting> <temperature>.

For a pure substance in bulk <phase coexistence>, label the initial and final <thermodynamic phases> $\alpha$ and $\beta$. Both have the same absolute <temperature> $T$, <pressure> $p$, and <chemical potential>. With $s$, $h$, and $v$ denoting <entropy>, <enthalpy>, and <volume (thermodynamics)> on one common basis, define $\Delta s=s_\beta-s_\alpha$, $\Delta h=h_\beta-h_\alpha$, and $\Delta v=v_\beta-v_\alpha$. Then
$$
\boxed{\frac{dp}{dT}=\frac{\Delta s}{\Delta v}=\frac{L}{T\Delta v}},\qquad L=\Delta h=T\Delta s.
$$
The <derivative> follows the <phase coexistence curve>; it is not a <partial derivative> within one <thermodynamic phase>. Use <molar volume> $v_m$ with molar <latent heat> $L_m$ in $\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}$, or <specific volume> $v$ with <latent heat> $L$ in $\mathrm{J\,kg^{-1}}$. Both give $dp/dT$ in $\mathrm{Pa\,K^{-1}}$. For <molar mass> $M$, $L_m=ML$ and $\Delta v_m=M\Delta v$, so the predicted slope is independent of the chosen basis. This formulation assumes a <simple compressible system>, fixed composition, and negligible interface contributions; a curved interface can instead require the <Young–Laplace equation>.

For positive <latent heat> in the stated conversion direction, the sign of the slope is the sign of $\Delta v$. Converting <liquid> to <gas> normally increases <volume (thermodynamics)> and gives a positive slope. The <negative melting-point slope of water> illustrates the opposite sign. At a <thermodynamic critical point>, the coexisting <gas> and <liquid> merge: both $L$ and $\Delta v$ vanish, so their ratio needs a limiting analysis rather than substitution of zero into the <Clapeyron equation>.<Statistical Physics by David Tong>[§5.1.2, equation (5.185) and the discussion of the critical endpoint]{ref}

\Image[clapeyron-coexistence-slopes-and-volume-jump.png]
{title=<Phase coexistence curve> tangents: positive vaporization slope and negative <water> <melting> slope}

A short derivation uses the molar <Gibbs free energy> $g_m$, equal to the <chemical potential> for a pure substance. Equality persists along the boundary, so $dg_{m,\alpha}=dg_{m,\beta}$ with $dg_m=-s_m\,dT+v_m\,dp$. Therefore $-\Delta s_m\,dT+\Delta v_m\,dp=0$. Equality of $g_m=h_m-Ts_m$ also gives $\Delta h_m=T\Delta s_m$. Eliminating $\Delta s_m$ yields the <Clapeyron equation>.

= Ideal-vapor Clausius-Clapeyron approximation
{c}
{parent=Clausius-Clapeyron relation}
{title2=$d\ln p/dT\simeq L_m/(RT^2)$}

For <liquid>–<gas> <phase coexistence>, make two distinct approximations: the <gas> obeys the <ideal gas law>, $v_{m,g}=RT/p$, and its <molar volume> greatly exceeds the <liquid> <molar volume>, $v_{m,g}\gg v_{m,l}$. Here $R$ is the <molar gas constant> and $p$ is the <saturation pressure> of the pure substance. Substitution into the <Clapeyron equation> gives
$$
\frac{dp}{dT}\simeq\frac{L_m(T)p}{RT^2},\qquad \frac{d\ln(p/p_*)}{dT}\simeq\frac{L_m(T)}{RT^2},
$$
where $p_*>0$ is any fixed reference <pressure>, making the <logarithm> dimensionless. Constant <latent heat> is a further approximation, needed for the elementary <integrated Clausius-Clapeyron relation> below.<Statistical Physics by David Tong>[§5.1.2, “An Approximate Solution to the Clausius-Clapeyron Equation”: the three assumptions and the exponential solution]{ref}

The neglected <liquid> <molar volume> has a transparent error estimate. If the <gas> is ideal but $\epsilon=v_{m,l}/v_{m,g}$ is retained, then
$$
\left(\frac{dp}{dT}\right)_{\rm full}=\frac{L_m p}{RT^2(1-\epsilon)},\qquad \frac{(dp/dT)_{\rm approx}}{(dp/dT)_{\rm full}}=1-\epsilon.
$$
Thus ignoring the <liquid> <molar volume> underestimates this slope by a relative amount $\epsilon$. This estimate does not bound errors from treating a <real gas> as ideal or from freezing $L_m(T)$. Near a <thermodynamic critical point>, the <gas> and <liquid> <molar volumes> approach each other and these dilute-vapor assumptions fail.

= Integrated Clausius-Clapeyron relation
{c}
{parent=Ideal-vapor Clausius-Clapeyron approximation}
{title2=$\ln(p_2/p_1)\simeq-(L_m/R)(1/T_2-1/T_1)$}

If the molar <latent heat> $L_m$ is approximately constant on the chosen <temperature> interval, the <ideal-vapor Clausius-Clapeyron approximation> integrates to
$$
\ln\frac{p_2}{p_1}\simeq-\frac{L_m}{R}\left(\frac{1}{T_2}-\frac{1}{T_1}\right),\qquad p(T)\simeq p_1\exp\left[\frac{L_m}{R}\left(\frac{1}{T_1}-\frac{1}{T}\right)\right].
$$
For $L_m>0$, higher absolute <temperature> gives higher <saturation pressure>. A plot of $\ln(p/p_1)$ against <inverse temperature> $1/T$ is a straight line of slope $-L_m/R$. One reference pair $(T_1,p_1)$ fixes its intercept. On a per-unit-<mass> basis replace $L_m/R$ by $L/R_s$, with $R_s=R/M$ and $M$ the <molar mass>; using $L$ in $\mathrm{J\,kg^{-1}}$ with the molar $R$ would give incompatible units.

If only the constant-<latent heat> approximation is relaxed, the same ideal-vapor model instead gives
$$
\ln\frac{p_2}{p_1}\simeq\frac{1}{R}\int_{T_1}^{T_2}\frac{L_m(T)}{T^2}\,dT.
$$
Consequently an apparent straight-line fit across a broad <temperature> range need not represent a single material constant. The simple exponential also approaches a finite value as $T\to\infty$; this is a property of the stipulated formula, not a prediction that <liquid>–<gas> <phase coexistence> extends beyond a <thermodynamic critical point>.

= Stipulated vapor-pressure calculation
{parent=Integrated Clausius-Clapeyron relation}

Consider an unnamed model substance with molar <latent heat> $L_m=40.0\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ and <saturation pressure> $p_1=10.0\,\mathrm{kPa}$ at $T_1=300\,\mathrm K$. Stipulate the <ideal-vapor Clausius-Clapeyron approximation> throughout $280\leq T\leq340\,\mathrm K$, constant $L_m$, and $R=8.314\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. These parameters define an illustrative model, not a measured dataset or a fit to <water>. At $T_2=330\,\mathrm K$,
$$
\ln\frac{p_2}{p_1}=\frac{40000}{8.314}\left(\frac{1}{300}-\frac{1}{330}\right)\simeq1.458,\qquad p_2\simeq42.97\,\mathrm{kPa}.
$$
At the reference state the <gas> <molar volume> is $RT_1/p_1=0.24942\,\mathrm{m^3\,mol^{-1}}$, and the local slope is $dp/dT\simeq0.5346\,\mathrm{kPa\,K^{-1}}$. The tangent estimate $p_2\approx10.0+30(0.5346)=26.04\,\mathrm{kPa}$ is much lower than the integrated result: a local <derivative> should not be extrapolated over this whole interval. By contrast, over a small <temperature> increment, the fractional sensitivity is $d\ln(p/p_1)/dT=L_m/(RT_1^2)\simeq0.05346\,\mathrm{K^{-1}}$.

\Image[ideal-vapor-pressure-and-inverse-temperature.png]
{title=Stipulated <saturation pressure> versus <temperature> and its linear <logarithm> versus <inverse temperature>}

= Latent heat from a vapor-pressure slope
{parent=Integrated Clausius-Clapeyron relation}
{title2=$L_m\simeq-R\,d\ln(p/p_*)/d(1/T)$}

Within the <ideal-vapor Clausius-Clapeyron approximation>, a local slope of the <natural logarithm> of <saturation pressure> against <inverse temperature> estimates the molar <latent heat>:
$$
L_m(T)\simeq-R\frac{d\ln(p/p_*)}{d(1/T)}.
$$
In the <stipulated vapor-pressure calculation>, the slope is $-4811.16\,\mathrm K$, which recovers $40.0\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$. For variable $L_m(T)$, a two-point estimate gives an average weighted by $T^{-2}$, rather than the <latent heat> at an arbitrary midpoint. Differentiating noisy <pressure> measurements amplifies their errors. Outside the ideal-vapor regime, use independently determined <molar volumes> in $L_m=T\Delta v_m\,dp/dT$; a straight-looking graph alone cannot establish that the <gas> is ideal.

= Saturation-pressure equilibrium measurement
{parent=Clausius-Clapeyron relation}

A concrete experiment turns the <phase coexistence curve> into pairs of <temperature> and <saturation pressure>. A 1969 paper reports <water> measurements made in 1942 and 1947–1949 using a boiler and precision <mercury (element)> <manometer>. <Platinum resistance thermometers> read the boiler <temperature> through a <resistance bridge>. The <pressure>-transfer line passed through an ice-water condenser and a dry-ice trap; <helium> filled connecting sections. Corrected <mercury (element)> column height gave the <pressure> at the thermometer coils, including <hydrostatic pressure> contributions from <water> vapor, <helium>, <mercury (element)>, and <mercury (element)> vapor.<Some Precise Measurements of the Vapor Pressure of Water in the Range From 25 to 100 °C by H. F. Stimson>[Stimson, §§1–2, pp. 493–495: boiler, bridge thermometry, and fluid-head corrections]{ref}

Controls included normal/reversed-lead readings, readings at two thermometer <electric currents> extrapolated to zero current to remove self-heating, repeated bridge calibration, and daily <triple point> <thermometer calibration> checks. Cellar <temperature> measurements corrected fluid-column and gauge-block <thermal expansion>. One measurement series was excluded because a trapped gas bubble was suspected of corrupting the <mercury (element)> head. The paper adjusted measurements to round <temperatures> using previously tabulated slopes; those adjusted values therefore are not independent measurements of those slopes.<Some Precise Measurements of the Vapor Pressure of Water in the Range From 25 to 100 °C by H. F. Stimson>[Stimson, §2, pp. 494–495, and §3, p. 495: corrections and suspected gas-bubble bias]{ref}

The thermodynamic inference requires <liquid> and <gas> in <phase coexistence>, a well-defined common <temperature>, and a <pressure> corrected to that same location. Leaks, contamination, or a <temperature gradient> can spoil that interpretation. A separate <calorimetry> measurement of <latent heat>, together with the two <specific volumes>, allows a test of the full <Clapeyron equation>. Converting a measured <phase coexistence curve> slope directly into <latent heat> instead assumes that equation; it is not an independent test.

= Common-pressure melting-point slope
{parent=Clausius-Clapeyron relation}
{title2=$dT_m/dp=-T_m(1/\rho_s-1/\rho_l)/L$}

When the solid and liquid have the same <pressure>, equality of <chemical potentials> implies $-(s_l-s_s)dT+(1/\rho_l-1/\rho_s)dp=0$. With <latent heat of fusion> $L=T_m(s_l-s_s)$, this gives the displayed slope. If solid is less dense than liquid, raising the common pressure depresses the <melting> temperature. $T_m$ is absolute <temperature>. This coexistence-curve derivative differs from a thermomolecular pressure difference between equal-density phases.

= Negative melting-point slope of water
{parent=Common-pressure melting-point slope}

For a local illustrative <ice>–<water> <melting> calculation, stipulate $T_m=273.15\,\mathrm K$, <latent heat of fusion> $L=334\,\mathrm{kJ\,kg^{-1}}$, <solid> <mass density> $\rho_s=917\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, and <liquid> <mass density> $\rho_l=1000\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$. These are explicitly stipulated rounded inputs, not measurements made here. The <specific volume> change on <melting> is
$$
\Delta v=\frac{1}{\rho_l}-\frac{1}{\rho_s}=-9.0513\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{m^3\,kg^{-1}}.
$$
The <common-pressure melting-point slope> therefore gives
$$
\frac{dT_m}{dp}=\frac{T_m\Delta v}{L}=-7.4022\times10^{-8}\,\mathrm{K\,Pa^{-1}}\simeq-0.07402\,\mathrm{K\,MPa^{-1}}.
$$
A small $1\,\mathrm{MPa}$ increase in the common <pressure> thus lowers the <melting> <temperature> by about $0.074\,\mathrm K$ in this local model. Positive <latent heat> does not force a positive <phase coexistence curve> slope: <ice> contracts on conversion to <liquid> <water>. Increasing <pressure> favors the smaller <specific volume>. This calculation compares equilibrium states of the same two <thermodynamic phases>; extending its tangent to large <pressures> would require updated <mass densities>, <latent heat of fusion>, and the relevant <ice> <phase diagram>.

= Chemical potential
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}
{title2=$\mu$}

The <chemical potential> measures how a <thermodynamic potential> changes when matter is added under specified constraints. It plays for matter exchange the role that <temperature> plays for <heat> exchange: a difference supplies a direction, and equality characterizes <thermodynamic equilibrium> when the corresponding exchange is allowed. This connects <diffusion>, <phase coexistence> and <chemical equilibrium> through one quantity.

For a single component, the particle convention is
$$
\mu_{\rm particle}=\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial N}\right)_{S,V},
$$
where $U$ is <internal energy>, $N$ counts particles, $S$ is <entropy> and $V$ is <volume (thermodynamics)>. This <partial derivative> holds <entropy> and <volume (thermodynamics)> fixed; it measures an incremental change rather than the average <energy> per particle. See https://davidtong.org/pdfs/teaching/statistical-physics/statphys1.pdf[David Tong, Statistical Physics, section 1.4].

In <chemistry> the molar convention instead uses <amount of substance> $n$, measured in <moles>:
$$
\mu_{\rm m}=\left(\frac{\partial G}{\partial n}\right)_{T,p},
\qquad n=\frac{N}{N_{\rm A}},
\qquad \mu_{\rm m}=N_{\rm A}\mu_{\rm particle}.
$$
Here $G$ is <Gibbs free energy>, $T$ is <temperature>, $p$ is <pressure> and $N_{\rm A}$ is the <Avogadro constant>. The units are $\mathrm J$ per particle versus $\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}$. For several <chemical species>, define each <chemical potential> by varying only its own particle count or <amount of substance>, with all other components' counts or amounts fixed. Many texts use $\mu$ for either convention, so the units and the variable being differentiated must identify it.

For a <simple compressible system> with variable component inventories, the fundamental differentials are
$$
dU=T\,dS-p\,dV+\sum_i\mu_{{\rm particle},i}\,dN_i,
\qquad
dG=-S\,dT+V\,dp+\sum_i\mu_{{\rm m},i}\,dn_i.
$$
They describe changes between states in <thermodynamic equilibrium> with only <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> <work>. If every component inventory is fixed, the sums vanish. The <Gibbs free energy> form follows by differentiating $G=U-TS+pV$ and using $dN_i=N_{\rm A}\,dn_i$; this is why the two definitions describe the same <chemical potential> in different units.

A <chemical potential> can be negative. Holding <entropy> fixed while adding matter need not increase <internal energy>; Tong explains this explicitly for a classical <ideal gas> in https://davidtong.org/pdfs/teaching/statistical-physics/statphys2.pdf[Statistical Physics, section 2.2.4]. Its absolute sign also depends on the reference <energy>: replacing $U$ by $U+\epsilon_0N$ shifts $\mu_{\rm particle}$ by $\epsilon_0$. A common reference shift cancels from differences for the same <chemical species>, which are what determine exchange. Thus “energy cost” means a signed increment, not an always positive amount that must be supplied.

= Chemical potential equality for particle exchange
{parent=Chemical potential}
{title2=$\mu_{{\rm m},A}=\mu_{{\rm m},B}$}

Consider two internally equilibrated reservoirs A and B at the same imposed <temperature> and <pressure>. They may have different compositions or represent different phases. Transfer a small positive <amount of substance> $\delta n$ of the same <chemical species> from A to B, holding the other inventories fixed. To first order, their total <Gibbs free energy> changes by
$$
\delta G_{\rm total}
=-\mu_{{\rm m},A}\delta n+\mu_{{\rm m},B}\delta n
=(\mu_{{\rm m},B}-\mu_{{\rm m},A})\delta n.
$$
Hence exchange toward lower <chemical potential> lowers <Gibbs free energy>. If transfer is allowed in both directions and the two reservoirs contain the exchangeable component, <thermodynamic equilibrium> requires equal <chemical potentials> for that component. See https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/5-60-thermodynamics-kinetics-spring-2008/e763a06aa3b5de09cd6bfea6280f219c_5_60_lecture14.pdf[MIT 5.60, Lecture 14, pages 1–2].

An impermeable partition can maintain unequal <chemical potentials>; equality is a condition on an allowed exchange. Two pure samples of the same <ideal gas> already have equal <chemical potentials> at the same <temperature> and <pressure>. Different compositions at a common <pressure> can instead give different <chemical potentials> for one component through its <thermodynamic activity>.

Equality concerns the same exchangeable component in different places, not all different <chemical species>. For a permitted <chemical reaction>, the corresponding interior <chemical equilibrium> criterion is $\sum_i\nu_i\mu_{{\rm m},i}=0$, with signed <stoichiometric vector> entries $\nu_i$. The existing <chemical equilibrium> and <constrained Gibbs minimization for chemical equilibrium> sections develop those composition constraints.

= Chemical potential and Helmholtz free energy
{parent=Chemical potential equality for particle exchange}
{title2=$\mu_{\rm m}=(\partial F/\partial n)_{T,V}$}

Rigid compartments impose a different constraint: fixed <volume (thermodynamics)> rather than fixed <pressure>. With <Helmholtz free energy> $F=U-TS$,
$$
dF=-S\,dT-p\,dV+\sum_i\mu_{{\rm m},i}\,dn_i,
\qquad
\mu_{{\rm m},i}=\left(\frac{\partial F}{\partial n_i}\right)_{T,V,n_{j\ne i}}.
$$
This follows immediately by differentiating $F=U-TS$ in the <chemical potential> differential above. It expresses the same <chemical potential> through the <thermodynamic potential> appropriate to fixed <temperature> and <volume (thermodynamics)>.

Consider a <closed system> consisting of two rigid compartments of equal <volume (thermodynamics)> $V$, maintained at a common fixed <temperature> $T$. A valve permits exchange of the same <ideal gas>; the partition stays in place. Initially $n_A=9\,\mathrm{mol}$ and $n_B=3\,\mathrm{mol}$, with conserved total <amount of substance> $n_{\rm tot}=12\,\mathrm{mol}$. These are macroscopic <moles>, modeled continuously, not twelve individual particles. By the <ideal gas law>, $p_B/p_A=n_B/n_A=1/3$, so initially
$$
\mu_{{\rm m},B}-\mu_{{\rm m},A}=-RT\ln3.
$$
At $300\,\mathrm K$ with the stipulated $R$, this is approximately $-2740.311\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}$, favoring transfer from A to B.

At fixed $T$ and equal $V$, integrating the ideal-gas <chemical potential> gives the total <Helmholtz free energy>, up to a term independent of the allocation,
$$
F_{\rm total}
=RT\left[n_A\ln\frac{n_A}{n_{\rm ref}}+n_B\ln\frac{n_B}{n_{\rm ref}}-n_{\rm tot}\right]+C.
$$
Here $n_{\rm ref}$ is a positive reference <amount of substance>; any omitted common term linear in $n_A+n_B$ is constant along this exchange. Let $x=n_A/n_{\rm tot}$ be the <dimensionless> fraction in A. The relative <Helmholtz free energy> then takes the particularly simple form
$$
\frac{F_{\rm total}(x)-F_{\rm total}(1/2)}{n_{\rm tot}RT}
=x\ln x+(1-x)\ln(1-x)+\ln2.
$$
Its <derivative> along the allowed transfer and its second <derivative> are
$$
\frac{dF_{\rm total}}{dn_A}
=\mu_{{\rm m},A}-\mu_{{\rm m},B}
=RT\ln\frac{x}{1-x},
\qquad
\frac{d^2F_{\rm total}}{dn_A^2}
=RT\left(\frac{1}{n_A}+\frac{1}{n_B}\right)>0.
$$
Thus the <Helmholtz free energy> is a strictly <convex function> of the allocation for positive amounts. Its unique minimum has $x=1/2$, or $n_A=n_B=6\,\mathrm{mol}$, and equal <chemical potentials>. Initially its excess above this minimum is $n_{\rm tot}RT[\tfrac34\ln(3/2)+\tfrac14\ln(1/2)]\simeq3915.474\,\mathrm J$ at $300\,\mathrm K$. Relaxation decreases the total <Helmholtz free energy> by this amount in the model; the final <pressures> are also equal because both compartments have the same <volume (thermodynamics)>.

\Image[chemical-potential-particle-transfer.png]
{title=<Chemical potential> differences are slopes of the total <Helmholtz free energy>}
{description=Two equal rigid compartments at fixed <temperature> start with $9\,\mathrm{mol}$ and $3\,\mathrm{mol}$ of the same <ideal gas>, and reach $6\,\mathrm{mol}$ each after exchange. Lower left: the <dimensionless> excess <Helmholtz free energy>. Lower right: its <derivative> with respect to the allocation, divided by $RT$, equals the <chemical potential> difference divided by $RT$. The horizontal variable is $x=n_A/n_{\rm tot}$.}

The beautiful connection is that the direction of matter exchange and the slope of a <free energy> curve are the same calculation. The <entropy> gained by redistributing the <ideal gas> makes equal allocation favorable here. As with <chemical equilibrium>, a <free energy> minimum identifies the favored state, while transport properties determine how quickly it is reached. The fixed-<temperature>, fixed-<pressure> <Gibbs free energy> criterion is summarized in <Chemistry 2e by Paul Flowers and others/16.4 Free Energy>; the rigid experiment instead uses <Helmholtz free energy>.

= Chemical potential of an ideal gas
{parent=Chemical potential}
{title2=$\mu_{\rm m}=\mu_{\rm m}^\circ+RT\ln(p/p^\circ)$}

For a pure <ideal gas> at <temperature> $T$ and <pressure> $p$,
$$
\mu_{\rm m}(T,p)=\mu_{\rm m}^\circ(T)+RT\ln\frac{p}{p^\circ},
$$
where $R$ is the <molar gas constant> and $p^\circ$ specifies the reference <pressure> of the <standard state>. The ratio inside the <natural logarithm> is <dimensionless>. The <chemical potential> difference therefore depends on a ratio of <pressures>, not an absolute difference. See https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/5-60-thermodynamics-kinetics-spring-2008/e763a06aa3b5de09cd6bfea6280f219c_5_60_lecture14.pdf[MIT 5.60, Lecture 14, page 4].

For a <chemical species> in an ideal <gas> <mixture>, replace $p$ by its <partial pressure>. More generally write $\mu_{{\rm m},i}=\mu_{{\rm m},i}^\circ+RT\ln a_i$ using its <thermodynamic activity> $a_i$ and a consistent <standard state>. For an ideal dilute <chemical solution>, $a_i=c_i/c^\circ$ with reference <concentration> $c^\circ$; the <thermodynamic activity> section describes departures from this idealization.

As a computed comparison, take identical <chemical species> at $T=300\,\mathrm K$, with $p_A=2p^\circ$ and $p_B=p^\circ$. Stipulate $R=8.314462618\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. Then
$$
\mu_{{\rm m},B}-\mu_{{\rm m},A}
=RT\ln\frac{p_B}{p_A}
=-RT\ln2
\simeq-1728.944\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}.
$$
Halving the <pressure> gives the same decrement whether starting from $2p^\circ$ or $20p^\circ$, provided the <ideal gas> approximation remains valid. This comparison uses different <pressures>; it is not the common-<pressure> reservoir setup of <chemical potential equality for particle exchange>. The fixed-<volume (thermodynamics)> example in <chemical potential and Helmholtz free energy> supplies an exchange experiment with unequal initial <pressures>.

\Image[chemical-potential-and-pressure.png]
{title=<Ideal gas> <chemical potential> difference versus <pressure> ratio}
{description=At a common <temperature>, the <chemical potential> difference is $RT\ln(p_B/p_A)$. The arrows give the initial transfer direction through an open route between rigid compartments; the decreasing <thermodynamic potential> there is <Helmholtz free energy>. The marked comparison uses $300\,\mathrm K$ and the stipulated <molar gas constant>.}

= Electrochemical potential
{parent=Chemical potential}
{wiki}
{title2=$\widetilde\mu_{\rm m}=\mu_{\rm m}+zF_{\rm Far}\psi$}

The molar <electrochemical potential> combines the molar <chemical potential> $\mu_{\rm m}$ with the electrical contribution $zF_{\rm Far}\psi$, where $z$ is the signed charge number of an <ion>, $F_{\rm Far}=N_{\rm A}e$ is the <Faraday constant> and $\psi$ is <electric potential>. Both terms have units $\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}$. In the particle convention the corresponding expression is $\widetilde\mu_{\rm particle}=\mu_{\rm particle}+ze\psi$, with <elementary charge> $e$; multiplying it by the <Avogadro constant> gives the molar form. Transfer between locations at the same <temperature> and <pressure> is driven by differences in <electrochemical potential>. The <membrane transport> examples use it to distinguish neutral <solute> transfer from <ion> transfer.

= Electrochemical gradient
{parent=Electrochemical potential}
{wiki}
{title2=$\nabla\widetilde\mu_{\rm m}$}

An <electrochemical gradient> combines spatial changes in <chemical potential> and <electric potential> for a charged species. <Passive transport> proceeds downhill in <electrochemical potential> when an appropriate route is available; a <concentration> difference alone does not determine the direction for an <ion>.

= Chemical potential of a composition field
{parent=Chemical potential}
{title2=$\mu=\delta F/\delta\phi$}

For a composition <free energy> $F=\int[f(\phi)+\kappa|\nabla\phi|^2/2]$, the local chemical potential is the <functional derivative>
$$
\mu=f'(\phi)-\kappa\nabla^2\phi.
$$
At <phase coexistence>, exchanges between the bulk phases require equal <chemical potentials>. Mechanical equilibrium additionally requires equal <pressure> for a flat interface, or a <Young–Laplace equation> pressure jump for a curved interface.

= Chemical equilibrium
{parent=Chemical potential}
{wiki}

At <chemical equilibrium>, a reversible <chemical reaction> has equal forward and reverse <reaction rates>, so its macroscopic composition stays constant. Individual <molecules> can keep changing identity: the reaction has not stopped. This dynamic balance is the definition described by the https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/C01023[IUPAC Gold Book, chemical equilibrium]{ref}. Equal <reaction rates> also need not mean equal <concentrations>. A small population converting rapidly can balance a large population converting slowly.

This distinction gives two useful questions. Which composition is favored at the specified <temperature> and other constraints? <Thermodynamics> answers through <chemical potentials> and an <equilibrium constant>. How quickly does a prepared mixture approach that composition? <Chemical kinetics> answers through <reaction rates>. Knowing the destination does not determine the journey's duration. A composition that barely changes during observation may reflect extremely slow <chemical kinetics>, so constancy alone does not establish <chemical equilibrium>.

Consider the idealized elementary <chemical reaction>
$$
\mathrm A\rightleftharpoons\mathrm B,
\qquad k_f=0.3\,\mathrm{s}^{-1},\qquad k_r=0.1\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}.
$$
Assume a well-mixed <closed system> at fixed <temperature> and <volume>, with first-order <law of mass action> rates in both directions and no competing <chemical reactions>. Write $a=[\mathrm A]$ and $b=[\mathrm B]$ for <concentrations> in $\mathrm M=\mathrm{mol}/\mathrm L$. The total $a+b=C=1\,\mathrm M$ is conserved because each conversion replaces one <molecule> by one <molecule>. This conservation is special to the chosen <stoichiometry>.

The forward and reverse <reaction rates>, measured as conversions per unit <volume> per unit time, are $J_f=k_fa$ and $J_r=k_rb$. Hence
$$
\frac{da}{dt}=-k_fa+k_rb=-(k_f+k_r)a+k_rC.
$$
Setting this <derivative> to zero gives
$$
a_{\rm eq}=\frac{k_rC}{k_f+k_r}=0.25\,\mathrm M,
\qquad b_{\rm eq}=0.75\,\mathrm M.
$$
With $a(0)=1\,\mathrm M$ and $b(0)=0$, solving the <ordinary differential equation> yields
$$
a(t)=\bigl(0.25+0.75e^{-0.4t/\mathrm s}\bigr)\,\mathrm M,
\qquad b(t)=\bigl(0.75-0.75e^{-0.4t/\mathrm s}\bigr)\,\mathrm M.
$$
At $t=5\,\mathrm s$, these are approximately $0.3515\,\mathrm M$ and $0.6485\,\mathrm M$. Initially $J_f=0.3\,\mathrm{M/s}$ and $J_r=0$. At <chemical equilibrium>, both are $0.075\,\mathrm{M/s}$, while the net <reaction rate> $J_f-J_r$ is zero. The persistent exchange is visible in the lower panel even after the upper panel has nearly flattened.

\Image[reversible-reaction-equilibrium.png]
{title=Constant equilibrium composition with continuing molecular conversion}
{description=The idealized <chemical reaction> starts entirely in A. Upper panel: <concentrations>. Lower panel: forward, reverse and net <reaction rates>. The equilibrium rates coincide at a positive value.}

Why does <chemical equilibrium> also involve a <free energy> minimum? For a <closed system> at fixed <temperature> and <pressure>, without imposed non-expansion <work>, stable equilibrium minimizes <Gibbs free energy> over compositions allowed by conserved elemental inventories. See https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/3-00-thermodynamics-of-materials-fall-2002/14c95abd6fac023d84bd33c5d74d787d_lecture_02_oneside.PDF[W. C. Carter, MIT thermodynamics notes, sections 2-1.14 and 2-1.19]. This is <constrained Gibbs minimization for chemical equilibrium>: freely minimizing over unrelated species amounts would change the inventory. At fixed <temperature> and <volume>, the appropriate potential is <Helmholtz free energy>. In either setting, varying an allowed <extent of reaction> gives the corresponding <free energy> slope $\sum_i\nu_i\mu_i$, with molar <chemical potentials> and signed <stoichiometric vector> entries. An interior equilibrium has zero slope; stability requires a minimum. For A converting to B, this says $\mu_{\mathrm B}=\mu_{\mathrm A}$.

The beauty of this connection is that molecular exchange, a macroscopic composition, and a geometric minimum describe the same balance. In the ideal concentration model, let $x=b/C$ and choose the standard molar <chemical potential> difference consistent with $K=3$. Up to a composition-independent constant, the fixed-volume <Helmholtz free energy> then obeys
$$
\frac{F}{nRT}=(1-x)\ln(1-x)+x\ln x-x\ln3,
$$
where $n$ is the conserved total <amount of substance> and $R$ the <molar gas constant>. Its <derivative> is $\ln[x/(1-x)]-\ln3$, vanishing at $x=3/4$. Its second <derivative> is $1/x+1/(1-x)>0$ inside $0<x<1$, so this balance is the unique minimum. The mixing <entropy> terms explain why the favored composition still contains A.

= Chemical disequilibrium
{parent=Chemical equilibrium}

A chemical system is out of local <chemical equilibrium> when reaction adjustment cannot keep pace with transport, imposed changes or external driving. Comparing the <chemical relaxation time> with transport and irradiation times identifies plausible departures. <Disequilibrium chemistry in an exoplanet atmosphere> combines this principle with mixing, horizontal advection and <atmospheric photochemistry>.

= Zero chemical potential from number-changing reactions
{parent=Chemical equilibrium}
{title2=$\mu_\phi=0$}

For one particle species in <chemical equilibrium> under $2\phi\leftrightarrow3\phi$, chemical balance gives $2\mu_\phi=3\mu_\phi$, hence $\mu_\phi=0$. Elastic $2\phi\leftrightarrow2\phi$ imposes no such constraint. In an expanding universe the number-changing reaction must remain fast compared with the <Hubble parameter>; after chemical freeze-out a nonzero <chemical potential> can develop even if elastic collisions maintain <kinetic equilibrium>.

= Chemical relaxation time
{title2=$\tau_{\rm chem}$}
{parent=Chemical equilibrium}

The chemical relaxation time estimates how rapidly a perturbed abundance returns toward <chemical equilibrium>. Comparing it with the <eddy mixing time> identifies a <chemical quench level>.

For the A-to-B model in <chemical equilibrium>, deviations decay by <exponential decay> with $\tau_{\rm chem}=1/(k_f+k_r)=2.5\,\mathrm s$. After one such interval, $1/e$ of the initial deviation remains; reaching one percent takes $\ln(100)\tau_{\rm chem}\simeq11.51\,\mathrm s$. Multiplying both <rate constants> by two preserves their ratio and the equilibrium composition, but halves $\tau_{\rm chem}$ to $1.25\,\mathrm s$. The equilibrium exchange rates double to $0.15\,\mathrm{M/s}$. Faster exchange therefore coexists with the same final composition and the same <free energy> minimum.

\Image[equilibrium-vs-rate.png]
{title=Doubling both rates changes the approach time but preserves equilibrium}
{description=Left: B <concentration> for two pairs of <rate constants> with the same ratio. Right: the shared ideal-mixture <Helmholtz free energy>, shifted to zero at its constrained minimum.}

= Equilibrium constant
{title2=$K$}
{parent=Chemical equilibrium}
{wiki}

For a specified <chemical reaction> and <standard state> convention, the dimensionless <equilibrium constant> is the equilibrium value of the <reaction quotient> $Q=\prod_i a_i^{\nu_i}$, where $a_i$ denotes <thermodynamic activity> and $\nu_i$ a signed <stoichiometric vector> entry. <Thermodynamic activities> incorporate the reference state and departures from ideal behavior. See https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/E02177[IUPAC Gold Book, equilibrium constant].

In the ideal concentration model for A converting to B, use $a_{\mathrm A}=[\mathrm A]/c^\circ$ and $a_{\mathrm B}=[\mathrm B]/c^\circ$ with the same reference $c^\circ=1\,\mathrm M$. Their ratio gives $Q=[\mathrm B]/[\mathrm A]$ and $K=0.75/0.25=3$. Equality $K=k_f/k_r$ applies to this elementary first-order pair; it is not a universal ratio of two measured <rate constants> for an arbitrary multistep <chemical reaction>.

At a specified constant <temperature>, with consistent <standard states>, the standard molar reaction <Gibbs free energy> is
$$
\Delta_rG^\circ=\sum_i\nu_i\mu_i^\circ=-RT\ln K,
\qquad \Delta_rG=\Delta_rG^\circ+RT\ln Q=RT\ln(Q/K).
$$
Here $\Delta_rG$ is the local slope per <mole> of <extent of reaction> at fixed <temperature> and <pressure>, rather than the finite total <Gibbs free energy> change between two mixtures. At equilibrium $\Delta_rG=0$, while $\Delta_rG^\circ$ generally remains nonzero. For $K=3$ at $298\,\mathrm K$, it is about $-2.72\,\mathrm{kJ/mol}$. For positive activities, $Q<K$ favors forward conversion and $Q>K$ favors reverse conversion; neither sign supplies a <reaction rate>. See https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/5-111sc-principles-of-chemical-science-fall-2014/ec12f084d867a5e5532b12ee99bdc767_MIT5_111F14_Lec18.pdf[MIT 5.111, Lecture 18, reaction free energy and equilibrium].

= Dissociation constant
{parent=Equilibrium constant}
{wiki}
{title2=$K_d$}

= Standard state
{parent=Chemical equilibrium}

A standard state is a specified reference state used to define a species' standard <chemical potential> $\mu_i^\circ$. The reference convention, including <temperature>, must be consistent when calculating <thermodynamic activities>, <reaction quotients>, and <equilibrium constants>.

= Thermodynamic activity
{parent=Standard state}
{title2=$a_i=\exp[(\mu_i-\mu_i^\circ)/(RT)]$}

The thermodynamic activity is the dimensionless quantity defined by $\mu_i=\mu_i^\circ+RT\ln a_i$, where $R$ is the <molar gas constant>. For an ideal dilute solution with a specified <concentration> reference, $a_i=c_i/c^\circ$; departures from ideality are represented by an <activity coefficient> multiplying this ratio.

= Activity coefficient
{parent=Thermodynamic activity}
{title2=$\gamma_i$}

= Reaction quotient
{parent=Chemical equilibrium}
{title2=$Q=\prod_i a_i^{\nu_i}$}

For a <chemical reaction> with signed <stoichiometric vector> entries $\nu_i$, positive for products and negative for reactants, the reaction quotient is the product of the species' <thermodynamic activities> raised to these powers. It equals the <equilibrium constant> at <chemical equilibrium>, with all quantities using the same <standard state> convention.

= Photon chemical potential
{parent=Chemical equilibrium}

The chemical potential of photons in thermal equilibrium is zero because their number is not conserved.

= Ideal gas
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}

= Perfect gas
{synonym}

An ideal gas is a model of a dilute <gas> whose particle centres move according to <classical mechanics> and occupy negligible <volume (thermodynamics)> compared with their container. Treating the particles as <point masses> removes the molecular-size correction; neglecting interactions between separated particles removes the intermolecular contribution to <potential energy>. The usual <kinetic theory> model retains brief <elastic collisions> with the walls and, optionally, with other particles: these transfer <momentum> while conserving the total <kinetic energy> of the colliding bodies. There is no appreciable interaction <energy> stored between <collisions>. These assumptions lead to the <ideal gas law>, $PV=Nk_BT$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/2-1-molecular-model-of-an-ideal-gas[OpenStax University Physics Volume 2, section 2.1: molecular model and ideal gas law]{ref} https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/2-2-pressure-temperature-and-rms-speed[OpenStax University Physics Volume 2, section 2.2: kinetic-model assumptions]{ref}

The model's attraction is its universality: at the same <temperature> and <number density>, different classical <ideal gases> have the same <pressure>, even when their particle <masses> differ. In <ideal gas pressure from wall collisions>, heavier particles move more slowly at a given <temperature>, and the two effects cancel. For example, one <mole> of any <ideal gas> at $300\,\mathrm K$ in $0.025\,\mathrm{m^3}$ has <pressure> approximately $99.774\,\mathrm{kPa}$, as computed under the <ideal gas law>.

The <ideal gas law> describes the translational contribution to <pressure>; it does not specify all of the <internal energy> or the <heat capacity>. A <monatomic gas> has three classical translational <degrees of freedom>, whereas a <gas> of <molecules> can also store <rotational kinetic energy> and <energy> in vibrational <normal modes>. An <equation of state> alone therefore does not determine how much <heat> a chosen <temperature> increase requires. It also does not select a process: <isothermal expansion> and <adiabatic expansion> need different additional constraints. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/2-3-heat-capacity-and-equipartition-of-energy[OpenStax University Physics Volume 2, section 2.3: internal degrees of freedom and heat capacity]{ref}

A <real gas> departs from this approximation when particle size or interactions become important, especially at high <number density> or low <temperature> near a <phase transition>. The <Van der Waals equation> adds such corrections. Independently, at sufficiently low <temperature> or high <number density>, <quantum statistics> can become essential: even particles with negligible interactions need not obey the classical <ideal gas law>. The classical statistical assumption is the <nondegenerate gas> regime, which is distinct from neglecting interactions. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/2-1-molecular-model-of-an-ideal-gas[OpenStax University Physics Volume 2, section 2.1: departures from ideal-gas behavior]{ref} <Statistical Physics by David Tong/3 Quantum Gases>{ref}

= Partial pressure
{parent=Ideal gas}
{wiki}
{title2=$p_i$}

The <partial pressure> of a component of a <gas> <mixture> is the <pressure> it would exert alone at the same <temperature> and total <volume (thermodynamics)>. For an ideal <mixture>, $p_i=n_iRT/V$ and $p=\sum_i p_i$. The <chemical potential of an ideal gas> uses $p_i$ for that component rather than the total <pressure> $p$.

= Molar gas constant
{parent=Ideal gas}
{title2=$R=N_Ak_B$}

The molar gas constant is the product of the <Avogadro constant> and the <Boltzmann constant>. It converts the particle-number form of the <ideal gas law>, $pV=Nk_BT$, into the amount-of-substance form $pV=nRT$, using $N=nN_A$ with $n$ measured in <moles>.

= Ideal gas law
{parent=Ideal gas}
{title2=$PV=Nk_BT=n_{\rm mol}RT$}
{wiki}

The <ideal gas law> is the <equation of state> relating the absolute <pressure> $P$, <volume (thermodynamics)> $V$, particle count $N$, and absolute <thermodynamic temperature> $T$ of a classical <ideal gas>:
$$
PV=Nk_BT=n_{\rm mol}RT,\qquad
N=n_{\rm mol}N_{\mathrm A},\qquad R=N_{\mathrm A}k_B.
$$
Here $k_B$ is the <Boltzmann constant>, $N_{\mathrm A}$ is the <Avogadro constant>, $R$ is the <molar gas constant>, and $n_{\rm mol}$ is the <amount of substance> in <moles>. The distinct symbol $n_{\rm mol}$ avoids confusing <amount of substance> with the <number density> $n=N/V$, measured in $\mathrm{m^{-3}}$: the same law can be written $P=nk_BT$. Use absolute <pressure>, relative to a <vacuum>, rather than an unconverted <gauge pressure>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/2-1-molecular-model-of-an-ideal-gas[OpenStax University Physics Volume 2, section 2.1: particle-count and molar forms]{ref}

The exact <SI> definitions of the <Avogadro constant> and <Boltzmann constant> give
$$
R=(6.02214076\times10^{23})(1.380649\times10^{-23})
\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}
=8.31446261815324\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}.
$$
This numerical value is exact; the applicability of the <ideal gas> model to a <real gas> is an approximation. https://www.bipm.org/en/si-base-units/mole[BIPM, exact Avogadro constant in the mole definition]{ref} https://www.bipm.org/en/si-base-units/kelvin[BIPM, exact Boltzmann constant in the kelvin definition]{ref}

For $n_{\rm mol}=1\,\mathrm{mol}$, $T=300\,\mathrm K$, and $V=0.025\,\mathrm{m^3}$, direct substitution gives
$$
RT=2494.338785445972\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}},\qquad
P=\frac{n_{\rm mol}RT}{V}
=99773.55141783888\,\mathrm{Pa}
\approx99.774\,\mathrm{kPa}.
$$
At fixed <temperature> and particle count, doubling the <volume (thermodynamics)> to $0.050\,\mathrm{m^3}$ halves the <pressure> to approximately $49.887\,\mathrm{kPa}$: this is <Boyle's law>. At fixed <volume (thermodynamics)> and particle count, doubling the <temperature> from $300\,\mathrm K$ to $600\,\mathrm K$ doubles the <pressure> to approximately $199.547\,\mathrm{kPa}$. These ratios use <kelvins>, not readings in the <degree Celsius> scale: $300\,\mathrm K$ is $26.85\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$, and $600\,\mathrm K$ is $326.85\,{}^\circ\mathrm C$.

\Image[ideal-gas-state-slices.png]
{title=Ideal gas law: pressure versus volume at fixed temperatures, and pressure versus absolute temperature at fixed volume}

The curved slices show <Boyle's law> at three <temperatures>; the straight slice shows proportionality to absolute <temperature> at one fixed <volume (thermodynamics)>. They are relations among equilibrium states, rather than a prescribed process history. Compression could hold the <temperature> fixed through <heat transfer>, or raise it during <adiabatic compression>. To calculate a process's <heat> or <work>, supplement the <ideal gas law> with the process constraint, the <first law of thermodynamics>, and the relevant <internal energy> or <heat capacity> information. The graph's extrapolation toward $T=0$ is mathematical; it does not assert that a <real gas> remains classical or gaseous there.

For a stellar <mixture> with fixed <mean molecular weight>, the <ideal gas law> can be written $P=\mathcal R\rho T/\mu$, where $\rho$ is <mass density> and $\mathcal R$ is the <stellar gas constant>. It assumes negligible interaction corrections and a <nondegenerate gas>; excitation or <ionization> can change the particle count and thermodynamic response.

In his 1834 memoir on the motive power of <heat>, Émile Clapeyron combined gas laws into a relation between <pressure>, <volume (thermodynamics)>, and <temperature> while analysing <heat engines> (printed page 164). The equation above uses modern <SI> notation and absolute <thermodynamic temperature>; it is not a transcription of his historical temperature convention. https://www3.nd.edu/~powers/ame.20231/clapeyron1834.pdf[Clapeyron, Mémoire sur la puissance motrice de la chaleur (1834), page 164]{ref}

= Ideal gas pressure from wall collisions
{parent=Ideal gas law}
{title2=$P=Nm\langle v_x^2\rangle/V$}

Consider a particle of <mass> $m$ in a rigid rectangular box of length $L$ along $x$ and wall <surface area> $A$, so $V=AL$ is the <volume (thermodynamics)>. Choose $+x$ toward the right wall. For an incoming normal <velocity> $v_x=v>0$, an <elastic collision> reverses the normal <momentum>:
$$
p_{x,\rm in}=mv,\qquad p_{x,\rm out}=-mv,\qquad
\Delta p_{x,\rm particle}=-2mv,\qquad
J_{x,\rm wall}=+2mv.
$$
The <impulse> delivered to the wall points outward, toward $+x$, by <Newton's third law>. The particle's opposite <momentum> change is not the wall's <impulse>. A tangential <velocity> component is unchanged by the ideal smooth-wall <collision>.

\Image[ideal-gas-wall-momentum.png]
{title=Schematic ideal gas model: an elastic bounce transfers outward momentum to a wall; this is not a real experimental setup}

Ignoring intervening particle-particle <collisions> in this counting model, the particle travels a normal distance $2L$ before hitting the same wall again. Its hit rate is $|v_x|/(2L)$; multiplying by the <impulse> per hit gives the time-averaged normal <force> and its contribution to <pressure>:
$$
\overline F_{\perp,1}=(2m|v_x|)\frac{|v_x|}{2L}
=\frac{mv_x^2}{L},\qquad
P_1=\frac{\overline F_{\perp,1}}{A}=\frac{mv_x^2}{V}.
$$
For example, stipulate $m=4\times10^{-26}\,\mathrm{kg}$, $|v_x|=500\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, $L=0.10\,\mathrm m$, and $A=0.010\,\mathrm{m^2}$. Then $V=0.0010\,\mathrm{m^3}$, the <impulse> per hit is $4\times10^{-23}\,\mathrm{N\,s}$, the hit rate is $2500\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$, and $P_1=10^{-17}\,\mathrm{Pa}$. This is one particle's tiny time-averaged contribution to one wall's <pressure>, not an <isotropic> equilibrium <gas> by itself.

For $N$ particles of equal <mass>, add their <force> contributions and define $\langle v_x^2\rangle=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^Nv_{x,i}^2$. In the <gas>'s rest frame, <isotropy> gives $\langle v_x^2\rangle=\langle v_y^2\rangle=\langle v_z^2\rangle=\langle v^2\rangle/3$, where $v^2=v_x^2+v_y^2+v_z^2$. Hence
$$
P=\frac{Nm\langle v_x^2\rangle}{V}
=\frac{Nm\langle v^2\rangle}{3V},\qquad
\frac12m\langle v^2\rangle=\frac32k_BT
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad PV=Nk_BT.
$$
The final equality uses the classical <equipartition theorem>. The <kinetic pressure of an isotropic gas> therefore recovers the <ideal gas law> from <momentum> transfers. Doubling the normal speed would double both the <impulse> per hit and the hit rate, giving four times the <pressure> contribution; <pressure> measures a mean squared speed. Interparticle <elastic collisions> redistribute <velocities> and help establish <isotropy>; the individual round-trip times need not remain $2L/|v_x|$, but the equilibrium <momentum flux> result survives. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/2-2-pressure-temperature-and-rms-speed[OpenStax University Physics Volume 2, section 2.2: wall-collision pressure derivation]{ref}

= Monatomic gas
{parent=Ideal gas}
{title2=$\gamma=5/3$}

A monatomic gas consists of individual atoms or ions rather than molecules. In the classical dilute ideal-gas regime, its three translational degrees of freedom give $U=3Nk_BT/2$ by <equipartition theorem>. Thus $C_V=3Nk_B/2$, while $C_P-C_V=Nk_B$ follows by differentiating the ideal-gas enthalpy $H=U+PV=5Nk_BT/2$. Consequently the <adiabatic index> is $\gamma=C_P/C_V=5/3$ and the <specific internal energy> is $e=3P/(2\rho)$. The same translational result applies to a fully ionized mixture when the particle numbers are fixed. This excludes additional excitation, ionization and radiation contributions to the heat capacity.

= Mean free path
{parent=Ideal gas}
{title2=$\ell$}

The mean free path is the average distance travelled between <collisions>. For dilute targets with <number density> $n$ and effective <collision cross-section> $\sigma_c$, $\ell\sim1/(n\sigma_c)$; identical-particle conventions introduce factors such as $\sqrt2$.

= Mean free path through randomly oriented disc absorbers
{parent=Mean free path}
{title2=$\ell^{-1}=n_{\rm com}(1+z)^3\pi R_d^2/2$}

For an unclustered population of fully covering circular disc absorbers, the inverse <mean free path> is proper number density times mean projected area. A comoving number density must first be multiplied by $(1+z)^3$. The incidence then constrains the proper radius; covering factor and multiplicity of detectable lines can change the inference.

= Mean molecular weight
{title2=$\mu$}
{parent=Ideal gas}
{wiki}

The mean molecular weight is the average mass per freely moving gas particle in units of the atomic mass unit. A fully ionized hydrogen-helium gas with mass fractions $X,Y$ has $\mu^{-1}=2X+3Y/4$.

= Stellar gas constant
{parent=Mean molecular weight}
{title2=$\mathcal R=k_B/m_u$}

In stellar <ideal gas> equations, the <stellar gas constant> $\mathcal R=k_B/m_u$ converts a dimensionless <mean molecular weight> $\mu$ into the specific thermal relation $P=\mathcal R\rho T/\mu$. Here $m_u$ is the reference atomic mass unit, commonly approximated by the proton mass in simplified hydrogen-helium models. It has units of energy per mass per temperature. It is the molar gas constant divided by the molar mass corresponding to $m_u$, rather than the molar gas constant used with a dimensional molar molecular weight.

= Mean molecular weight per ion
{parent=Mean molecular weight}
{title2=$\mu_i^{-1}=\sum_jX_j/A_j$}

= Ion mean molecular weight
{synonym}

The <mean molecular weight> per ion counts nuclei rather than nuclei plus free electrons: $\rho/(\mu_i m_u)$ is the ion number density. For species with mass fractions $X_j$ and mass numbers $A_j$, $\mu_i^{-1}=\sum_jX_j/A_j$. It is useful when ions provide a thermal <ideal gas> pressure but <electron degeneracy pressure> supplies a separate contribution.

= Mean molecular weight per electron
{title2=$\mu_e=\rho/(m_un_e)$}
{parent=Mean molecular weight}

The mean molecular weight per electron is the mass carried per free electron in units of atomic mass $m_u$. Fully ionized hydrogen has $\mu_e=1$, while helium, carbon, and oxygen have approximately $\mu_e=2$. It converts <electron number density> to bulk <mass density> in the <equation of state of a cold electron gas>.

= Heat capacity ratio
{title2=$\gamma$}
{parent=Ideal gas}
{wiki}

= Specific-heat ratio
{synonym}

= Adiabatic exponent
{synonym}

= Adiabatic index
{synonym}

The heat capacity ratio compares <heat capacity at constant pressure> $C_p$ with <heat capacity at constant volume> $C_V$ at the same equilibrium state and fixed composition:
$$
\gamma=\frac{C_p}{C_V}
=\frac{C_{p,\mathrm m}}{C_{V,\mathrm m}}
=\frac{c_p}{c_V}.
$$
Here $C_p,C_V$ refer to the whole sample, in $\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}$; <molar heat capacities> $C_{p,\mathrm m}=C_p/n$ and $C_{V,\mathrm m}=C_V/n$ are per <mole>; <specific heat capacities> $c_p=C_p/m$ and $c_V=C_V/m$ are per unit <mass>. The <amount of substance> $n$ or <mass> $m$ cancels only when both capacities use the same normalization. Thus <heat capacity ratio> is dimensionless and independent of sample size. For a fixed-composition <ideal gas> with positive finite $C_V$, <Mayer's relation> gives
$$
\gamma(T)=1+\frac{nR}{C_V(T)}=1+\frac{R}{C_{V,\mathrm m}(T)}>1,
$$
where $R$ is the <molar gas constant>. More ways to store <internal energy> make a given <temperature> increase require more <heat>, bringing <heat capacity ratio> closer to unity. https://web.mit.edu/16.unified/www/FALL/thermodynamics/notes/node18.html[MIT Unified Engineering, Specific heats: definitions and ideal-gas relations]{ref}

The <equipartition theorem> predicts $C_{V,\mathrm m}=fR/2$ when $f$ independent quadratic terms in molecular <energy> are thermally active and their number stays fixed; consequently $\gamma=1+2/f$. A classical <monatomic gas> with no appreciable electronic excitation has $f=3$, giving $C_{V,\mathrm m}=3R/2$ and $\gamma=5/3$. For an <ideal gas> of diatomic <molecules> with three translational and two rotational terms active, but its vibrational <normal mode> effectively frozen, $f=5$ gives $C_{V,\mathrm m}=5R/2$ and $\gamma=7/5$. A fully classical harmonic vibration adds both <kinetic energy> and <potential energy>, giving $f=7$ and $\gamma=9/7$. These are model regimes, not universal constants of every <gas>: rotational freezing, electronic excitation, and changing composition invalidate the simple count.<Statistical Physics by David Tong/2 Classical Gases>[§§2.2.1 and 2.4: equipartition and diatomic gases]{ref}

The mechanical significance of <heat capacity ratio> follows from the <first law of thermodynamics>. For a fixed amount of <ideal gas> in a reversible <adiabatic process>, $C_V(T)\,dT=-p\,dV$. Combining this with the <ideal gas law> gives the local relation
$$
\left(\frac{\partial\ln p}{\partial\ln V}\right)_S=-\gamma(T),\qquad
\int_{T_1}^{T_2}\frac{C_V(T)}{T}\,dT+nR\ln\frac{V_2}{V_1}=0.
$$
The <partial derivative> holds at fixed <entropy> and composition even when <heat capacity ratio> varies with <temperature>. The finite-path formulas $pV^\gamma=\mathrm{constant}$ and $TV^{\gamma-1}=\mathrm{constant}$ require the additional approximation of constant <heat capacity ratio>. A rapid compression also needs internal equilibration to approximate this path; rapid motion alone does not ensure reversibility. The same local response gives <isentropic compressibility> $1/(\gamma p)$ and, for small sound disturbances in this <ideal gas> regime, <speed of sound> $a=\sqrt{\gamma p/\rho}$, where $\rho$ is <mass density>. Thus thermal storage controls mechanical stiffness, sound propagation and the measurements in <Rüchardt experiment> and <Clément-Desormes experiment>.

= Rüchardt experiment
{c}
{parent=Heat capacity ratio}

The Rüchardt experiment infers <heat capacity ratio> from the <period of an oscillation> of a piston supported by trapped <gas>. Use a vertical precision cylinder with a close-fitting movable piston, known moving <mass> $M$, face <area> $A$, trapped equilibrium <volume (thermodynamics)> $V_0$, and local atmospheric <pressure> $p_{\rm atm}$. With negligible static <friction>, <force balance> gives the absolute gas <pressure> $p_0=p_{\rm atm}+Mg/A$, where $g$ is gravitational acceleration. Displace the piston slightly and release it: compression raises <pressure>, and expansion lowers it, producing a restoring <force>. The <oscillation> is a mechanical probe of thermal response, without separately measuring two <heat> inputs. https://groups.ist.utl.pt/wwwelab/wiki/index.php?title=Determination_of_the_Adiabatic_Constant[Instituto Superior Técnico e-lab, Determination of the Adiabatic Constant: piston apparatus and period model]{ref}

Let $x$ be upward <displacement> from equilibrium, so $V=V_0+Ax$. For constant <heat capacity ratio> and a reversible <adiabatic process>, the net upward <force>, including the ambient load and weight, is
$$
F(x)=A p_0\left[\left(1+\frac{Ax}{V_0}\right)^{-\gamma}-1\right]
\simeq-\frac{\gamma p_0A^2}{V_0}x.
$$
The <linear approximation> requires $|Ax|/V_0\ll1$. Applying <Newton's second law> gives <simple harmonic motion> with
$$
\omega_0^2=\frac{\gamma p_0A^2}{MV_0},\qquad
\gamma=\frac{4\pi^2MV_0}{p_0A^2\mathcal T^2},\qquad
\mathcal T=\frac{2\pi}{\omega_0}.
$$
Here $\omega_0$ is the undamped <angular frequency> and $\mathcal T$ the undamped <oscillation period>, rather than <temperature>. Record repeated cycles, measure $M,A,V_0,p_{\rm atm}$ independently, and include all connected trapped <volume (thermodynamics)>. An illustrative model with $M=0.020\,\mathrm{kg}$, $A=2.0\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2}$, $V_0=0.010\,\mathrm{m^3}$, $p_{\rm atm}=100000\,\mathrm{Pa}$, $g=9.81\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$ and $\gamma=1.4$ gives $p_0=100981\,\mathrm{Pa}$ and $\mathcal T\simeq1.182\,\mathrm s$. These stipulated values are not measurements.

\Image[heat-capacity-ratio-gas-spring.png]
{title=<Rüchardt experiment>: <heat capacity ratio> sets the restoring stiffness of trapped <gas>}
{description=Original schematic and analytical <ideal gas> curves. Upward piston <displacement> increases <volume (thermodynamics)>; the blue curve uses constant $\gamma=1.4$, its dashed tangent gives the small-<amplitude> model, and the orange curve is the <isothermal process> limit. The shaded central interval illustrates a small fractional <volume (thermodynamics)> change, without specifying an experimental acceptance threshold. No measured waveform is shown.}

In Shearwood and Sloan's implementation, piston-mounted magnets induce an <electromotive force> in a stationary coil through <Faraday's law of induction>; an oscilloscope records the signal. They observed faster <oscillations> at smaller trapped <volume (thermodynamics)> values and stronger <damping> at small cylinder <volume (thermodynamics)> values. The signal tracks <velocity> locally, so its <amplitude> is not directly piston <displacement>. Calibration also identified inlet dead space beyond the graduated cylinder volume. https://arxiv.org/html/2209.03323v1[Chris Shearwood and Peter A. Sloan, The Rüchardt experiment revisited, §§3–4: signal, dead volume and observations]{ref}

Repeat at smaller initial <amplitudes> to test the <linear approximation>, and compare several $V_0$ values. <Heat transfer> weakens the restoring <force> toward the <isothermal process> limit, biasing the inference toward $1$. Check leakage through resting-piston drift and before-and-after trapped <volume (thermodynamics)> readings. For viscous <damping> with envelope $e^{-t/\tau}$, the measured <angular frequency> obeys $\omega_0^2=\omega_d^2+\tau^{-2}$; this correction does not remove thermal leakage. Internal equilibration must be faster than an <oscillation period>, with <heat transfer> and leakage slow over a run. Large <amplitude> or sticking <friction> require a richer model. https://arxiv.org/html/2209.03323v1[Shearwood and Sloan, §§2.1, 2.4 and 3.2: damping, heat exchange and leakage limitations]{ref}

= Clément-Desormes experiment
{c}
{parent=Heat capacity ratio}

The Clément-Desormes experiment infers <heat capacity ratio> from a rapid <pressure> reduction followed by reheating. Use a rigid bottle, pump or bellows, a quick release valve, and a U-tube <pressure> gauge connected to the bottle and atmosphere. First pump to a small excess <pressure>, close the inlet, and wait for <thermal equilibrium> with the room at $T_0$. Measure $p_1>p_a$, where $p_a$ is local absolute atmospheric <pressure>. Briefly open the release valve until the bottle reaches $p_a$, then close it promptly. The remaining <gas> is cooler than the room. As it warms back to $T_0$ at fixed bottle <volume (thermodynamics)>, its <pressure> rises to $p_3$, with $p_a<p_3<p_1$ in the ideal procedure. The rebound reveals cooling during release. https://labs.physik.kit.edu/prakt-nebenfach-manuals/Experiments/Adiabatenexponent_Luft/[KIT Faculty of Physics, Clément-Desormes laboratory protocol: release and pressure rebound]{ref}

The bottle loses <molecules> while the valve is open: it is not a <closed system> whose geometrical <volume (thermodynamics)> expands. In the well-mixed, negligible-<heat-transfer> discharge model with constant <heat capacity ratio>, the remaining <gas> obeys $T_2/T_0=(p_a/p_1)^{(\gamma-1)/\gamma}$. Subsequent heating at fixed <amount of substance> and bottle <volume (thermodynamics)> gives $p_3/p_a=T_0/T_2$ by the <ideal gas law>. Eliminating the unmeasured $T_2$ yields
$$
\gamma=\frac{\ln(p_1/p_a)}{\ln(p_1/p_3)}
\simeq\frac{\Delta p_1}{\Delta p_1-\Delta p_3}
=\frac{h_1}{h_1-h_3},\qquad
\Delta p_i=p_i-p_a=\rho_{\rm liquid}gh_i.
$$
The <logarithms> use absolute <pressure> ratios. The approximation requires small <gauge pressures> $\Delta p_i\ll p_a$; $h_i$ are signed height differences between the two liquid surfaces, and the final equality uses the same liquid in both readings with <hydrostatic equilibrium>. It is not an instruction to insert gauge readings into the logarithmic formula. For synthetic states generated with $p_a=100000\,\mathrm{Pa}$, $p_1=102000\,\mathrm{Pa}$ and $\gamma=1.4$, the model gives $p_3\simeq100567.39\,\mathrm{Pa}$. The logarithmic inference recovers $1.4$; the small-<pressure> approximation gives approximately $1.3961$.
