Wait for a stable initial <pressure> after pumping and for a stable final <pressure> after closing. Vary the valve-open interval and initial excess <pressure> across repeated trials: closing too early leaves residual excess <pressure>, while leaving the valve open during warming lets more <gas> escape and suppresses the rebound. Test the seal for leaks, and read both liquid levels consistently. In a variant with incomplete release, measure the actual immediate post-release absolute <pressure> $p_2$ and replace the numerator by $\ln(p_1/p_2)$. The Zurich laboratory manual explains that liquid-column <oscillations> can delay this reading until appreciable reheating has occurred; its proposed backward extrapolation assumes an exponential thermal relaxation and must be checked against the recorded readings. These controls separate a thermal signal from valve timing, instrumental lag and leakage. https://www.physik.uzh.ch/~matthias/espace-assistant/manuals/en/anleitung-cd_e.pdf[University of Zurich, PHY112/122 laboratory manual, §1.3 and Appendix 1.4(a): pressure readings and reheating correction]{ref}

= Nondegenerate gas
{parent=Ideal gas}

A gas is nondegenerate when its occupation numbers are low enough that <quantum statistics> reduce to the <Maxwell-Boltzmann distribution>. A common criterion is $n\lambda_{\rm th}^3\ll1$, where $\lambda_{\rm th}$ is the thermal de Broglie wavelength.

For an <ideal gas>, this is the classical statistical regime; nondegeneracy alone does not assert the absence of interactions.

= Internal energy of an ideal gas
{parent=Ideal gas}

For a fixed <amount of substance> and composition of classical <ideal gas>, <internal energy> depends only on <temperature>: $U=U(T,n)$, with no independent <volume (thermodynamics)> dependence. This does not require constant <heat capacity at constant volume>. Between equilibrium endpoints,
$$
dU=C_V(T)\,dT=nC_{V,\mathrm m}(T)\,dT,
\qquad U(T_2)-U(T_1)=n\int_{T_1}^{T_2}C_{V,\mathrm m}(T)\,dT.
$$
Here $C_V$ is total <heat capacity at constant volume>, $C_{V,\mathrm m}$ is <molar heat capacity>, and $U/m$ for sample <mass> $m$ is <specific internal energy>, whose change is $\int_{T_1}^{T_2}c_V(T)\,dT$. These endpoint relations apply even when the intervening process is not at fixed <volume (thermodynamics)>; they do not say that transferred <heat> always equals $\Delta U$. With constant $C_V$, the safe statement is $U(T)=U(T_*)+C_V(T-T_*)$. Writing $U=C_VT$ additionally chooses the reference <energy>; it does not prove that a physical <gas> remains classical and ideal down to zero <temperature>. https://www.feynmanlectures.caltech.edu/I_45.html#Ch45-S1[Richard Feynman, Robert Leighton and Matthew Sands, §45-1: internal-energy derivatives and heat capacity]{ref}

A thermodynamic proof outline uses the <first law of thermodynamics> in equilibrium form, $dU=T\,dS-p\,dV$, and the <Maxwell relation> $(\partial S/\partial V)_T=(\partial p/\partial T)_V$:
$$
\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial V}\right)_T
=T\left(\frac{\partial p}{\partial T}\right)_V-p
=T\frac{nR}{V}-\frac{nRT}{V}=0.
$$
The last step uses the <ideal gas law> and the <molar gas constant> $R$, with fixed composition and <amount of substance>. Microscopically, separated <ideal gas> particles store no intermolecular interaction <energy>; their translational <kinetic energy> and any internal molecular <energy> are determined by <temperature>. Negligible intermolecular interactions still allow a <molecule> to have internal vibrational <potential energy>.

An original analytical model makes the distinction between an <ideal gas> and constant <heat capacity> concrete. Assume classical translation, two fully active rotational terms, and one independent <quantum harmonic oscillator> per diatomic <molecule>, with fixed <angular frequency> $\omega$. Define the vibrational <temperature> scale $\Theta=\hbar\omega/k_B$, using the reduced <Planck constant> $\hbar=h/(2\pi)$ and <Boltzmann constant> $k_B$. Summing its thermal occupations gives
$$
\frac{U(T)-U_*}{nR}=\frac52T+\frac{\Theta}{e^{\Theta/T}-1},\qquad
\frac{C_{V,\mathrm m}(T)}{R}=\frac52+\frac{x^2e^{-x}}{(1-e^{-x})^2},\qquad x=\frac{\Theta}{T}.
$$
The constant $U_*$ includes the chosen reference and <zero-point energy>, whose <temperature> derivative vanishes. When $T\ll\Theta$ the vibrational contribution to <heat capacity> is exponentially small; when $T\gg\Theta$ it tends to $R$ per <mole>. This model assumes rotations remain active throughout the interval and excludes dissociation, electronic excitation and <real gas> corrections. Its low-$T/\Theta$ edge is not a model of rotational freezing or of an actual substance at absolute zero.<Statistical Physics by David Tong/3 Quantum Gases>[§3.4: quantum vibrational spectrum, thermal occupations and zero-point energy]{ref}

\Image[heat-capacity-ratio-vibrational-model.png]
{title=Temperature-dependent <internal energy of an ideal gas>, <molar heat capacities> and <heat capacity ratio> in a vibrational model}
{description=Analytical illustration with active rotations and one <quantum harmonic oscillator> per <molecule>. Left: <Mayer's relation> keeps the capacity difference equal to $R$. Middle: <heat capacity ratio> falls from $7/5$ toward $9/7$. Right: the <internal energy> change from $T=0.2\Theta$ is an <integral> of varying <heat capacity at constant volume>; the dashed line suppresses the vibrational contribution. No measured gas curve or material-specific vibrational frequency is implied.}

For this stipulated model, heating from $T_1=0.2\Theta$ to $T_2=\Theta$ gives $\Delta U/(nR\Theta)\simeq2.57519$. Holding the frozen-vibration value $C_{V,\mathrm m}=5R/2$ throughout would give $2$, underestimating the required <internal energy> increase by about $22.3\%$ of the model's full value. The <integral> measures all thermal storage activated along the interval. At fixed <volume (thermodynamics)> with only <heat> input, this is also the supplied <heat>; at fixed <pressure>, expansion <work> must be added through <Mayer's relation>.

<Joule's free-expansion experiment> tests the independence from <volume (thermodynamics)> in a different way: an insulated rigid vessel allows <gas> into an evacuated compartment, so no external <work> is delivered and ideally no <heat> is exchanged. The <first law of thermodynamics> gives $\Delta U=0$; positive $C_V$ then predicts equal initial and final equilibrium <temperatures> for the <ideal gas>. The apparatus, thermal-capacity controls and limitations of this inference are described in <Joule's free-expansion experiment> and <temperature sensitivity of a free-expansion experiment>. This endpoint test differs from the reversible <adiabatic expansion> of a loaded piston, which delivers <work> and lowers <internal energy>.

= Mayer's relation
{c}
{parent=Ideal gas}
{wiki}

= Mayer relation
{c}
{synonym}

Mayer's relation states that the difference between <heat capacity at constant pressure> and <heat capacity at constant volume> for a fixed-composition <ideal gas> is
$$
C_p(T)-C_V(T)=nR=Nk_B.
$$
Here both capacities are total <heat capacities> in $\mathrm{J\,K^{-1}}$, $n$ is <amount of substance>, $N$ is the number of particles, $R$ is the <molar gas constant> and $k_B$ is the <Boltzmann constant>. In other normalizations the same statement is
$$
C_{p,\mathrm m}(T)-C_{V,\mathrm m}(T)=R,\qquad
c_p(T)-c_V(T)=\frac{nR}{m}.
$$
The first is a difference of <molar heat capacities>, in $\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$; the second uses <specific heat capacities>, in $\mathrm{J\,kg^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$. Writing $C_p-C_V=R$ with total capacities would omit the sample's <amount of substance>. The relation holds pointwise even when the capacities depend on <temperature>. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/3-5-heat-capacities-of-an-ideal-gas[Samuel J. Ling, William Moebs and Jeff Sanny, University Physics Volume 2, §3.5: ideal-gas capacity difference]{ref}

The proof outline is short: <enthalpy> is $H=U+pV=U+nRT$, and <internal energy of an ideal gas> is independent of <volume (thermodynamics)> at fixed <temperature>. Differentiating at fixed composition and <amount of substance> therefore gives $C_p=dH/dT=dU/dT+nR=C_V+nR$. It uses the <ideal gas law>, rather than assuming any particular number of molecular <degrees of freedom>. At fixed <pressure>, the additional <heat> supplies expansion <work>, $p\,dV=nR\,dT$. The two processes can have the same <temperature> endpoints and equal $\Delta U$ while requiring different <heat> inputs.

For one <mole> of the rigid-rotation diatomic <ideal gas> model, $C_V=5R/2$ and $C_p=7R/2$. Heating by $10\,\mathrm K$ with $R\simeq8.31446\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$ gives $Q_V\simeq207.86\,\mathrm J$ in a rigid sealed vessel and $Q_p\simeq291.01\,\mathrm J$ under a frictionless piston at fixed <pressure>. Both gain $207.86\,\mathrm J$ of <internal energy>, while the piston delivers approximately $83.14\,\mathrm J$ of <work>. This is a calculated model budget. If the capacities vary with <temperature>, the corresponding finite statement is still
$$
Q_p-Q_V=nR(T_2-T_1),\qquad
Q_V=n\int_{T_1}^{T_2}C_{V,\mathrm m}(T)\,dT,
$$
under the fixed-<pressure> and fixed-<volume (thermodynamics)> heating assumptions in <heat capacity>. Their final <volume (thermodynamics)> values generally differ. Combining <Mayer's relation> with <heat capacity ratio> also gives $C_V=nR/(\gamma-1)$ and $C_p=\gamma nR/(\gamma-1)$ locally; constant values cannot be extrapolated across intervals in which $\gamma(T)$ changes.

For a smooth stable single <thermodynamic phase> of a general <simple compressible system>, the broader <Maxwell relation> identity is
$$
C_p-C_V=T\left(\frac{\partial p}{\partial T}\right)_V\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_p
=\frac{TV\alpha^2}{\kappa_T},
$$
where $\alpha$ is the <coefficient of thermal expansion> and $\kappa_T$ the <isothermal compressibility>, with composition fixed throughout. Substituting the <ideal gas law> gives $nR$; a <real gas> need not do so. This distinction matters near a <phase transition> or when interactions appreciably change the <equation of state>. It explains why <Mayer's relation> is useful: thermal measurements under different constraints become linked to a mechanical response, while the difference retains a particularly simple value for an <ideal gas>.<Statistical Physics by David Tong/4 Classical Thermodynamics>[§4.4.2: Maxwell relations and the general heat-capacity difference]{ref}

= Real gas
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}

A <real gas> departs from the <ideal gas> law because interactions and molecular volume matter. Nonideal <equations of state>, including the <Dieterici equation>, model such departures.

= Dieterici equation
{parent=Real gas}
{c}

The Dieterici equation of state
$$
p=\frac{k_BT}{v-b}\exp\left(-\frac{a}{k_BTv}\right)
$$
models excluded volume through $b$ and attraction through $a$. Its critical point has $v_c=2b$ and $T_c=a/(4bk_B)$.

= Intensive and extensive thermodynamic quantities
{parent=Thermodynamics}

An extensive quantity scales in proportion to the amount of material, while an intensive quantity is unchanged when the system is replicated. Energy, entropy, volume, and particle number are extensive; temperature, pressure, and chemical potential are intensive.

= Extensive quantity
{parent=Intensive and extensive thermodynamic quantities}

= Extensive variable
{synonym}

An extensive quantity scales in proportion to the size of a thermodynamic system: combining $n$ independent identical copies multiplies it by $n$. <Energy>, <entropy> and <volume> are examples.

= Euler relation in thermodynamics
{c}
{parent=Extensive quantity}
{title2=$E=TS-PV+\mu N$}

= Thermodynamic Euler relation
{synonym}

For a single-component equilibrium system with extensive <internal energy> $E(S,V,N)$, scaling its <entropy>, <volume> and particle number together gives $E(\lambda S,\lambda V,\lambda N)=\lambda E(S,V,N)$. The <Euler theorem for homogeneous functions> therefore gives $E=S\partial_SE+V\partial_VE+N\partial_NE$. The <first law of thermodynamics> identifies these derivatives as $T,-P,\mu$, respectively, proving $E=TS-PV+\mu N$. Dividing by volume yields the <entropy density> formula $s=(\rho+P-\mu n)/T$. This step fixes the entropy normalization rather than merely identifying its differential. Differentiating the Euler relation and subtracting $dE=T\,dS-P\,dV+\mu\,dN$ gives the <Gibbs-Duhem equation> $S\,dT-V\,dP+N\,d\mu=0$. The argument requires extensivity; it is not an assumption about arbitrary nonadditive systems.

= Gibbs-Duhem equation
{c}
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki=Gibbs–Duhem_equation}

The <Euler theorem for homogeneous functions> applied to an <extensive quantity>, together with $dE=T\,dS-p\,dV+\mu\,dN$ give
$$
E=TS-pV+\mu N,
\qquad
S\,dT-V\,dp+N\,d\mu=0.
$$

= Conjugate variables
{disambiguate=thermodynamics}
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}

= Thermodynamic conjugate variable
{synonym}

Two thermodynamic variables are conjugate when their product has units of energy and they occur as a paired term in a thermodynamic differential, such as pressure and volume or tension and length.

= First law of thermodynamics
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{title2=$dU=\delta Q-\delta W$}
{wiki}

The first law applies <conservation of energy> to a thermodynamic system. For a <closed system> with unchanged bulk <kinetic energy> and <potential energy>, write
$$
dU=\delta Q-\delta W,\qquad \Delta U=Q-W.
$$
Here $U$ is <internal energy>, $Q>0$ is <heat> entering the system and $W>0$ is <work> done by the system on its surroundings. Compression therefore gives negative $W$. Some references define <work> positive when done on the system; their plus sign represents the same balance with a different convention. If bulk <kinetic energy> or <potential energy> changes, include it in the total <energy> balance. https://web.mit.edu/16.unified/www/FALL/thermodynamics/notes/node15.html[MIT, First law and work sign convention]{ref}

Supplying $1000\,\mathrm J$ of <heat> while a gas does $300\,\mathrm J$ of <work> increases its <internal energy> by $700\,\mathrm J$. Compressing it with $200\,\mathrm J$ of input <work> in an <adiabatic process> instead gives $Q=0$, $W=-200\,\mathrm J$ and $\Delta U=200\,\mathrm J$.

The distinction between $d$ and $\delta$ records state versus path: <internal energy> is a <state function>, whereas <heat> and <work> depend on the process. For an <ideal gas> expanding reversibly at constant <temperature>, $W=Nk_BT\log(V_2/V_1)$ and $Q=W$, since <internal energy of an ideal gas> depends only on <temperature>. Choose $Nk_BT=1000\,\mathrm J$ and $V_2=2V_1$: then $Q=W=693.15\,\mathrm J$. An insulated <free expansion> into vacuum between the same equilibrium endpoints has $Q=W=0$. Both have $\Delta U=0$, yet their transfers differ. Their common <entropy> increase is $Nk_B\log2$. https://web.mit.edu/16.unified/www/FALL/thermodynamics/notes/node40.html[MIT, Ideal-gas entropy differences]{ref}

For a simple compressible equilibrium system of fixed composition, combining the laws gives
$$
dU=T\,dS-p\,dV.
$$
Here $p$ is <pressure> and $V$ is <volume>. This identity relates neighboring equilibrium states. Identifying $T\,dS$ with actual <heat> and $p\,dV$ with actual <work> requires a suitable <reversible thermodynamic process>; an irreversible piston calculation uses the external resisting <pressure> for boundary <work>. This distinction prevents applying a <reversible ideal-gas adiabat> to an <irreversible adiabatic piston compression>.

= Adiabatic process
{parent=First law of thermodynamics}
{wiki}

An <adiabatic process> has no <heat transfer> across the chosen boundary throughout the process: $\delta Q=0$, rather than merely zero net <heat> after incoming and outgoing transfers cancel. It is a condition on <energy> transfer, whereas an <isothermal process> holds <temperature> constant. A sealed cylinder can approach an <adiabatic process> through insulation or through motion short compared with the time needed for appreciable <heat transfer>. Its <temperature> can nevertheless change because <work> crosses the boundary.<Statistical Physics by David Tong/4 Classical Thermodynamics>[§§4.2–4.3: work, heat, and adiabatic legs of a cycle]{ref}

Take the trapped <gas> as a <closed system>, with fixed <amount of substance> and composition and no net change in bulk <kinetic energy> or <potential energy> between the endpoints. Define $Q_{\rm in}>0$ as <heat> entering and $W_{\rm out}>0$ as <work> delivered to the surroundings. The <first law of thermodynamics> gives
$$
\Delta U=Q_{\rm in}-W_{\rm out},\qquad
Q_{\rm in}=0\ \Longrightarrow\ \Delta U=-W_{\rm out}.
$$
Here $U$ is <internal energy>. An <adiabatic compression> normally supplies <work>, so $W_{\rm out}<0$; an <adiabatic expansion> against a resisting load delivers <work>, so $W_{\rm out}>0$. An <adiabatic process> need not preserve <internal energy> and need not make the <closed system> an <isolated system>. <Thermodynamics and Propulsion/2.5 Control volume form of the conservation laws>[MIT Unified Engineering, §2.5.2: heat inward and work outward in the first-law balance]{ref}

For a <reversible thermodynamic process>, $dS=\delta Q_{\rm rev}/T$ at positive absolute <temperature> $T$. A reversible <adiabatic process> therefore has $dS=0$: it is an <isentropic process>. Irreversible <adiabatic processes> can instead generate <entropy>; <free expansion> is the existing counterexample, with its calculation in <entropy change in free expansion>. Conversely, constant <entropy> alone does not certify an <adiabatic process> or reversibility: outward <heat> can export the <entropy> generated internally. For a <closed system> the macroscopic <entropy> balance is
$$
\Delta S=\int\frac{\delta Q_{\rm in}}{T_b}+S_{\rm gen},\qquad S_{\rm gen}\geq0,
$$
where $T_b$ is the absolute boundary <temperature> where each <heat> transfer occurs and $S_{\rm gen}$ is internal <entropy production>. With no <heat transfer>, this reduces to $\Delta S=S_{\rm gen}$; reversibility makes $S_{\rm gen}=0$. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/4-6-entropy[Ling, Moebs and Sanny, University Physics Volume 2, §4.6: reversible heat and irreversible entropy increase]{ref}

A motivating experiment measures the <gas> <temperature> directly during compression and expansion, instead of inferring it from how warm a cylinder feels. PASCO's authored TD-8565 manual describes a sealed piston cylinder, a fine nickel-wire <thermometer>, an absolute <pressure> sensor and a position readout for <volume (thermodynamics)>. A computer records all three channels together. The wire's <electrical resistance> changes with <temperature>; its small size permits a rapid response. https://cdn.pasco.com/product_document/Adiabatic-Gas-Law-Apparatus-TD-8565-Manual.pdf[PASCO scientific, Adiabatic Gas Law Apparatus, guide 012-05110D, pp. 1–2: apparatus and readouts]{ref}

Use ordinary trapped air and the apparatus's designed travel stops; mount the base securely, close the valve during a stroke, and record the actual <volume (thermodynamics)> limits. John Hart's university demonstration uses a large transparent cylinder with a low-<heat capacity> <thermocouple>, a <pressure> sensor, a travel gauge and a safety stop. It explicitly keeps the release valve closed during the demonstration. The basic observable is a rise in <temperature> on compression and a fall on loaded expansion, alongside the corresponding <pressure> and <volume (thermodynamics)> changes. Hart discusses wall <thermal conduction>, sensor response and leakage as reasons real records differ from the reversible model. https://atoc.colorado.edu/TeachingandLearning/Demonstrations/AdiabaticExpansion/Adiabatic_expansion.htm[John Hart, University of Colorado (2000), Adiabatic Expansion and Gas Law Physics: sensor demonstration and limitations]{ref}

For a proposed rapid-versus-slow comparison, let the air and walls return to the same initial <thermal equilibrium> before each run, then compare the same starting and finishing <volume (thermodynamics)> values with different stroke durations. Read <temperature>, absolute <pressure> and position continuously, including the hold after motion stops. A rapid compression gives a temporary <temperature> rise; holding the smaller <volume (thermodynamics)> lets <heat> leave and the <temperature> approach the surroundings again. Slow compression in good thermal contact allows that <heat transfer> during the stroke and approaches an <isothermal compression>. Loaded expansion reverses the signs: the rapid stroke cools, while <heat> entering during slow motion reduces the cooling. PASCO explicitly supports rapid nearly <adiabatic processes> and slow <isothermal processes>; the matched-run controls here are a proposed extension, not a reported set of measurements. https://www.pasco.com/products/lab-apparatus/thermodynamics/gas-laws/td-8565[PASCO scientific, TD-8565 product documentation: simultaneous readouts and adiabatic/isothermal comparisons]{ref}

Insulation suppresses <thermal conduction>; it does not prove that <heat transfer> vanishes. As proposed controls, repeat the matched strokes with and without a removable insulating jacket, record room and wall <temperatures>, measure fixed-position drift, and check valve closure and seal leakage. Calibrate the <thermometer> and position readout independently, including any connected dead <volume (thermodynamics)>; account for the <heat capacity> of the probe rather than treating it as a passive observer. Hart explicitly identifies the small air sample relative to the probe and walls, sensor inertia and seal leakage as limitations. A fast display cannot eliminate those physical effects. https://atoc.colorado.edu/TeachingandLearning/Demonstrations/AdiabaticExpansion/Adiabatic_expansion.htm[John Hart, Adiabatic Expansion and Gas Law Physics: wall, probe and seal limitations]{ref}

There are two distinct timing requirements. Let $\tau_{\rm eq}$ describe internal equilibration, $\tau_{\rm probe}$ the <thermometer> response, $t_{\rm move}$ the stroke duration and $\tau_{\rm heat}$ the effective <heat transfer> <time constant>. A useful sufficient window for a resolved, nearly reversible <adiabatic process> is
$$
\tau_{\rm eq},\tau_{\rm probe}\ll t_{\rm move}\ll\tau_{\rm heat},
$$
with negligible <friction>. The left inequality permits a <quasistatic process> and a usable readout; the right limits <heat> leakage. A violent stroke may satisfy only the right inequality. PASCO reports a roughly $30$–$50\,\mathrm{ms}$ lag of its <temperature> channel behind the other channels, so apparent simultaneous samples require care. If no such window exists, analyze equilibrium endpoints with the <first law of thermodynamics>, and regard the path as approximate. https://cdn.pasco.com/product_document/Adiabatic-Gas-Law-Apparatus-TD-8565-Manual.pdf[PASCO scientific, guide 012-05110D, p. 4: temperature-sensor lag]{ref}

\Image[adiabatic-processes-sensor-and-timescales.png]
{title=Direct <temperature> readout and a <heat transfer> <time constant> in an <adiabatic process> experiment}
{description=Original apparatus schematic and analytical fixed-<volume (thermodynamics)> relaxation model, not measured records. After a stroke and internal equilibration, the stipulated <Newton's law of cooling> model gives $[T(t)-T_b]/|T(0)-T_b|=\pm e^{-t/\tau_{\rm heat}}$. The positive and negative curves illustrate the holds after compression and loaded expansion. The drawing is a generic sensor arrangement, not a reproduction of a commercial device; it distinguishes the small probe from the walls and their possible <heat transfer>.}

The diagram's model is deliberately a hold after the stroke, when boundary <work> is zero. For constant whole-sample <heat capacity at constant volume> $C_V$ and effective thermal conductance $K>0$, it assumes
$$
C_V\frac{dT}{dt}=-K(T-T_b),\qquad
\tau_{\rm heat}=\frac{C_V}{K},\qquad
T(t)-T_b=[T(0)-T_b]e^{-t/\tau_{\rm heat}}.
$$
This is <Newton's law of cooling> with one uniform <gas> <temperature> and a fixed surrounding <temperature>; actual wall and probe dynamics can require more than one <time constant>. At $t/\tau_{\rm heat}=0.1$, the fraction of the initial <temperature> anomaly erased is $1-e^{-0.1}\simeq0.0952$; at one <time constant> it is about $0.6321$. These are analytical fractions, not experimental tolerances or evidence that the preceding stroke was reversible. Measuring the hold helps estimate <heat> leakage independently of the motion.

The usefulness of an <adiabatic process> is that it separates mechanical <work> from <heat transfer>. Its reversible limit connects <temperature> changes, mechanical stiffness and <entropy> in one model: <Rüchardt experiment> and <Clément-Desormes experiment> already use that connection to measure <heat capacity ratio>. The contrast with <free expansion> explains the deeper point: suppressing <heat> does not determine how much <work> is exchanged or how much <entropy> is produced.

= Adiabatic compression
{parent=Adiabatic process}

<Adiabatic compression> decreases the <volume (thermodynamics)> of a sample without <heat transfer> across its boundary. For the fixed-composition <ideal gas> and endpoint assumptions in <adiabatic process>, compression <work> increases <internal energy>; positive <heat capacity at constant volume> then means a higher final <temperature>. The effect comes from supplied <work>, even though no <heat> enters. The amount of <temperature> increase depends on the <work> actually delivered to the <gas>, not on the <adiabatic process> condition alone.

The <reversible ideal-gas adiabat> supplies a particularly useful quantitative benchmark. Assume a fixed <amount of substance> $n$, a <quasistatic process>, negligible <friction>, only <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> <work>, and constant <heat capacity ratio> $\gamma=C_p/C_V>1$. Here $C_p$ and $C_V$ are the whole sample's <heat capacity at constant pressure> and <heat capacity at constant volume>. With $R$ the <molar gas constant>, <Mayer's relation> gives $C_V=nR/(\gamma-1)$. The proof outline is $C_V\,dT=-p\,dV$ from the <first law of thermodynamics>, followed by $p=nRT/V$ from the <ideal gas law> and integration. The result is
$$
pV^\gamma=\text{constant},\qquad TV^{\gamma-1}=\text{constant}.
$$
These finite power laws belong to this reversible, constant-$\gamma$ <ideal gas> model. If <heat capacity ratio> varies, use the integral relation in <heat capacity ratio>; if the compression is irreversible, use the actual <work> balance, as in <irreversible adiabatic piston compression>. A short stroke by itself justifies neither power law. https://openstax.org/books/university-physics-volume-2/pages/3-6-adiabatic-processes-for-an-ideal-gas[Ling, Moebs and Sanny, University Physics Volume 2, §3.6: quasi-static ideal-gas adiabat]{ref}

Writing $r=V_1/V_2>1$ for the compression ratio, the reversible model predicts
$$
\frac{T_2}{T_1}=r^{\gamma-1},\qquad
\frac{p_2}{p_1}=r^\gamma,\qquad
W_{\rm in}=-W_{\rm out}=C_V(T_2-T_1)
=\frac{p_1V_1}{\gamma-1}\left(r^{\gamma-1}-1\right).
$$
All <temperatures> in these ratios are absolute. The larger <pressure> rise than in an <isothermal compression>, where $p_2/p_1=r$, explains the greater local stiffness described by <isentropic compressibility>.

For an original stipulated example, set $\gamma=7/5$, $p_1V_1=nRT_1=1\,\mathrm{kJ}$ and $r=2$. Then $T_2/T_1=2^{0.4}\simeq1.31951$ and $p_2/p_1=2^{1.4}\simeq2.63902$. The input <work> and <internal energy> increase are both $0.79877\,\mathrm{kJ}$, while $Q_{\rm in}=0$. No initial <temperature> or apparatus performance is implied by the chosen <energy> scale. Independent integration gives the signed outward <work>
$$
W_{\rm out}=\int_{V_1}^{V_2}p\,dV
=\frac{p_2V_2-p_1V_1}{1-\gamma},
$$
which is negative during compression and agrees with $-\Delta U$. This <integral> uses equilibrium gas <pressure> only because the path is reversible.

\Image[adiabatic-processes-work-and-temperature.png]
{title=Reversible <adiabatic compression> and <adiabatic expansion>: <temperature> changes balance signed <work>}
{description=Original analytical <ideal gas> model with constant $\gamma=1.4$ and common initial state $p_1V_1=1\,\mathrm{kJ}$. The two runs go from $V_1$ separately to $V_1/2$ and $2V_1$; they are not a compression followed by its reversal. Shaded <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> areas give <work> magnitudes, with signs supplied by the direction of motion. The dashed <isothermal process> lines are comparisons, not additional <adiabatic processes>. The right panel displays $\Delta U=-W_{\rm out}$ for each run. No curves are measured data.}

In the sensor experiment of <adiabatic process>, compare the <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> <integral> with $C_V\Delta T$ as an <energy> check only when internal <pressure> is sufficiently uniform and piston dynamics permit that boundary-<work> estimate. Account for <friction> separately before comparing it with hand input <work>. PASCO's manual supplies a compression procedure and an illustrative logarithmic-slope result $1.39$, compared with the model value $1.40$ for a diatomic <gas>; that is its sample result, not new measurements here. https://cdn.pasco.com/product_document/Adiabatic-Gas-Law-Apparatus-TD-8565-Manual.pdf[PASCO scientific, guide 012-05110D, pp. 5–6: work comparison and sample fitted exponent]{ref}

= Adiabatic expansion
{parent=Adiabatic process}

<Adiabatic expansion> increases the accessible <volume (thermodynamics)> without <heat transfer>. In a loaded piston expansion of the fixed-composition <ideal gas> considered in <adiabatic process>, positive outward <work> comes from <internal energy>, so the final <temperature> decreases. This provides a cooling mechanism without removing <heat> during the stroke. In the <Carnot cycle>, reversible <adiabatic expansion> connects two different <temperatures> between its <isothermal processes>.<Statistical Physics by David Tong/4 Classical Thermodynamics>[§4.3.1: adiabatic expansion between the isothermal legs]{ref}

For the same constant-$\gamma$ <reversible ideal-gas adiabat>, put $b=V_2/V_1>1$. The scaling and <first law of thermodynamics> give
$$
\frac{T_2}{T_1}=b^{1-\gamma},\qquad
\frac{p_2}{p_1}=b^{-\gamma},\qquad
W_{\rm out}=C_V(T_1-T_2)
=\frac{p_1V_1}{\gamma-1}\left(1-b^{1-\gamma}\right)>0.
$$
With the figure's stipulated $\gamma=1.4$, $p_1V_1=1\,\mathrm{kJ}$ and $b=2$, the final ratios are $T_2/T_1\simeq0.75786$ and $p_2/p_1\simeq0.37893$. Outward <work> is $0.60535\,\mathrm{kJ}$ and $\Delta U=-0.60535\,\mathrm{kJ}$. Reversing this particular path from its cooled endpoint would require the same <work> input; the figure's separate compression starts at the original state, so its input is different. Endpoint <entropy> supplies another independent check:
$$
\Delta S=C_V\ln\frac{T_2}{T_1}+nR\ln\frac{V_2}{V_1}=0.
$$
The two <logarithm> terms cancel using $C_V(\gamma-1)=nR$, confirming the reversible path is an <isentropic process>. https://web.mit.edu/16.unified/www/FALL/thermodynamics/notes/node40.html[MIT Unified Engineering, §5.4: ideal-gas endpoint entropy and constant-entropy scaling]{ref}

PASCO's expansion demonstration first compresses trapped air and waits for <thermal equilibrium> before the rapid expansion. Its manual warns that <friction>-warmed cylinder regions contact the <gas> during expansion, compromising quantitative comparison. https://cdn.pasco.com/product_document/Adiabatic-Gas-Law-Apparatus-TD-8565-Manual.pdf[PASCO scientific, guide 012-05110D, p. 3: expansion preparation and frictional wall-heating limitation]{ref} The hold separates cooling caused by expansion from simply undoing the earlier hot compression. As proposed controls, observe the <temperature> dip and subsequent warming, compare rapid and slow strokes from matched initial states, and assess wall <heat transfer> and probe lag before fitting a reversible model.

Cooling is not a universal consequence of <adiabatic expansion>: <free expansion> of an <ideal gas> has no outward <work> and returns to the same equilibrium <temperature>. Its <entropy> increases, so the reversible power laws do not describe it. Reuse <free expansion>, <Joule's free-expansion experiment> and <temperature sensitivity of a free-expansion experiment> for that apparatus, its <energy> budget and the real-gas qualifications. The useful distinction is the load: expansion cools this <ideal gas> when it must spend <internal energy> doing <work>.

= Isentropic process
{parent=Adiabatic process}
{wiki}

An isentropic process keeps the <entropy> constant. Every reversible <adiabatic process> is isentropic, while an irreversible adiabatic process can increase entropy.

= Reversible ideal-gas adiabat
{parent=Adiabatic process}

For a fixed amount of ideal gas with constant heat capacities, the first law and $\delta Q=0$ give
$$
TV^{\gamma-1}=\text{constant},
\qquad
pV^\gamma=\text{constant},
$$
where $\gamma=C_p/C_V$.

= Enthalpy
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{title2=$H=U+pV$}
{wiki}

Enthalpy is the <state function> obtained by adding <pressure> times <volume (thermodynamics)> to <internal energy>:
$$
H=U+pV.
$$
Here $U$ denotes <internal energy>, also written $E$ in some references. Like <internal energy>, <enthalpy> is measured in <joules> and is an <extensive variable>. Its definition packages the <internal energy> change and expansion <work> into one useful quantity for fixed-<pressure> calculations. It does not define a body's stored <heat>. https://goldbook.iupac.org/terms/view/E02141/plain[IUPAC Gold Book, enthalpy]{ref}

For a fixed amount and composition of a <simple compressible system>, the relation between neighboring equilibrium states is
$$
dH=T\,dS+V\,dp.
$$
Here $T$ is absolute <temperature> and $S$ is <entropy>. Thus <entropy> and <pressure> are the natural variables of this <thermodynamic potential>, with <partial derivatives> $T=(\partial H/\partial S)_p$ and $V=(\partial H/\partial p)_S$. Fixed composition matters: changing composition adds further terms. The formula follows in one line from the equilibrium <first law of thermodynamics> identity:
$$
dH=dU+p\,dV+V\,dp=(T\,dS-p\,dV)+p\,dV+V\,dp=T\,dS+V\,dp.
$$
The cancellation of $p\,dV$ is the reason <enthalpy> replaces <volume (thermodynamics)> with <pressure> as a natural variable. This identity concerns equilibrium states; it does not identify $T\,dS$ with actual irreversible <heat> transfer. https://davidtong.org/teaching/statistical-physics/statmechhtml/S4#S4.SS4.SSS1[David Tong, Statistical Physics, section 4.4.1: enthalpy]{ref}

For a <closed system> undergoing a <quasistatic process> at constant <pressure>, require fixed amount and composition, only <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> <work>, no <friction>, and unchanged bulk <kinetic energy> and <potential energy>. Then the actual <first law of thermodynamics> balance gives
$$
\delta Q=dU+p\,dV=dH-V\,dp,\qquad
dp=0\ \Longrightarrow\ Q_p=\Delta H.
$$
The symbol $Q_p$ means transferred <heat> for this fixed-<pressure> process. If the piston example in <heat> additionally meets these conditions, its $20\,\mathrm{kJ}$ of incoming <heat> equals $\Delta H$, while $\Delta U=15\,\mathrm{kJ}$ and $p\Delta V=W=5\,\mathrm{kJ}$. The example shows exactly what <enthalpy> adds to <internal energy>: the expansion <work> required at the imposed <pressure>.

Within a single <thermodynamic phase>, <heat capacity at constant pressure> gives
$$
C_p=\left(\frac{\partial H}{\partial T}\right)_p,\qquad
\Delta H=\int_{T_1}^{T_2}C_p(T)\,dT
$$
with the amount and composition fixed. Constant $C_p$ gives $\Delta H=C_p\Delta T$; dividing by <mass> gives <specific heat capacity at constant pressure>. At equilibrium <melting>, <enthalpy> can instead change through <latent heat> while <temperature> stays fixed. The <sensible heat> example therefore adds two single-<thermodynamic phase> <enthalpy> changes and one <latent heat> contribution.

The usefulness of <enthalpy> lies in matching the imposed constraint: a fixed-<pressure> experiment naturally reports $\Delta H$, while fixed-<volume (thermodynamics)> heating with no other <work> reports $\Delta U$. Neither equality is universal. Additional <work> or changing <pressure> alters the relation between supplied <heat> and $\Delta H$; irreversible <entropy> production also prevents identifying actual $\delta Q$ with $T\,dS$. The separate <Joule-Thomson coefficient> branch uses <enthalpy> to describe throttling under its own assumptions.

= Molar enthalpy
{parent=Enthalpy}
{title2=$h_{\rm m}=H/n$}

<Molar enthalpy> is <enthalpy> per <amount of substance> $n$, measured in $\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}$. With $u_{\rm m}=U/n$ and <molar volume> $v_{\rm m}=V/n$, $h_{\rm m}=u_{\rm m}+pv_{\rm m}$. Its fixed-<pressure> <temperature> derivative is the corresponding <molar heat capacity> $C_{p,\rm m}$, at fixed composition.

= Isenthalpic process
{parent=Enthalpy}
{wiki}

An <isenthalpic process> preserves <enthalpy> for a fixed amount of material, or preserves <specific enthalpy> between inlet and outlet when comparing a flowing <fluid> of fixed composition. It need not preserve <temperature> or <entropy>. An insulated throttle satisfying the <steady-flow energy balance> assumptions is an important example.

= Steady-flow energy balance
{parent=Enthalpy}
{title2=$\dot Q-\dot W_s=\dot m\,\Delta(h+w^2/2+gz)$}

For <steady flow> through a fixed <control volume> with one inlet and one outlet, the <first law of thermodynamics> and conservation of <mass> give
$$
\dot Q-\dot W_s=\dot m\left[h_2-h_1+\frac{w_2^2-w_1^2}{2}+g(z_2-z_1)\right].
$$
Here $\dot m$ is the <mass flow rate>, $\dot Q$ incoming <heat> per unit time, $\dot W_s$ outgoing non-flow <work> per unit time, $w$ the flow speed, $z$ elevation and $g$ <gravitational acceleration>. The <specific enthalpy> $h=u+p/\rho$ includes both the transported <internal energy> per unit <mass> $u$ and the <pressure> <work> needed to push <volume (thermodynamics)> through the boundary; $\rho$ is <mass density>. The inlet supplies that <pressure> <work>, while the outlet consumes it to displace the downstream <fluid>.<Thermodynamics and Propulsion>[§2.5.1–2.5.2, equation 2.11: mass conservation, transported enthalpy and the steady-flow energy equation]{ref}

For an insulated stationary throttle, require negligible <heat> transfer, no shaft or other non-flow <work>, and negligible changes in bulk <kinetic energy> and gravitational <potential energy>. Then $h_2=h_1$. With unchanged composition this is also equality of <molar enthalpy>. The upstream and downstream states must admit equilibrium <pressure>, <temperature> and property values, although the constriction itself is irreversible and can be far from <thermodynamic equilibrium>. Start-up storage of <energy> in the walls and <fluid>, heat leakage, or a large outlet speed invalidates the reduced balance. A nozzle can convert <specific enthalpy> into bulk <kinetic energy> even when it is insulated.

The same accounting for a fixed transported amount is $U_2-U_1=p_1V_1-p_2V_2$, hence $U_1+p_1V_1=U_2+p_2V_2$. This explains why dropping the two <pressure> <work> terms would give the wrong conserved quantity.<Thermodynamics and Statistical Mechanics by Richard Fitzpatrick/Joule-Thompson Throttling>[Fitzpatrick, Joule-Thompson Throttling, equations 6.167–6.174: inlet/outlet pressure work and enthalpy conservation]{ref}

= Joule-Thomson effect
{c}
{parent=Enthalpy}
{wiki=Joule–Thomson_effect}

The <Joule-Thomson effect> is the <temperature> response of a <fluid> flowing from higher to lower <pressure> through a valve or a plug of <porous medium>, when the throttle is insulated and the other <steady-flow energy balance> assumptions hold. Its equilibrium endpoints have equal <specific enthalpy>. The <Joule-Thomson coefficient> determines whether a small drop cools or warms; cooling is not universal.

\Image[joule-thomson-throttle-enthalpy-balance.png]
{id=joule-thomson-throttle-enthalpy-balance}
{title=<Enthalpy> accounting for an insulated <Joule-Thomson effect> throttle}
{description=Original schematic of the <steady-flow energy balance>, with $u$ denoting <internal energy> per unit <mass>. The arrows include inlet and outlet <pressure> <work>. The reduced balance requires negligible bulk <kinetic energy> and <potential energy> changes. No particular apparatus or measured <temperature> drop is depicted.}

A useful contrast is between three insulated expansions. In <free expansion> of a gas-only <closed system> into a <vacuum>, no external <work> occurs and $U_2=U_1$; an <ideal gas> returns to its initial equilibrium <temperature>. In a throttle, continuous flow carries <pressure> <work> across the boundary and preserves <specific enthalpy> under the stated assumptions; an <ideal gas> again has no endpoint <temperature> change. In a reversible <adiabatic expansion> of a loaded <closed system>, the <gas> supplies <work>, loses <internal energy> and cools, even if it is an <ideal gas>. That process preserves <entropy>; throttling generally produces <entropy>. For a small constant-<enthalpy> step, $dH=T\,dS+V\,dp=0$ gives $dS=-V\,dp/T>0$ when $dp<0$ and $T,V>0$. This equilibrium comparison computes endpoint <entropy> differences without supplying a reversible path through the plug.

The effect is useful in <refrigerators> because an appropriately prepared <real gas> can cool while passing through a simple valve. The inlet must lie in a suitable positive-<Joule-Thomson coefficient> region. The <Joule-Thomson inversion curve> makes that requirement precise; a pressure drop by itself is not a guarantee of refrigeration.

= Measuring the Joule-Thomson effect with a porous plug
{parent=Joule-Thomson effect}

The 2024 operating instructions for PHYWE's 04361-00 apparatus describe a frit between two gas chambers. A copper-coil <heat exchanger> first restores inlet <temperature> after cylinder-regulator cooling. An upstream manometer reads <gauge pressure>; the outlet stays at <atmospheric pressure>. Two NiCr-Ni <thermocouples> sit 2–5 mm from the frit. The digital meter's difference mode reports $0.01\,\mathrm K$ precision.<Joule-Thomson apparatus 04361-00, Operating instructions by PHYWE>[PHYWE, Joule-Thomson apparatus 04361-00, 2024 instructions, pp. 1–2, sections 2–4: heat exchanger, frit, manometer, probes and difference readout]{ref}

The manual specifies at most $1\,\mathrm{bar}$ <gauge pressure>, one hour of apparatus acclimatization, half an hour of meter warm-up, about 1.5 minutes of flow settling, then 10–15 seconds of averaging. It identifies a stagnant-gas zeroing error that shifts the fitted intercept and notes a maximum <nitrogen> signal of about $0.3\,\mathrm K$ in this setup.<Joule-Thomson apparatus 04361-00, Operating instructions by PHYWE>[PHYWE, 2024 instructions, p. 2, section 4, Hints concerning the experiment: pressure limit, equilibration, drift, averaging and zero compensation]{ref}

Define $x=p_1-p_2>0$ and $y=T_1-T_2$, so a small-drop fit is $y\simeq\mu_{\rm JT}x+c$. A constant sensor offset $c$ leaves the slope unchanged; pressure-dependent drift or <heat> leakage can alter it. The observable is the differential <temperature> slope across the plug, not cylinder-outlet cooling. Display precision alone does not establish accuracy or prove an <isenthalpic process>. This low-pressure, room-temperature apparatus does not map a complete <Joule-Thomson inversion curve>.

= Joule-Thomson coefficient
{c}
{parent=Enthalpy}
{title2=$\mu=(\partial T/\partial p)_H$}

The <Joule-Thomson coefficient> is the local <temperature> response to changing <pressure> at fixed <enthalpy> and fixed amount and composition:
$$
\mu_{\rm JT}=\left(\frac{\partial T}{\partial p}\right)_H,
\qquad [\mu_{\rm JT}]=\mathrm{K\,Pa^{-1}}.
$$
It describes the equilibrium endpoints of a <Joule-Thomson effect> experiment meeting the <steady-flow energy balance> assumptions. With $\Delta p=p_2-p_1<0$, a sufficiently small drop gives $\Delta T\simeq\mu_{\rm JT}\Delta p$. Thus $\mu_{\rm JT}>0$ means cooling, $\mu_{\rm JT}<0$ warming, and $\mu_{\rm JT}=0$ no first-order change. At zero coefficient, a finite drop can still change <temperature>. For a chosen illustrative local value $\mu_{\rm JT}=0.20\,\mathrm{K\,bar^{-1}}$ and $\Delta p=-0.50\,\mathrm{bar}$, the linear prediction is $\Delta T=-0.10\,\mathrm K$; these are stipulated numbers, not measured gas properties.

For a fixed-composition <simple compressible system>, the measurable form is
$$
\mu_{\rm JT}
=\frac{T(\partial V/\partial T)_p-V}{C_p}
=\frac{V}{C_p}(T\alpha-1).
$$
Here $C_p$ is the whole sample's <heat capacity at constant pressure> and $\alpha$ its volumetric <coefficient of thermal expansion>; alternatively use <molar volume> and <molar heat capacity> consistently. An <ideal gas> has $\alpha=1/T$, so $\mu_{\rm JT}=0$. Its <enthalpy> depends only on <temperature>; positive <heat capacity at constant pressure> then makes any such finite throttle preserve <temperature>. A <real gas> can have either sign. For $C_p>0$, compare $T\alpha$ with one: the <thermal expansion> response decides the sign, not insulation alone.<Thermodynamics and Statistical Mechanics by Richard Fitzpatrick/Joule-Thompson Throttling>[Fitzpatrick, Joule-Thompson Throttling, equations 6.175–6.182: ideal-gas limit and coefficient]{ref}

A short derivation uses $dH=T\,dS+V\,dp$ and the <Maxwell relation> $(\partial S/\partial p)_T=-(\partial V/\partial T)_p$:
$$
dH=C_p\,dT+\left[V-T\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_p\right]dp.
$$
Setting $dH=0$ gives the coefficient. These are equilibrium <state function> identities, not a claim of a <reversible thermodynamic process> inside the throttle. For a finite drop, solve the endpoint <enthalpy> equality or integrate
$$
T_2-T_1=\int_{p_1}^{p_2}\mu_{\rm JT}(T(p),p)\,dp
$$
along the constant-<enthalpy> equilibrium curve. The coefficient can change sign during that integration, as the <Van der Waals throttling model> illustrates.

= Joule-Thomson inversion curve
{c}
{parent=Joule-Thomson coefficient}
{title2=$\mu_{\rm JT}(T,p)=0$}

The <Joule-Thomson inversion curve> is the set of equilibrium <temperature>–<pressure> states where the <Joule-Thomson coefficient> vanishes. For positive <heat capacity at constant pressure>, its condition is $T\alpha=1$, with $\alpha$ the <coefficient of thermal expansion>. The curve separates locally cooling and warming throttle regions. A finite <isenthalpic process> can cross it, so the inlet sign alone does not fix the total <temperature> change. An <ideal gas> has zero coefficient everywhere, rather than an isolated dividing curve.

= Joule-Thomson inversion temperature
{c}
{parent=Joule-Thomson inversion curve}
{title2=$T_{\rm inv}(p)$}

A <Joule-Thomson inversion temperature> is a <temperature> at which the <Joule-Thomson coefficient> is zero at a specified <pressure>. There can be more than one such <temperature> at that <pressure>; distinguish the upper and lower branches from a maximum or a dilute-gas limiting value.

= Van der Waals throttling model
{c}
{parent=Joule-Thomson coefficient}

A hypothetical <Van der Waals equation> model illustrates positive and negative <Joule-Thomson coefficient> regions and a finite throttle that first warms, then cools. Use the molar convention already distinguished in <Van der Waals equation>:
$$
p=\frac{RT}{v_{\rm m}-b_{\rm m}}-\frac{a_{\rm m}}{v_{\rm m}^2},
\qquad
u_{\rm m}=C_{V,\rm m}T-\frac{a_{\rm m}}{v_{\rm m}},
\qquad C_{V,\rm m}=\frac32R.
$$
Here $v_{\rm m}$ is <molar volume>, $R$ the <molar gas constant>, $u_{\rm m}$ <internal energy> per <mole>, and $C_{V,\rm m}$ the stipulated constant <molar heat capacity> at fixed <volume (thermodynamics)>. The second equation is an additional caloric assumption: an <equation of state> alone does not specify the <temperature> dependence of <internal energy>. It satisfies the equilibrium identity $(\partial u_{\rm m}/\partial v_{\rm m})_T=T(\partial p/\partial T)_{v_{\rm m}}-p=a_{\rm m}/v_{\rm m}^2$. The arbitrary <energy> reference is set to zero.

\Image[van-der-waals-throttling-inversion-model.png]
{id=van-der-waals-throttling-inversion-model}
{title=Hypothetical <Van der Waals throttling model>: <Joule-Thomson inversion curve> and finite <temperature> responses}
{description=All curves are original model calculations, not <nitrogen>, <carbon dioxide> or other gas measurements. Colors label the response along constant <molar enthalpy>; arrows point toward lower <pressure>. The displayed states are above the model <Van der Waals critical point> <temperature> and have positive <isothermal compressibility>. The purple path crosses the zero-coefficient curve and has a <temperature> maximum.}

Define dimensionless variables $r=v_{\rm m}/b_{\rm m}$, $\theta=Rb_{\rm m}T/a_{\rm m}$, $\pi=b_{\rm m}^2p/a_{\rm m}$ and $\eta=b_{\rm m}h_{\rm m}/a_{\rm m}$, where $h_{\rm m}$ is <molar enthalpy>. These differ from the critical-value scaling in <Van der Waals law of corresponding states>. The throttle calculation reduces to
$$
\pi=\frac{\theta}{r-1}-\frac1{r^2},\qquad
\eta=\frac32\theta+\pi r-\frac1r,
\qquad
\theta(r;\eta)=\frac{\eta+2/r}{3/2+r/(r-1)}.
$$
Thus a prescribed inlet fixes $\eta$, and solving the first and last equations at the outlet <pressure> gives the final <temperature>. For an inlet $(\theta_1,\pi_1)=(0.8,0.12)$ and outlet $\pi_2=0.025$, the model gives $\theta_2\simeq0.752287$, a decrease of $0.047713$. The inlet $(2.2,0.12)$ instead gives $\theta_2\simeq2.205786$, an increase of $0.005786$. The purple inlet $(1.45,0.30)$ initially warms but ends at $\theta_2\simeq1.437585$ for the same outlet <pressure>. These are reduced toy values, not kelvin readings or experimental evidence. No physical gas parameters were fitted.

For a short inversion derivation, implicit <differentiation> of the <Van der Waals equation> at fixed <pressure> gives
$$
\left(\frac{\partial v_{\rm m}}{\partial T}\right)_p
=\frac{R/(v_{\rm m}-b_{\rm m})}{RT/(v_{\rm m}-b_{\rm m})^2-2a_{\rm m}/v_{\rm m}^3}.
$$
Insert this into $T(\partial v_{\rm m}/\partial T)_p=v_{\rm m}$ and substitute back into the <equation of state>. The <Joule-Thomson inversion curve> is
$$
T_{\rm inv}=\frac{2a_{\rm m}}{Rb_{\rm m}}\left(1-\frac{b_{\rm m}}{v_{\rm m}}\right)^2,
\qquad
\theta_{\rm inv}=2(1-1/r)^2,
\qquad
\pi_{\rm inv}=\frac{2r-3}{r^2}.
$$
Positive inversion <pressure> requires $r>3/2$. Its maximum <pressure> is $\pi=1/3$ at $r=3$, while the upper dilute limit is $\theta_{\rm inv}\to2$. Using <Van der Waals critical point> values, the latter is $T_{\rm inv,max}/T_c=27/4$. The inversion locus depends on the <equation of state>; the chosen <molar heat capacity> additionally determines how much <temperature> changes along each finite throttle.<Thermodynamics and Statistical Mechanics by Richard Fitzpatrick/Joule-Thompson Throttling>[Fitzpatrick, Joule-Thompson Throttling, equations 6.188–6.190 and following paragraph: inversion locus, maximum temperature and limits of the van der Waals prediction]{ref}

The displayed region has $\theta>8/27$, above the model's critical <temperature>, and uses only $r>1$, $p>0$, $(\partial p/\partial v_{\rm m})_T<0$ and positive $C_{p,\rm m}$. Subcritical calculations require attention to <phase coexistence>, <metastability> and the <Maxwell construction>; an unstable homogeneous continuation is not an equilibrium throttle endpoint. Even on a stable branch, this <mean-field theory> with constant $C_{V,\rm m}$ is a qualitative model, not an accurate inversion map for a particular <real gas>. <Phase transitions>, changing composition, appreciable <heat> leakage or bulk <kinetic energy> changes require a different property calculation or <steady-flow energy balance>. At <phase coexistence>, the smooth single-<thermodynamic phase> coefficient formula cannot replace a balance involving the <phase fraction>.

= Virial criterion for Joule-Thomson cooling
{parent=Joule-Thomson coefficient}

For $p=k_BT[n+B_2(T)n^2]$, the coefficient is $\mu=N[TB_2'-B_2]/[C_p(1+2B_2n)]$. On the stable dilute branch it is positive precisely when $d(B_2/T)/dT>0$.

= Specific enthalpy
{title2=$h$}
{parent=Enthalpy}

Specific enthalpy is <enthalpy> per unit mass. For a simple compressible fluid of fixed composition, the <first law of thermodynamics> gives
$$
dh=T\,ds+\frac{dp}{\rho},
$$
where $T$ is <temperature>, $s$ is <specific entropy>, $p$ is <pressure>, and $\rho$ is <mass density>. Along an <isentropic flow>, $dh=dp/\rho$. For a <perfect gas> with constant <specific-heat ratio> $\gamma$, $h=\gamma p/[(\gamma-1)\rho]$.

= Polytropic enthalpy
{parent=Specific enthalpy}
{title2=$Q=(n+1)p/\rho$}

For $p=K\rho^{1+1/n}$ with $K,n>0$, the specific <enthalpy> relative to vacuum is $Q=(n+1)K\rho^{1/n}=(n+1)p/\rho$. It obeys $\nabla Q=\nabla p/\rho$ and $nD_tQ=-Q\nabla\cdot\mathbf u$. The vacuum reference is fixed when these equations are combined with the <polytropic equation of state>.

= Enthalpy Maxwell relation
{parent=Enthalpy}

Since $T=(\partial H/\partial S)_p$ and $V=(\partial H/\partial p)_S$, equality of mixed derivatives gives $(\partial T/\partial p)_S=(\partial V/\partial S)_p$.

= Heat capacity at constant pressure
{parent=Enthalpy}
{title2=$C_p=(\partial H/\partial T)_p$}

= Constant-pressure heat capacity
{synonym}

For a fixed-composition <closed system>, the <heat capacity> at fixed <pressure> is $C_p=(\partial H/\partial T)_p$, with <enthalpy> $H$. Under the <quasistatic process> assumptions in <heat capacity>, only <pressure>-<volume (thermodynamics)> <work> is permitted, so $dp=0$ gives $\delta Q=dH=C_p\,dT$. Thus supplied <heat> is $Q=\Delta H$, or $Q=C_p\Delta T$ within a single <thermodynamic phase> when $C_p$ is constant. For an <ideal gas>, the expansion <work> explains why this exceeds the required <heat> at fixed <volume (thermodynamics)> for the same <temperature> increase. Its per-unit-<mass> counterpart is <specific heat capacity at constant pressure>.

= Difference between constant-pressure and constant-volume heat capacities
{title2=$C_P-C_V$}
{parent=Heat capacity at constant pressure}

For a simple compressible system,
$$
C_P-C_V
=T\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial T}\right)_P
\left(\frac{\partial P}{\partial T}\right)_V.
$$
This follows by expressing the entropy as a function of temperature and volume and using a Maxwell relation.

= Diatomic ideal-gas enthalpy
{parent=Thermodynamics}

With translational and rotational modes active but vibration frozen, a diatomic ideal gas has $E=5Nk_BT/2$ and $H=7Nk_BT/2$.

= Irreversible adiabatic piston compression
{parent=Thermodynamics}

After a sudden increase to constant external pressure $p_1$, an insulated gas obeys $\Delta E=p_1(V_0-V_1)$ rather than a reversible adiabatic power law.

= Constant-pressure heating of an ideal gas
{parent=Thermodynamics}

At fixed pressure, $Q=\Delta H$ and the expansion work is $p\Delta V=Nk_B\Delta T$.

= Thermodynamic cycle
{parent=Thermodynamics}
{wiki}

A thermodynamic cycle returns a working substance to its initial state. Its net internal-energy change is zero, so the net work output equals net heat input.

= Thermal efficiency
{title2=$\eta=W/Q_{\rm in}$}
{parent=Thermodynamic cycle}
{wiki}

The thermal efficiency of a heat engine is the net work output divided by heat input. For a cycle absorbing $Q_{\rm in}$ and rejecting the positive amount $Q_{\rm out}$,
$$
\eta=1-\frac{Q_{\rm out}}{Q_{\rm in}}.
$$

= Otto cycle
{c}
{parent=Thermodynamic cycle}
{wiki}

The ideal Otto cycle has adiabatic compression, constant-volume heat addition, adiabatic expansion, and constant-volume heat rejection. For an ideal gas with compression ratio $r=V_1/V_2$ and specific-heat ratio $\gamma$,
$$
\eta=1-\frac1{r^{\gamma-1}}.
$$

= Diesel cycle
{c}
{parent=Thermodynamic cycle}
{wiki}

The ideal Diesel cycle consists of reversible adiabatic compression, constant-pressure heat addition, reversible adiabatic expansion, and constant-volume heat rejection.
